Spaces of distributions on product metric spaces associated with operators Thanks: The third author has been supported by Grant KP-06-N62/4 of the Fund for Scientific Research of the Bulgarian Ministry of Education and Science.
A. G. Georgiadis, G. Kyriazis, P. Petrushev

Abstract
. We lay down the foundation of the theory of spaces of distributions on the product X 1 × X 2 X_{1}\times X_{2} of doubling metric measure spaces X 1 X_{1} , X 2 X_{2} in the presence of non-negative self-adjoint operators L 1 L_{1} , L 2 L_{2} , whose heat kernels have Gaussian localization and the Markov property. This theory includes the development of two-parameter functional calculus induced by L 1 , L 2 L_{1},L_{2} , integral operators with highly localized kernels, test functions and distributions associated to L 1 , L 2 L_{1},L_{2} , spectral spaces accompanied by maximal Peetre and Nikolski type inequalities. Hardy spaces are developed in this two-parameter product setup. Two types of Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces are introduced and studied: ordinary spaces and spaces with dominating mixed smoothness, with emphasis on the latter. Embedding results are obtained and spectral multiplies are developed.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 58J35, 46E35, 43A85, 42B30 ; Secondary 42B25, 42B15, 42C15, 42C40

1.
Introduction

The purpose of this article is to is to develop the theory of spaces of distributions, in particular, Hardy, Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces,
on the product
X_{1}\times X_{2}
of doubling metric measure spaces
X_{1}
,
X_{2}

in the presence of non-negative self-adjoint operators
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
,
whose heat kernels have Gaussian localization and the Markov property.

Our first step in this undertaking is to develop the two-parameter functional calculus induced by
L_{1},L_{2}
,
which includes the development of the associated two-parameter spectral measure and spectral resolution.
The latter is in principle well-known but we will add some details to the picture.
An important part of the functional calculus is the development of integral operators with highly localized kernels.
The development of distributions associated to
L_{1},L_{2}
is the next step,
followed by the development of spectral spaces accompanied by maximal Peetre and Nikolski type inequalities.

The Hardy spaces
H^{p}
,
0<p<\infty
, in the two-parameter setting of this article are the first type of spaces that we consider.
We show that the two-parameter Hardy spaces
H^{p}
,
0<p<\infty
, can be equivalently defined by means of several maximal operators
and can be identified with the Lebesgue spaces
L^{p}
for
1<p<\infty
.

Another major focus of our study is on the Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces in the two-parameter setting.
The classical Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces have been mainly developed for
the purposes of approximation theory and partial differential equations.
We refer the reader to the monographs
[
52
,
53
]
of H. Triebel for the history
and a complete account of this theory.
The Littlewood-Paley approach to Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces in the classical case on
\mathbb{R}^{n}

has been initiated by J. Peetre
[
44
]

and further implemented mainly by H. Triebel
[
52
]
, M. Frazier and B. Javerth
[
25
,
26
,
27
]
.
In this theory the Laplace operator
\Delta
plays a prominent role.

It is natural to develop abstract Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces,
where the role of
\Delta
is played by a self-adjoint operator
L
and
the spectral decomposition of the Laplacian (via the Fourier transform)
is replaced by the spectral decomposition associated to
L
.
This idea has been promoted by J. Peetre
[
44
, Chapter 10]
and H. Triebel
[
51
, Chapter 3]
.
The problem, however, is that very little can be achieved if one operates in the general setting of
an abstract Hilbert space with the presence of a self-adjoint operator
L
.
Additional conditions need to be imposed in order to have a substantial theory.

It turns out that Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces with full set of parameters can be developed
in the setting of a doubling metric measure space accompanied by a non-negative self-adjoint operator
L

whose heat kernel has Gaussian localization, Hölder continuity and the Markov property.
The basics of this theory have been developed in
[
12
,
39
]
,
where distributions were introduced and Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces of distributions
with full set of parameters were defined and studied in complete analogy with the classical case on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Various aspects of the theory have been further developed in
[
2
,
3
,
14
,
29
,
30
,
32
,
33
,
36
,
42
,
56
]
.
The point is that many particular cases are covered by this setting.
Examples include the classical setting on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the sphere, ball, and simplex in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with weights
as well as Lie groups or homogeneous spaces with polynomial volume growth
and Riemannian manifolds with Ricci curvature bounded from below and satisfying the
volume doubling condition
.
It turns out that in the general setting of strictly local regular Dirichlet spaces
with a complete intrinsic metric it suffices to only verify
the local Poincaré inequality and the global doubling condition on the measure
and then the above general setting applies in full.
This theory allows to verify the conditions of our setting in a number of specific cases.
See
[
12
,
39
]
for details.

One of the main goals of this article is to develop the basics of the theory
of Hardy, Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces on product domains
X_{1}\times X_{2}

associated with non-negative self-adjoint operators
L_{1},L_{2}
.
The roots of this theory can be traced back to the seventies in the work of
S.-Y. A. Chang and R. Fefferman
[
6
,
7
,
8
,
20
,
21
,
22
]
on the Multiparameter Harmonic Analysis
which basically extended the Calderón-Zygmund Theory to product domains on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
We refer the reader also to
[
23
,
28
,
38
]
for these early developments.

We assume that
X_{1},X_{2}
are metric measure spaces with the doubling property
and the heat kernels associated to
L_{1},L_{2}
have Gaussian localization, Hölder continuity and the Markov property.
In developing the Hardy spaces
H^{p}
in this two-parameter setting we show that they can be equivalently defined via several maximal operators
in the spirit of the classical case on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

In the same setting we consider two kinds of Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces:
ordinary spaces and spaces with dominating mixed smoothness.
The ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

are simply the respective spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
and depend on a single smoothness parameter.
The Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces with dominating mixed smoothness on
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

depend on two-parameter
(s_{1},s_{2})
smoothness.
For short we will call these spaces
mixed-smoothness B and F-spaces
.
The theory of the mixed smoothness B and F-spaces in the classical setting
on
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
has been mainly developed by H.-J. Schmeisser and H. Triebel
[
49
]
,
see also
[
48
,
54
]
and the references therein.
These spaces are closely related to approximation from hyperbolic cross trigonometric polynomials, splines, and wavelets,
see
[
16
,
48
]
and the references therein.
Homogeneous mixed smoothness B and F-spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
are introduced in
[
31
]
and used
in nonlinear
m
-term approximation from hyperbolic cross wavelets.

It should be pointed out that the theory of Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces with dominating mixed smoothness
on product domains other than
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
[0,2\pi)^{m}\times[0,2\pi)^{n}
(the torus) is virtually not existent.
Ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces on product domains are introduced and studied in
[
37
]
.
More specifically, in
[
37
]
ordinary B and F-spaces are considered on
[-1,1]^{d}
associated with the Jacobi operator
and on
{\mathbb{B}}^{d_{1}}\times[-1,1]^{d_{2}}
associated with operators with polynomial eigenfunctions
on the unit ball
{\mathbb{B}}^{d_{1}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
and
[-1,1]^{d_{2}}
.
Ordinary B and F-spaces on products of other domains such as the sphere, simplex, torus, and
\mathbb{R}^{d}

associated with operators are also discussed in
[
37
]
.
All these cases are naturally covered by our theory.
Furthermore, our theory covers mixed-smoothness B and F-spaces on products of domains such as
the sphere, ball, simplex as well as Riemannian manifolds, Lee groups and more general Dirichlet spaces.

For notational convenience
we consider Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces on the product
X_{1}\times X_{2}
of only two domains
X_{1},X_{2}

associated with two operators
L_{1},L_{2}
.
The extension of this theory to Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces
on product domains
X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}

associated with operators
L_{1},\dots,L_{n}

is almost automatic by changing the notation.
The main focus of this article is on the development of the mixed-smoothness Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces,
while the ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces will be sparingly developed for comparison without many proofs.

The main contributions of this article can be summarised as follows:

•

The underlying two-parameter smooth functional calculus on
the product domain
X_{1}\times X_{2}
associated with the operators
L_{1},L_{2}
is developed,
which includes the development of the two-parameter spectral measure induced by
L_{1},L_{2}
,
and the development of integral operators of the form
F(\sqrt{L_{1}},\sqrt{L_{2}})

with highly localized kernels.

•

Distributions associated to the operators
L_{1},L_{2}
are developed,
some of their basic properties are presented,
and a fundamental Carderón reproducing formula is established.

•

Spectral spaces, a substitute for band limited functions, associated to
L_{1},L_{2}
are introduced
and Peetre and Nikolksi type estimates are established.

•

We utilize our development of integral operators and distributions
to develop Hardy spaces in the two-parameter setting of this article;
we show that the two-parameter Hardy spaces
H^{p}
,
p>0
, can be equivalently defined by means of several different maximal operators.

•

Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces with dominating mixed smoothness
on
X_{1}\times X_{2}
associated with
L_{1},L_{2}
are introduced and explored.
In particular, embeddings between test functions, mixed-smoothness B and F-spaces, and distributions
associated with operators are obtained.

•

Spectral multipliers are introduced and their boundedness on test functions and distributions
as well as on mixed-smoothness B and F-spaces are obtained.

•

The basics of ordinary (one parameter) Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces
on
X_{1}\times X_{2}
associated with
L_{1},L_{2}
are also developed.
The main purpose of these results is to put in perspective our development of
the mixed-smoothness B and F-spaces.

Basic components of the theory of Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces on product domains associated with operators such as
frame decomposition,
interpolation, and
atomic and molecular decompositions of B-F-spaces
are left for future development.

An outline of this article is as follows:
In Section
2
, we introduce our setting and present some basic facts about the coordinate spaces
X_{1},X_{2}
.
In Section
3
, we introduce the product spaces and present some of their properties.
In Section
4
, we develop the underlying two-parameter smooth functional calculus on
the product domain
X_{1}\times X_{2}
associated with the operators
L_{1},L_{2}
.
Distributions associated to the operators
L_{1},L_{2}
are developed in Section
5
.
Spectral spaces are introduced in Section
6

and Peetre and Nikolksi type estimates are established.
Section
7
is devoted to the development of Hardy spaces in the two-parameter setting of this article.
Mixed-smoothness Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces are introduced and explored in Section
8
.
Section
9
is devoted to the study of spectral multipliers.
Bounded spectral multipliers on test functions and distributions
as well as on mixed-smoothness B and F-spaces are obtained.
In Section
10
, we develop the basics of ordinary B and F-spaces
associated with
L_{1},L_{2}
.
Examples of coordinate spaces covered by our theory are given in the appendix. We also place the lengthy proof of a proposition from Section
4
in the appendix.

Notation
:
Throughout this article
{\mathbb{N}}
stands for the set of all integers and
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}:={\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
;

\mathbb{R}_{+}:=(0,\infty)
and
{\mathbbm{1}}_{E}
stands for the characteristic function of the set
E
.
Also,
\|\cdot\|_{p}
stands for the
L^{p}
norm in the respective space.
As usual
\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R})
denotes the set of all functions on
\mathbb{R}
with continuous derivatives of order up to
k

and
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
stands for the Schwartz class on
\mathbb{R}
.
Positive constants are denoted by
c,\tilde{c},c_{1},\dots
and they may vary at every occurrence.
The notation
a\sim b
stands for
c^{-1}\leq a/b\leq c
.
We also use the standard notation

a\wedge b:=\min\{a,b\}
and

a\vee b:=\max\{a,b\}
.

As a general rule letters in bold like
{\mathbf{x}}
,
{\mathbf{y}}
,
{\mathbf{z}}
,
{\mathbf{t}}
,
{\bm{\delta}}
,
{\bm{\lambda}}
,
{\bm{\beta}}
,
{\bm{\gamma}}
, etc.
stand for vectors or multi-indices.
In particular, we will use the notation
{\bm{1}}:=(1,1)
and
{\bm{0}}:=(0,0)
.
For a vector, say,
{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
we denote
|{\bm{\lambda}}|:=(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})^{1/2}
,
while for a multi-index, say,
{\bm{\delta}}=(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
we denote
|{\bm{\delta}}|:=\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}
.
For multi-indices
{\bm{\delta}}=(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
,
{\bm{\sigma}}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
all operations should be understood coordinate-wise,
in particular,

{\bm{\delta}}\pm{\bm{\sigma}}:=(\delta_{1}\pm\sigma_{1},\delta_{2}\pm\sigma_{2})
,

{\bm{\delta}}{\bm{\sigma}}:=(\delta_{1}\sigma_{1},\delta_{2}\sigma_{2})
,

{\bm{\delta}}/{\bm{\sigma}}:=(\delta_{1}/\sigma_{1},\delta_{2}/\sigma_{2})
,
especially,
{\bm{1}}/{\bm{\delta}}={\bm{\delta}}^{-1}:=\big(\frac{1}{\delta_{1}},\frac{1}{\delta_{2}}\big)
,

{\bm{\delta}}^{{\bm{\sigma}}}:=\delta_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}\delta_{2}^{\sigma_{2}}
,
and

{\bm{\delta}}\cdot{\bm{\sigma}}:=\delta_{1}\sigma_{1}+\delta_{2}\sigma_{2}
.
Also,

{\bm{\sigma}}\geq{\bm{\delta}}
stands for
\sigma_{i}\geq\delta_{i}
,
i=1,2
.

2.
Setting and geometry of the coordinate spaces

As alluded to in the introduction we are interested in studying
function spaces defined on the product
X=X_{1}\times X_{2}

of spaces
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
of homogeneous type associated with shelf-adjoint operators
L_{1},L_{2}
.
In what follows we present the main conditions on the coordinate spaces
X_{1}
,
X_{2}

as well as some of their basic properties.

2.1.
The setting

We will operate in the following general setting, where for
i=1,2
we stipulate:

I.
(X_{i},\rho_{i},\mu_{i})
is a metric measure space such that

(X_{i},\rho_{i})
is locally compact with distance
\rho_{i}(\cdot,\cdot)

and
\mu_{i}
is a positive Radon measure.
We assume the following
doubling volume condition
:
There exists a constant
c_{i0}>1
such that

(2.1)

0<\mu_{i}(B_{i}(x_{i},2r))\leq c_{i0}\mu_{i}(B_{i}(x_{i},r))<\infty\quad\hbox{for $x_{i}\in X_{i}$ and $r>0$,}

where

B_{i}(x_{i},r):=\big\{y_{i}\in X_{i}:\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})<r\big\}
.
We will use the abbreviated notation

(2.2)

V_{i}(x_{i},r):=\mu_{i}(B_{i}(x_{i},r)).

II. The space
(X_{i},\rho_{i},\mu_{i})
is accompanied by
a densely defined non-negative self-adjoint
operator

L_{i}
, mapping real-valued to real-valued functions,
such that the associated semigroup
P_{i,t}=e^{-tL_{i}}
consists of integral operators with
(heat) kernel
p_{i,t}(x_{i},y_{i})
obeying the conditions:

(a)

Gaussian upper bound:
There exist constants
c_{i1},\;c_{i2}>0
such that

(2.3)

|p_{i,t}(x_{i},y_{i})|\leq\frac{c_{i1}\exp\big(-\frac{\rho_{i}^{2}(x_{i},y_{i})}{c_{i2}t}\big)}{\big[V_{i}(x_{i},\sqrt{t})V_{i}(y_{i},\sqrt{t})\big]^{1/2}}\quad\hbox{for}\;\;x_{i},y_{i}\in X_{i},\,t>0.

(b)

Hölder continuity:
There exists a constant
\alpha_{i}>0
such that

(2.4)

\big|p_{i,t}(x_{i},y_{i})-p_{i,t}(x_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\big|\leq c_{i1}\Big(\frac{\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)^{\alpha_{i}}\frac{\exp\big(-\frac{\rho_{i}^{2}(x_{i},y_{i})}{c_{i2}t}\big)}{\big[V_{i}(x_{i},\sqrt{t})V_{i}(y_{i},\sqrt{t})\big]^{1/2}}

for
x_{i},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime}\in X_{i}
and
t>0
, whenever
\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\sqrt{t}
.

(c)

Markov property:

(2.5)

\int_{X_{i}}p_{i,t}(x_{i},y_{i})d\mu_{i}(y_{i})=1\quad\hbox{for $t>0$.}

There is a variety of spaces
(X_{i},\rho_{i},\mu_{i})
associated with operators
L_{i}

that are covered by this setting. Several examples are given in Section
11.1
.

In a few instances we will assume that the following additional condition is satisfied:

(d)

Non-collapsing condition:

There exists a constant
c_{i3}>0
such that

(2.6)

\inf_{x_{i}\in X_{i}}V_{i}(x_{i},1)\geq c_{i3}

Note that this condition is always satisfied in the case of compact spaces (see
[
12
]
).
However, we do not consider (
2.6
) a part of our general setting.

Observe that
e^{-tL_{i}}
extends to a holomorphic semigroup on
L^{2}(X_{i})
and
from (
2.3
) and (
2.1
) it follows that
e^{-zL_{i}}
,
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
,
is an integral operator with kernel
p_{i,z}(x_{i},y_{i})
satisfying

(2.7)

|p_{i,z}(x_{i},y_{i})|\leq\frac{c_{i1}\exp\big(-c_{i2}\operatorname{Re}\frac{\rho_{i}^{2}(x_{i},y_{i})}{z}\big)}{\big[V_{i}(x_{i},\sqrt{\operatorname{Re}z})V_{i}(y_{i},\sqrt{\operatorname{Re}z})\big]^{1/2}}\quad\hbox{for}\;\;x_{i},y_{i}\in X_{i},\;\operatorname{Re}z>0.

Furthermore, by analytic continuation it follows from (
2.5
) that

(2.8)

\int_{X_{i}}p_{i,z}(x_{i},y_{i})d\mu_{i}(y_{i})=1,\quad\hbox{$\operatorname{Re}z>0$.}

For more details, see (1.5) and (1.8) in
[
12
]
.

From the doubling volume property (
2.1
) it readily follows that there exist constants
\hat{c}_{i0}>0
and
0<d_{i}\leq\log_{2}c_{i0}
such that

(2.9)

V_{i}(x_{i},\lambda r)\leq\hat{c}_{i0}\lambda^{d_{i}}V_{i}(x_{i},r)\quad\hbox{for every}\;\;x_{i}\in X_{i},r>0,\text{ and }\lambda\geq 1.

Of course
d_{i}
can be a non-integer number; it serves as a dimension of the doubling space.

2.2.
Auxiliary inequalities

We next present several inequalities that will be needed in what follows;
their simple proofs can be found in
[
12
,
39
]
.

The volumes of balls with different centers
x_{i},y_{i}\in X_{i},i=1,2
, and the same radius
r>0

can be compared using the inequality

(2.10)

V_{i}(x_{i},r)^{\gamma_{i}}\leq c\big(1+r^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{d_{i}|\gamma_{i}|}V_{i}(y_{i},r)^{\gamma_{i}},\quad\gamma_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}.

If we assume the non-collapsing condition (
2.6
),
then using (
2.9
) it follows that

(2.11)

V_{i}(x_{i},r)\geq(c_{i3}/c_{i})r^{d_{i}},\quad x_{i}\in X_{i},\ 0<r\leq 1.

If
\tau_{i}>d_{i}
, then there exists a constant
c=c(\tau_{i})>0
such that

(2.12)

\int_{X_{i}}\big(1+r^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{-\tau_{i}}d\mu_{i}(y_{i})\leq cV_{i}(x_{i},r),\quad r>0,

and

(2.13)

\int_{X_{i}}\frac{d\mu_{i}(y_{i})}{\big(1+r^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{\tau_{i}}\big(1+r^{-1}\rho_{i}(y_{i},z_{i})\big)^{\tau_{i}}}\leq\frac{cV_{i}(x_{i},r)}{\big(1+r^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},z_{i})\big)^{\tau_{i}-d_{i}}}.

If
\tau_{i}>2d_{i}
, then there exists a constant
c=c(\tau_{i})>0
such that

(2.14)

\int_{X_{i}}\frac{d\mu_{i}(y_{i})}{V_{i}(y_{i},r)\big(1+r^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{\tau_{i}}\big(1+r^{-1}\rho_{i}(y_{i},z_{i})\big)^{\tau_{i}}}\leq\frac{c}{\big(1+r^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},z_{i})\big)^{\tau_{i}}},

and if in addition
r\leq 1
, then

(2.15)

\int_{X_{i}}\frac{d\mu_{i}(y_{i})}{V_{i}(y_{i},r)\big(1+r^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{\tau_{i}}\big(1+\rho_{i}(y_{i},z_{i})\big)^{\tau_{i}}}\leq\frac{c}{\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},z_{i})\big)^{\tau_{i}}}.

2.3.
Functional calculus

Since
L_{i}
is a non-negative self-adjoint operator, by Spectral theory
there exists a unique spectral resolution
E^{i}_{\lambda}
associated to
L_{i}
,
consisting of orthoprojections in
L^{2}(X_{i},d\mu_{i})
such that

(2.16)

L_{i}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda dE^{i}_{\lambda}.

Moreover,
L_{i}
maps real-valued to real-valued functions and for any real-valued, measurable and
bounded function
F_{i}
on
[0,\infty)
the operator (spectral multiplier)
F_{i}(L_{i})
, defined by

(2.17)

F_{i}(L_{i}):=\int_{0}^{\infty}F_{i}(\lambda)dE^{i}_{\lambda}

is bounded on
L^{2}(X_{i},d\mu_{i})
, self-adjoint, and also maps real-valued functions to real-valued functions.
Furthermore, if
F_{i}(L_{i})
is an integral operator,
then its kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{i}(L_{i})}(x_{i},y_{i})

is real-valued and

{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{i}(L_{i})}(y_{i},x_{i})={\mathscr{K}}_{F_{i}(L_{i})}(x_{i},y_{i})
.
In particular, for the heat kernel
p_{i,t}(x_{i},y_{i})

we have
p_{i,t}(x_{i},y_{i})\in\mathbb{R}
and
p_{i,t}(y_{i},x_{i})=p_{i,t}(x_{i},y_{i})
.

The following
Davies-Gaffney estimate
follows from our basic assumptions I - II (see
[
13
,
39
]
):

(2.18)

|\langle P_{i,t}f_{1},f_{2}\rangle|\leq\exp\Big\{-\frac{c^{\star}_{i}r^{2}}{t}\Big\}\|f_{1}\|_{2}\|f_{2}\|_{2},\quad t>0,

for all open sets
U_{j}\subset X_{i}
and
f_{j}\in L^{2}(X_{i})
with
\operatorname{supp}f_{j}\subset U_{j}
,
j=1,2
,
where
r:=\rho(U_{1},U_{2})
and
c^{\star}_{i}=c_{i2}^{-1}
with
c_{i2}>0
the constant from (
2.3
).

In turn, the Davies-Gaffney estimate implies (is equivalent to, see
[
13
]
)
the
finite speed propagation property
, which will play an important role in what follows:

(2.19)

\big\langle\cos(t\sqrt{L_{i}})f_{1},f_{2}\big\rangle=0,\quad 0<{\tilde{c}}_{i}t<r,\quad{\tilde{c}}_{i}:=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{c^{\star}_{i}}},

for all open sets
U_{j}\subset X_{i}
,
f_{j}\in L^{2}(X_{i})
,
\operatorname{supp}f_{j}\subset U_{j}
,

j=1,2
, where
r:=\rho(U_{1},U_{2})
.

We next present the main localization result in the functional calculus induced by
L_{i}
(see
[
39
, Theorem 3.4]
).

Theorem 2.1
.

For
i=1,2
let
F_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{k_{i}}({\mathbb{R}})
,
k_{i}>d_{i}
,
be real-valued and even and let

(2.20)

|F_{i}^{(\nu)}(\lambda)|\leq c^{\diamond}(1+\lambda)^{-r_{i}}\ \text{for}\ \lambda\geq 0\ \text{and}\ 0\leq\nu\leq k_{i},\ \text{where}\ r_{i}\geq k_{i}+d_{i}+1.

Then
F_{i}(\delta\sqrt{L}_{i})
,
\delta>0
, is an integral operator with kernel

{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{i}(\delta\sqrt{L}_{i})}(x_{i},y_{i})
satisfying

(2.21)

\big|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{i}(\delta\sqrt{L}_{i})}(x_{i},y_{i})\big|\leq c^{\prime}V_{i}(y_{i},\delta)^{-1}\big(1+\delta^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{-k_{i}+d_{i}/2}

and

(2.22)

\big|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{i}(\delta\sqrt{L}_{i})}(x_{i},y_{i})-{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{i}(\delta\sqrt{L}_{i})}(x_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\big|\leq\frac{c^{\prime\prime}V_{i}(y_{i},\delta)^{-1}\big(\frac{\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})}{\delta}\big)^{\alpha_{i}}}{\big(1+\delta^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{k_{i}-d_{i}/2}},

if
\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\delta
,
where
\alpha_{i}
is the constant from (
2.4
)
and the constant
c^{\prime}>0
depends on the structural constants from (
2.1
), (
2.3
), (
2.4
), and
c^{\diamond}
,
k_{i}
;
the constant
c^{\prime\prime}
depends in addition on
\alpha
.
Furthermore,

\int_{X_{i}}{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{i}(\delta\sqrt{L}_{i})}(x_{i},y_{i})d\mu_{i}(y_{i})=F_{i}(0),\quad x_{i}\in X_{i}.

Note that instead of the decay condition (
2.20
)
one can assume that
F_{i}
is an even
\mathcal{C}^{k_{i}}({\mathbb{R}})
function with compact support.

3.
Product metric measure spaces

In this section we recall the definition of product measure spaces and set up our notation.

We assume that
(X_{1},\rho_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(X_{2},\rho_{2},\mu_{2})
are two measure metric spaces
associated with non-negative self-adjoint operators
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, respectively,
which satisfy conditions I – II of Section
2.1
.
The
product space

X=X_{1}\times X_{2}
is defined by

X=X_{1}\times X_{2}:=\big\{{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2}):\;x_{i}\in X_{i},\;i=1,2\big\}.

We consider
X
equipped with the
metric

(3.1)

\rho({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}):=\max\big(\rho_{1}(x_{1},y_{1}),\rho_{2}(x_{2},y_{2})\big),\quad{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X,

and the
product measure

\mu:=\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
, that is,

(3.2)

d\mu({\mathbf{x}})=d\mu_{1}(x_{1})d\mu_{2}(x_{2}),\quad{\mathbf{x}}\in X.

Clearly, the
ball

B({\mathbf{x}},r)\subset X
centered at
{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in X
of radius
r>0

is given by

(3.3)

B({\mathbf{x}},r)=B_{1}(x_{1},r)\times B_{2}(x_{2},r)

and

(3.4)

\mu(B({\mathbf{x}},r))=\mu_{1}(B_{1}(x_{1},r))\mu_{2}(B_{2}(x_{2},r)),

where
B_{i}(x_{i},r)
is the respective balls in
X_{i}
.
Therefore, by (
2.1
) it follows that
(X,\rho,\mu)
has the doubling volume property

(3.5)

\mu(B({\mathbf{x}},2r))\leq c_{10}c_{20}\mu(B({\mathbf{x}},r))

and from (
2.9
)

(3.6)

\mu(B({\mathbf{x}},\lambda r))\leq c\lambda^{d_{1}+d_{2}}\mu(B({\mathbf{x}},r)),\quad\forall r>0,\;\lambda\geq 1.

The following dimension-type vector

(3.7)

{\mathbf{d}}:=(d_{1},d_{2}).

will appear frequently in what follows.

For any
{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in X
and
{\bm{\delta}}\in(0,\infty)^{2}

we define the
rectangle

Q({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})
by

(3.8)

Q({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}}):=B_{1}(x_{1},\delta_{1})\times B_{2}(x_{2},\delta_{2}).

To simplify our notation we set for
{\mathbf{x}}\in X
,
{\bm{\delta}}\in(0,\infty)^{2}
,

(3.9)

V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}}):=\mu(Q({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}}))=\mu_{1}(B_{1}(x_{1},\delta_{1}))\mu_{2}(B_{2}(x_{2},\delta_{2}))=V_{1}(x_{1},\delta_{1})V_{2}(x_{2},\delta_{2}).

Also, for
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X
and
{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}\in(0,\infty)^{2}
, we define

(3.10)

{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}):=\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\delta_{i}^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{-\sigma_{i}}\quad\hbox{and}\quad

(3.11)

{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}):=\frac{{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})}{[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})]^{1/2}}=\frac{\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\delta_{i}^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{-\sigma_{i}}}{[V_{1}(x_{1},\delta_{1})V_{2}(x_{2},\delta_{2})V_{1}(y_{1},\delta_{1})V_{2}(y_{2},\delta_{2})]^{1/2}}.

3.1.
Geometric properties of product spaces

By (
2.9
) and (
3.9
) it readily follows that

(3.12)

V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\lambda}}{\bm{\delta}})\leq c{\bm{\lambda}}^{\mathbf{d}}V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})),\quad{\bm{\delta}}\in(0,\infty)^{2},\;{\bm{\lambda}}\in[1,\infty)^{2}.

Recall the notation
{\bm{\lambda}}{\bm{\delta}}:=(\lambda_{1}\delta_{1},\lambda_{2}\delta_{2})

and
{\bm{\lambda}}^{\mathbf{d}}:=\lambda_{1}^{d_{1}}\lambda_{2}^{d_{2}}
.

To compare the volumes of products of balls with different centers we use (
2.10
) and (
3.9
)
to obtain

(3.13)

\displaystyle V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})

\displaystyle\leq cV({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\delta_{i}^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{d_{i}}

\displaystyle=cV({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}}){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{d}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})^{-1}.

Using (
3.13
) it easily follows that

(3.14)

{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

We will frequently use the following notation

(3.15)

V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{\bm{\gamma}}:=V_{1}(x_{1},\delta_{1})^{\gamma_{1}}V_{2}(x_{2},\delta_{2})^{\gamma_{2}},

where
{\mathbf{x}}\in X
,
{\bm{\delta}}\in(0,\infty)^{2}
,
{\bm{\gamma}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
As a variation of this we will use for example the notation

(3.16)

V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}:=V_{1}(x_{1},2^{-j_{1}})^{-s_{1}/d_{1}}V_{2}(x_{2},2^{-j_{2}})^{-s_{2}/d_{2}},

where by definition
2^{-{\mathbf{j}}}:=(2^{-j_{1}},2^{-j_{2}})
,
{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}:=(s_{1}/d_{1},s_{2}/d_{2})
.

From (
2.10
) and (
3.9
) we get the following useful inequality
when changing centers of balls:

(3.17)

V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{\bm{\gamma}}\leq cV({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})^{\bm{\gamma}}\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\delta_{i}^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{d_{i}|\gamma_{i}|},\quad{\bm{\gamma}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

If we assume that the non-collapsing condition (
2.6
) is satisfied,
then (
2.11
) is valid, which in turn implies

(3.18)

V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})\geq c{\bm{\delta}}^{{\mathbf{d}}},\quad{\bm{\delta}}\in(0,1]^{2}.

3.2.
Integral estimates

The following integral estimates follow readily from (
2.12
)–(
2.15
):
If
{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}\in(0,\infty)^{2}
and
{\bm{\sigma}}>{\mathbf{d}}
,
i.e.
\sigma_{i}>d_{i}
,
i=1,2
,
then

(3.19)

\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}}),\quad\forall{\mathbf{x}}\in X,\quad\text{and}

(3.20)

\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{z}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}}){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}-{\mathbf{d}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}}),\quad\forall{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}}\in X.

Furthermore, if
{\bm{\sigma}}>2{\mathbf{d}}
,
then

(3.21)

\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}){\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{z}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\leq c{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}}),\quad\forall{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}}\in X,

and if in addition
{\bm{\delta}}\in(0,1]^{2}
, then

(3.22)

\int_{X}V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{z}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\leq c{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}}),\quad\forall{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}}\in X.

4.
Two-parameter functional calculus on product spaces

The present section lays down some of the ground work that will be needed in developing our theory,
namely, the development of the two-parameter functional calculus induced by the operators
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
on the doubling product measure spaces
X=X_{1}\times X_{2}
.
This theory includes the development of the associated two-parameter spectral measure and spectral resolution
as well as the development of associated integral operators with highly localized kernels.
The theory of the two-parameter spectral measure and spectral resolution in our setting is in principe well-know (see e.g.
[
50
]
or
[
45
]
).
However, since we do not find it in the form we needed in the literature we will present it in sufficient detail.
The development of integral operators with highly localized kernels in our setting is based on the respective one-parameter integral operators from
[
12
,
39
]
.

Just as in Section
2.1
we assume that
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
are two
densely defined self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(X_{1},d\mu_{1})
and
L^{2}(X_{2},d\mu_{2})
, respectively.
We also assume that the spaces
L^{2}(X_{1},d\mu_{1})
and
L^{2}(X_{2},d\mu_{2})
are separable.
As is shown in
[
39
]
this is the case in our setting because there exist countable frames for the respective spaces.
At this point we do not assume that
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
are non-negative operators.

We first recall the definition and basic properties of tensor product operators of the form

L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}}
and then
present the basics of the two-parameter functional calculus induced by
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
.
We will use the standard notation
f\otimes g
for the tensor product of any two functions
f,g

defined on
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
,
that is,

(4.1)

f\otimes g({\mathbf{x}}):=f(x_{1})g(x_{2}),\quad{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in X_{1}\times X_{2}.

4.1.
Tensor product operators of the form
L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}}

For
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
we denote by
D(L_{1}^{\nu_{1}})
and
D(L_{2}^{\nu_{2}})

the domains of the operators
L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}
and
L_{2}^{\nu_{2}}
.
Since
L_{i}
is self-adjoint, then
L_{i}^{\nu_{i}}
is also self-adjoint for any
\nu_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
i=1,2

(see e.g.
[
46
, Theorem VIII.6]
).
Denote by
D(L_{1}^{\nu_{1}})\otimes D(L_{2}^{\nu_{2}})
the set of all finite linear combinations of functions
of the form
f\otimes g
, where
f\in D(L_{1}^{\nu_{1}})
and
g\in D(L_{2}^{\nu_{2}})
.
As
D(L_{i}^{\nu_{i}})
is dense in
L^{2}(X_{i},d\mu_{i})
,
i=1,2
,
then
D(L_{1}^{\nu_{1}})\otimes D(L_{2}^{\nu_{2}})
is dense in
L^{2}(X_{1}\times X_{2},d\mu_{1}\otimes d\mu_{2})
.
The operator
L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}}
is defined on
D(L_{1}^{\nu_{1}})\otimes D(L_{2}^{\nu_{2}})
by

(4.2)

\big(L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}}\big)\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}\otimes g_{j}\Big):=\sum_{j=1}^{N}L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}f_{j}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}}g_{j}

for any

\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}\otimes g_{j}\in D(L_{1}^{\nu_{1}})\otimes D(L_{2}^{\nu_{2}})
,
N<\infty
.
As is well-known
L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}}
is well defined
and the following claim is valid, see e.g.
[
46
, Section VIII.10]
.

Proposition 4.1
.

For any
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
the operator
L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}}
is essentially self-adjoint
on
D(L_{1}^{\nu_{1}})\otimes D(L_{2}^{\nu_{2}})
.

We will denote the closure of
L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}}
again by
L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}}
.

4.2.
Two-parameter spectral measure induced by
L_{1}
and
L_{2}

We will be dealing with operators
F(L_{1},L_{2})
on
L^{2}(X,d\mu)

generated by functions
F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
.

The space
\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu)
.

Denote by

\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu)=\mathscr{L}^{2}(X_{1}\times X_{2},d\mu_{1}\otimes d\mu_{2})

the subspace of
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
consisting of all functions of the form

\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}\otimes g_{j},\quad f_{j}\in L^{2}(X_{1},d\mu_{1}),\;g_{j}\in L^{2}(X_{2},d\mu_{2}),

for some
N<\infty
that may vary.

In what follows we will denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and
\|\cdot\|

the inner product and norm in
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
or
L^{2}(X_{i},d\mu_{i})
.
It will be clear from the context which
L^{2}
-space is considered.

For
i=1,2
, let
E_{i}
be the spectral measure induced by the self-adjoint operator
L_{i}
.
The operator
E_{i}
is an orthogonal projection operator in
L^{2}(X_{i},d\mu_{i})
.
More precisely, if we denote by
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})
the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathbb{R}
,
then
E_{i}(S)
is an orthogonal projector for any Borel set
S\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})

obeying the following conditions:

(a)
E_{i}(\mathbb{R})=I
, the identity operator, and

(b) for any sequence
\{S_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
of disjoint sets in
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})
one has

(4.3)

E_{i}\big(\cup_{n\geq 1}S_{n}\big)=\sum_{n\geq 1}E_{i}(S_{n}),

where the convergence is in the strong sense, i.e.

\Big\|E_{i}\big(\cup_{n\geq 1}S_{n}\big)f-\sum_{n=1}^{N}E_{i}(S_{n})f\Big\|\to 0\quad\hbox{as $\;\;N\to\infty$},\quad\forall f\in L^{2}(X_{i},d\mu_{i}).

We next define the product spectral measure
E=E_{1}\otimes E_{2}

on the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
in three steps.

Step 1.
Denote

(4.4)

\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}):=\{(a,b]:a<b\},\quad-\infty\leq a<b\leq\infty.

For any
J_{1},J_{2}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R})
we define the operator

(4.5)

E_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})=E_{1}(J_{1})\otimes E_{2}(J_{2})\quad\hbox{on}\quad\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu)

as follows:
For any

\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}\otimes g_{j}
in
\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu)
we set

(4.6)

E_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}\otimes g_{j}\Big):=\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{1}(J_{1})f_{j}\otimes E_{2}(J_{2})g_{j}.

Lemma 4.2
.

For any
J_{1},J_{2}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R})
the linear operator
E_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})
is bounded
as an operator from
\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu)
into
L^{2}(X,d\mu)

and
\|E_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})\|=1
.

Proof.

The argument here is quite standard.
We give it for completeness.

Let
\{u_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
and
\{v_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
be orthonormal bases
for the (separable Hilbert) spaces
L^{2}(X_{1},d\mu_{1})
and
L^{2}(X_{2},d\mu_{2})
, respectively.
As is well known, e.g.
[
46
, page 51]
,

\{u_{j}\otimes v_{k}\}_{j,k\geq 1}
is an orthonormal basis for
the space
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
.
Therefore, the set of all finite sums
\sum_{j,k}c_{jk}u_{j}\otimes v_{k}

that is a subspace of
\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu)
is dense in
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
.
For any such sum we have

\displaystyle\Big\|(E_{1}(J_{1})\otimes I)\sum_{j,k}c_{jk}u_{j}\otimes v_{k}\Big\|^{2}=\Big\|\sum_{k}\Big(E_{1}(J_{1})\sum_{j}c_{jk}u_{j}\Big)\otimes v_{k}\Big\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\Big\|E_{1}(J_{1})\sum_{j}c_{jk}u_{j}\Big\|^{2}\leq\sum_{k}\Big\|\sum_{j}c_{jk}u_{j}\Big\|^{2}=\sum_{j,k}|c_{jk}|^{2}=\Big\|\sum_{j,k}c_{jk}u_{j}\otimes v_{k}\Big\|^{2}

and hence

\|E_{1}(J_{1})\otimes I\|\leq 1
,
where
I
is the identity operator in
L^{2}(X_{2},d\mu_{2})
.
Above we used that

\|E_{1}(J_{1})\|=1
.
Similarly as above we get
\|I\otimes E_{2}(J_{2})\|\leq 1
.
Evidently,

E_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})=(E_{1}(J_{1})\otimes I)(I\otimes E_{2}(J_{2}))

and from above it follows that

(4.7)

\|E_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})\|\leq\|E_{1}(J_{1})\otimes I\|\|I\otimes E_{2}(J_{2})\|\leq 1.

For the inequality in the other direction, choose
f_{i}\in L^{2}(X_{i},d\mu_{i})
,
i=1,2
, so that

\|f_{i}\|_{L^{2}}=1
and

E_{i}(J_{i})f_{i}=f_{i}
.
Then

E_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})(f_{1}\otimes f_{2})=E_{1}(J_{1})f_{1}\otimes E_{2}(J_{2})f_{2}=f_{1}\otimes f_{2}

and hence

\|E_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})\|\geq 1
.
This along with (
4.7
) completes the proof.
∎

Because
\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu)
is dense in
L^{2}(X,d\mu)

and for any
J_{1},J_{2}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R})
the linear operator
E_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})
is bounded (
\|E_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})\|=1
)
there exists a unique extension of
E_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})
to
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
.
We denote this extension by
\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})
.

Proposition 4.3
.

For any
J_{1},J_{2}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R})
the linear operator
\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})
is self-adjoint and bounded
on
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
with norm
\|\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})\|=1
.
Moreover, the operator
\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})
is idempotent,
i.e.
\overline{E}_{0}^{2}(J_{1}\times J_{2})=\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})
.
Therefore,
\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})
is a projector.
In addition, for
J_{1},J_{2},I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R})

(4.8)

\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1}\times I_{2})=0\quad\hbox{if}\quad(J_{1}\times J_{2})\cap(I_{1}\times I_{2})=\emptyset.

Proof.

Fix
J_{1},J_{2}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R})
.
Since
E_{1}(J_{1})
is a projector there exists a closed subspace
H_{1}
of
L^{2}(X_{1},d\mu_{1})

such that
E_{1}(J_{1})
projects onto
H_{1}
.
We will use the notation
E_{H_{1}}:=E_{1}(J_{1})
.
Clearly, there exists an orthonormal basis
\{u_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
for the space
L^{2}(X_{1},d\mu_{1})

such that for each
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have either

E_{H_{1}}u_{j}=u_{j}

or

E_{H_{1}}u_{j}=0
.
Thus,
\{u_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
is the union of orthonormal bases
for the subspaces
H_{1}
and
H_{1}^{\perp}
.
Denote by
{\mathbb{N}}_{1}
the set of all
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
E_{H_{1}}u_{j}=u_{j}
,
i.e.
\{u_{j}\}_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{1}}
is a basis for the subspace
H_{1}
.

Similarly, there exists a closed subspace
H_{2}
of
L^{2}(X_{2},d\mu_{2})

such that
E_{2}(J_{2})
projects onto
H_{2}
.
Denote
E_{H_{2}}:=E_{2}(J_{2})
.
Also, assume that
\{v_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
is an orthonormal basis
for the spaces
L^{2}(X_{2},d\mu_{2})

such that for each
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have either

E_{H_{2}}v_{j}=v_{j}

or

E_{H_{2}}v_{j}=0
.
Denote by
{\mathbb{N}}_{2}
the set of all
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
E_{H_{2}}v_{j}=v_{j}
.

As was alluded to above
\{u_{j}\otimes v_{k}\}_{j,k\geq 1}
is an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
.
Then for any function
h\in L^{2}(X,d\mu)
we have

h=\sum_{j,k\geq 1}c_{jk}u_{j}\otimes v_{k},\quad c_{jk}=\langle h,u_{j}\otimes v_{k}\rangle.

With the notation from the proof of Lemma
4.2
we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})h

\displaystyle=\lim_{N,K\to\infty}E_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{K}c_{jk}u_{j}\otimes v_{k}\Big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N,K\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{K}c_{jk}E_{1}(J_{1})u_{j}\otimes E_{2}(J_{2})v_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k\geq 1}c_{jk}E_{H_{1}}u_{j}\otimes E_{H_{2}}v_{k}=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{1},k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{2}}c_{jk}E_{H_{1}}u_{j}\otimes E_{H_{2}}v_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{1},k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{2}}c_{jk}u_{j}\otimes v_{k},

where the convergence is in
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
; in the last three series the convergence is unconditional.
From above it follows that the operator
\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})
is symmetric
and

\|\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})h\|^{2}=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{1},k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{2}}|c_{jk}|^{2}\leq\|h\|^{2}
.
Hence
\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})
is self-adjoint.
Also, it follows readily that

\overline{E}_{0}^{2}(J_{1}\times J_{2})=\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1}\times J_{2})

and identity (
4.8
) is valid.
∎

Step 2.

Let
\mathscr{B}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be the collection of all sets
R
of the form

(4.9)

R=\cup_{j=1}^{N}J_{1j}\times J_{2j},\quad J_{ij}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}),\;(J_{1j}\times J_{2j})\cap(J_{1k}\times J_{2k})=\emptyset,j\neq k,

for some
N<\infty
.
It is readily seen that
\mathscr{B}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is an algebra (it is closed under finite unions and complements)
that generates the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

We now extend the definition of
\overline{E}_{0}(R)
to all sets
R
from
\mathscr{B}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
For any set
R
as in (
4.9
) we define

(4.10)

\overline{E}_{0}(R):=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1j}\times J_{2j})\quad\hbox{on}\quad L^{2}(X,d\mu).

Proposition 4.4
.

The operator
\overline{E}_{0}(R)
is well defined.
Furthermore,
\overline{E}_{0}(R)
is a projection-valued set function
that is a premeasure in
\mathscr{B}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
i.e.

\overline{E}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=I
, the identity operator,
and

(4.11)

\overline{E}_{0}\big(\cup_{j\geq 1}R_{j}\big)=\sum_{j\geq 1}\overline{E}_{0}(R_{j})

for any sequence
\{R_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
of disjoint sets from
\mathscr{B}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

such that

\cup_{j\geq 1}R_{j}\in\mathscr{B}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

To streamline our presentation we divert the lengthy proof of this proposition to the appendix.

Step 3.

The following theorem completes the construction of the two-parameter spectral measure
E
.

Theorem 4.5
.

There is a unique spectral measure
E(R)
on the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

such that

(4.12)

E(R)=\overline{E}_{0}(R),\quad\forall R\in\mathscr{B}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Recall that by definition
E(S)
is a spectral measure on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

if it obeys the following conditions:

E(\mathbb{R}^{2})=I
and

(4.13)

E\big(\cup_{j\geq 1}S_{j}\big)=\sum_{j\geq 1}E(S_{j})

for any sequence
\{S_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
of disjoint sets from
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

This theorem is standard and follows from Proposition
4.4
,
see e.g.
[
50
, Lemma 4.9]
.

From Theorem
4.5
it readily follows that

(4.14)

E(\emptyset)=0\quad\hbox{and}\quad E(S)E(R)=E(S\cap R),\;\forall S,R\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Also, as a projector operator
E(S)
for
S\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a self-adjoint bounded operator on
L^{2}(X,d\mu)

with
\|E(S)\|=1
.

4.3.
Definition of operators
F(L_{1},L_{2})

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a measurable and bounded function.
We next define the operator
F(L_{1},L_{2})
.

Let
E
be the two-parameter spectral measure defined above.
As is well known and easy to see for any
f\in L^{2}(X,d\mu)

(4.15)

\nu_{f}(S):=\langle E(S)f,f\rangle,\quad S\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

is a finite measure on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Clearly,

(4.16)

\nu_{f}(S)=\langle E(S)^{2}f,f\rangle=\langle E(S)f,E(S)f\rangle=\|E(S)f\|^{2}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\nu_{f}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=\|f\|^{2}.

From (
4.15
) it follows by polarization that for any
f,g\in L^{2}(X,d\mu)

(4.17)

\nu_{f,g}(S):=\langle E(S)f,g\rangle,\quad S\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

is a linear combination of four positive measures on
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Therefore,

(4.18)

(f,g):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Fd\nu_{f,g},\quad f,g\in L^{2}(X,d\mu),

is a bilinear form on
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
.

We next show that the bilinear form
(f,g)
is bounded.
By a standard construction in Measure theory (see e.g.
[
24
]
) there exist a sequence
\{\phi_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}

of simple functions

\phi_{k}=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}c_{kj}{\mathbbm{1}}_{S_{kj}},\quad S_{kj}\cap S_{k\ell}=\emptyset,\;j\neq\ell,

such that

|\phi_{1}|\leq|\phi_{2}|\leq\cdots\leq|F|\leq\|F\|_{\infty}

and for any
f,g\in L^{2}(X,d\mu)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi_{k}({\bm{\lambda}})d\nu_{f,g}({\bm{\lambda}})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F({\bm{\lambda}})d\nu_{f,g}({\bm{\lambda}}).

We have

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}

\displaystyle\phi_{k}({\bm{\lambda}})d\nu_{f,g}({\bm{\lambda}})\Big|=\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}c_{kj}\nu_{f,g}(S_{kj})\Big|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}|c_{kj}||\langle E(S_{kj})f,g\rangle|

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}|c_{kj}||\langle E(S_{kj})f,E(S_{kj})g\rangle|\leq\|F\|_{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\|E(S_{kj})f\|\|E(S_{kj})g\|

\displaystyle\leq\|F\|_{\infty}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\|E(S_{kj})f\|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\|E(S_{kj})g\|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\|F\|_{\infty}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\langle E(S_{kj})f,f\rangle\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\langle E(S_{kj})g,g\rangle\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\|F\|_{\infty}\Big|\Big\langle\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}E(S_{kj})\Big)f,f\Big\rangle\Big|^{1/2}\Big|\Big\langle\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}E(S_{kj})\Big)g,g\Big\rangle\Big|^{1/2}\leq\|F\|_{\infty}\|f\|\|g\|.

Above we used twice the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the fact that

E(S_{kj})
and
\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}E(S_{kj})
are projectors.
From above it follows that

|(f,g)|\leq\|F\|_{\infty}\|f\|\|g\|
.
Therefore, by the Riesz theorem there exists a bounded linear operator, denoted by
F(L_{1},L_{2})
, on
L^{2}(X,d\mu)

such that

\langle F(L_{1},L_{2})f,g\rangle=(f,g).

Thus, we define
F(L_{1},L_{2})
by

(4.19)

\langle F(L_{1},L_{2})f,g\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Fd\langle Ef,g\rangle,\quad\forall f,g\in L^{2}(X,d\mu).

Clearly,

\|F(L_{1},L_{2})\|\leq\|F\|_{\infty}
.

Proposition 4.6
.

If
F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
is a real-valued bounded Borel measurable function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
then the operator
F(L_{1},L_{2})
is self-adjoint.

Proof.

If
f,g\in L^{2}(X,d\mu)
, then from (
4.19
) we get

\displaystyle\langle F(L_{1},L_{2})f,g\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Fd\langle Ef,g\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Fd\langle f,Eg\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Fd\overline{\langle Eg,f\rangle}

\displaystyle=\overline{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Fd\langle Eg,f\rangle}=\overline{\langle F(L_{1},L_{2})g,f\rangle}=\langle f,F(L_{1},L_{2})g\rangle,

where we used that the operator
E(S)
is self-adjoint, and
\overline{z}
stands for the complex conjugate of
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
.
Therefore, the operator
F(L_{1},L_{2})
is symmetric and bounded and hence it is self-adjoint.
∎

Some properties of the operators
F(L_{1},L_{2})
are given in the following

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
and
G(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
be two bounded Borel measurable functions on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Then for any
a,b\in{\mathbb{C}}

(4.20)

(aF+bG)(L_{1},L_{2})=aF(L_{1},L_{2})+bG(L_{1},L_{2})

and

(4.21)

(FG)(L_{1},L_{2})=F(L_{1},L_{2})G(L_{1},L_{2}),

and, therefore,

F(L_{1},L_{2})
and
G(L_{1},L_{2})
commute.

For the proof of this theorem we need the following well known

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
(X,d\mu)
be a measure space.
Assume that
f
and
g
are
\mu
-measurable complex-valued functions on
X
and

fg
and
g
are
\mu
-integrable.
Let the complex measure
\mu_{1}
be defined by

\mu_{1}(S):=\int_{S}g({\bm{\lambda}})d\mu({\bm{\lambda}})\quad\hbox{for any measurable set}\;\;S\subset X.

Then
f
is
\mu_{1}
-integrable and

\int_{X}f({\bm{\lambda}})d\mu_{1}({\bm{\lambda}})=\int_{X}f({\bm{\lambda}})g({\bm{\lambda}})d\mu({\bm{\lambda}}).

Proof of Theorem
4.7
.

Identity (
4.20
) follows readily by the definition of
F(L_{1},L_{2})
and
G(L_{1},L_{2})
.

We now prove (
4.21
).
Let
f,g\in L^{2}(X,d\mu)
. Then using (
4.19
) we have

\langle F(L_{1},L_{2})G(L_{1},L_{2})f,g\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Fd\langle EG(L_{1},L_{2})f,g\rangle.

Using again (
4.19
) we have for any
S\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

(4.22)

\langle E(S)G(L_{1},L_{2})f,g\rangle=\langle G(L_{1},L_{2})f,E(S)g\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Gd\langle Ef,E(S)g\rangle.

But, for each
R\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

\langle E(R)f,E(S)g\rangle=\langle E(S)E(R)f,g\rangle=\langle E(S\cap R)f,g\rangle,

where we used (
4.14
).
Hence the measure
\nu_{f,g}:=\langle E(R)f,E(S)g\rangle
,
R\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, where
S\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is fixed,
vanishes on sets outside
S
.
From this and (
4.22
) it follows that

\displaystyle\langle E(S)G(L_{1},L_{2})f,g\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Gd\langle Ef,E(S)g\rangle=\int_{S}Gd\langle Ef,g\rangle.

Now, appealing to Lemma
4.8
we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Fd\langle EG(L_{1},L_{2})f,g\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}FGd\langle Ef,g\rangle=\langle(FG)(L_{1},L_{2})f,g\rangle,

and (
4.21
) follows.
∎

4.4.
Spectral resolution (decomposition of the identity)

It is sometimes more convenient to work with a spectral resolution rather then a spectral measure.
For any
{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we define
the
spectral function

E_{\bm{\lambda}}
by

(4.23)

E_{\bm{\lambda}}=E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}:=E(I_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}),\quad I_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}:=(-\infty,\lambda_{1}]\times(-\infty,\lambda_{2}].

It is easy to see that the projection-valued function
E_{\bm{\lambda}}=E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}

has the following properties:

(i)

{\rm Range}\,(E_{\bm{\lambda}})\subset{\rm Range}\,(E_{{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}})\quad\hbox{if}\quad{\bm{\lambda}}\leq{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}
,
where for
{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
,
{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}=(\lambda^{\prime}_{1},\lambda^{\prime}_{2})

{\bm{\lambda}}\leq{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}
means that
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{1}^{\prime}
and
\lambda_{2}\leq\lambda_{2}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

E_{\bm{\lambda}}=\lim_{{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}\to{\bm{\lambda}},{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}\geq{\bm{\lambda}}}E_{{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}}
,

(iii)

E_{-\infty}=\lim_{{\bm{\lambda}}\to-\infty}E_{\bm{\lambda}}=0
and

E_{\infty}=\lim_{{\bm{\lambda}}\to\infty}E_{\bm{\lambda}}=I
.

Above
{\bm{\lambda}}\to-\infty
stands for
\lambda_{1}\to-\infty
and
\lambda_{2}\to-\infty
,
and the convergence is in the strong sense.

As is well known properties (i) - (iii) completely characterise the spectral measure
E(S)
,
see e.g.
[
45
, Theorem 5.6]
.

Using the notation from above it follows from (
4.16
) and (
4.17
)
that for any
f,g\in L^{2}(X,d\mu)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}1\,d\|E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}f\|^{2}=\|f\|^{2}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}1\,d\langle E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}f,g\rangle=\langle f,g\rangle.

Furthermore, from (
4.19
) it follows that for any bounded Borel measurable function

F:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to{\mathbb{C}}

(4.24)

\langle F(L_{1},L_{2})f,g\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})d\langle E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}f,g\rangle,\quad\forall f,g\in L^{2}(X,d\mu).

4.5.
Tensor-product integral operators

Let
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
be two bounded Borel measurable functions on
\mathbb{R}
.
Set
F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=F_{1}(\lambda_{1})F_{2}(\lambda_{2})
.
We define the operator
F_{1}(L_{1})\otimes F_{2}(L_{2})
by

(4.25)

F_{1}(L_{1})\otimes F_{2}(L_{2}):=F(L_{1},L_{2}).

It is easy to see that if
f_{i}\in L^{2}(X_{i},d\mu_{i})
,
i=1,2
,
then with
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
as above we have

(4.26)

\big[F_{1}(L_{1})\otimes F_{2}(L_{2})\big](f_{1}\otimes f_{2})=\big[F_{1}(L_{1})f_{1}\big]\otimes\big[F_{2}(L_{2})f_{2}\big]\quad\hbox{(see \eqref{def-FL1L2})}.

Tensor-product integral operators will play an important role.
Let the operators
F_{i}(L_{i})
,
i=1,2
, be as above
and assume that
F_{i}(L_{i})
is an integral operator of the form

(4.27)

F_{i}(L_{i})f(x_{i}):=\int_{X_{i}}{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{i}(L_{i})}(x_{i},y_{i})f(y_{i})d\mu_{i}(y_{i}),

where

(4.28)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{i}(L_{i})}(x_{i},y_{i})|\leq c\big[V_{i}(x_{i},\delta_{i})V(y_{i},\delta_{i})\big]^{-1/2}\big(1+\delta_{i}^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{-\sigma_{i}}

with
\delta_{i}>0
and
\sigma_{i}>2d_{i}
.
Then it follows from (
2.10
) and (
2.12
) that

\sup_{x_{i}\in X_{i}}\|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{i}(L_{i})}(x_{i},\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}\leq c\quad\hbox{and}\quad\sup_{y_{i}\in X_{i}}\|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{i}(L_{i})}(\cdot,y_{i})\|_{L^{1}}\leq c.

Now, by the Schur lemma or interpolation it follows that

(4.29)

\|F_{i}(L_{i})f\|_{p}\leq c^{\prime}\|f\|_{p},\quad\forall f\in L^{p}(X_{i},d\mu_{i}),\;1\leq p\leq\infty.

Proposition 4.9
.

Under the assumptions from above

F_{1}(L_{1})\otimes F_{2}(L_{2})

is an integral operator with kernel

(4.30)

{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{1}(L_{1})\otimes F_{2}(L_{2})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})={\mathscr{K}}_{F_{1}(L_{1})}(x_{1},y_{1}){\mathscr{K}}_{F_{2}(L_{2})}(x_{2},y_{2})

and hence

(4.31)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{1}(L_{1})\otimes F_{2}(L_{2})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c\frac{\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1-\frac{\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})}{\delta_{i}}\big)^{-\sigma_{i}}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}}=c{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

Proof.

If
f_{i}\in L^{2}(X_{i},d\mu_{i})
,
i=1,2
, then by (
4.26
)

\displaystyle\big[F_{1}(L_{1})

\displaystyle\otimes F_{2}(L_{2})\big](f_{1}\otimes f_{2})({\mathbf{x}})=\big[F_{1}(L_{1})f_{1}(x_{1})\big]\big[F_{2}(L_{2})f_{2}(x_{2})\big]

\displaystyle=\int_{X_{1}}{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{1}(L_{1})}(x_{1},y_{1})f_{1}(y_{1})d\mu_{1}(y_{1})\int_{X_{2}}{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{2}(L_{2})}(x_{2},y_{2})f_{2}(y_{2})d\mu_{1}(y_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{X_{1}\times X_{2}}{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{1}(L_{1})}(x_{1},y_{1}){\mathscr{K}}_{F_{2}(L_{2})}(x_{2},y_{2})(f_{1}\otimes f_{2})(y_{1},y_{2})d\mu(y_{1},y_{2}),

where the last equality is easily justified applying Fubini’s theorem.
From above it readily follows that for any function

\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}\otimes g_{j}
in
\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu)
we have

\displaystyle\big[F_{1}(L_{1})\otimes F_{2}(L_{2})\big]\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}\otimes g_{j}\Big)({\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle=\int_{X_{1}\times X_{2}}{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{1}(L_{1})}(x_{1},y_{1}){\mathscr{K}}_{F_{2}(L_{2})}(x_{2},y_{2})\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}\otimes g_{j}\Big)(y_{1},y_{2})d\mu(y_{1},y_{2}).

But, as we know
\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu)
is dense in
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
and by a limiting argument it follows that

\big[F_{1}(L_{1})\otimes F_{2}(L_{2})\big]f({\mathbf{x}})=\int_{X_{1}\times X_{2}}{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{1}(L_{1})}(x_{1},y_{1}){\mathscr{K}}_{F_{2}(L_{2})}(x_{2},y_{2})f({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

for all
f\in L^{2}(X,d\mu)
,
which implies (
4.30
).
Estimate (
4.31
) follows at once from (
4.30
) and (
4.28
).
∎

Assumption:

Henceforth we will assume that
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are the two
non-negative
self-adjoint operators
from our general setting described in Section
2.1
.
The fact that the operators
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are non-negative implies that all statements
in the two-parameter calculus presented above
are valid with
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
replaced by
\mathscr{B}([0,\infty)^{2})
.

Product of the semigroups
e^{-t_{1}L_{1}}\otimes e^{-t_{2}L_{2}}
.

The product

P_{1,t_{1}}\otimes P_{2,t_{2}}:=e^{-t_{1}L_{1}}\otimes e^{-t_{2}L_{2}},\quad t_{1},t_{2}\geq 0,

will play an important role in what follows.
In light of Proposition
4.9
the operator
e^{-t_{1}L_{1}}\otimes e^{-t_{2}L_{2}}
is an integral operator.
We will denote its kernel by
p_{\mathbf{t}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=p_{(t_{1},t_{2})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
.
Applying Proposition
4.9
we get

(4.32)

p_{(t_{1},t_{2})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=p_{1,t_{1}}(x_{1},y_{1})p_{2,t_{2}}(x_{2},y_{2}),\quad{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X.

Observe that the above theory works also for complex valued functions
F_{1},F_{2}

and, in particular,
e^{-z_{1}L_{1}}\otimes e^{-z_{2}L_{2}}
,
z_{1},z_{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}:=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:\operatorname{Re}z>0\}
, is an integral operator
with kernel
p_{(z_{1},z_{2})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
represented in the form

(4.33)

p_{(z_{1},z_{2})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=p_{1,z_{1}}(x_{1},y_{1})p_{2,z_{2}}(x_{2},y_{2}),\quad{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X,\;z_{1},z_{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Identity (
4.32
) leads to the following

Proposition 4.10
.

For any
{\bm{\sigma}}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})>{\bm{0}}
and
{\mathbf{t}}=(t_{1},t_{2})>{\bm{0}}
we have:

(a)
Gaussian upper bound:

(4.34)

|p_{(t_{1},t_{2})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c\frac{\prod_{i=1,2}\exp\big(-\frac{\rho_{i}^{2}(x_{i},y_{i})}{c_{i2}t_{i}}\big)}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},\sqrt{{\mathbf{t}}})V({\mathbf{y}},\sqrt{{\mathbf{t}}})\big]^{1/2}}\leq c{\mathfrak{D}}_{\sqrt{{\mathbf{t}}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}),\quad{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X.

(b)
Hölder continuity:
If
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X
with
\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\sqrt{t_{i}}
,
i=1,2
, then

\displaystyle|p_{(t_{1},t_{2})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-p_{(t_{1},t_{2})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|

(4.35)

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{i=1,2}\Big(\frac{\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})}{\sqrt{t_{i}}}\Big)^{\alpha_{i}}\frac{\prod_{j=1,2}\exp\big(-\frac{\rho_{j}^{2}(x_{j},y_{j})}{2c_{j2}t_{j}}\big)}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},\sqrt{{\mathbf{t}}})V({\mathbf{y}},\sqrt{{\mathbf{t}}})\big]^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{i=1,2}\Big(\frac{\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})}{\sqrt{t_{i}}}\Big)^{\alpha_{i}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{\sqrt{{\mathbf{t}}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

Above
\sqrt{{\mathbf{t}}}:=(\sqrt{t_{1}},\sqrt{t_{2}})
and

c_{12},c_{22}
are the structural constants from
(\ref{Gauss-local})
and
(
2.4
)

and the constant
c>0
depends only on the constants
c_{i0},c_{i1},c_{i2}

from
\eqref{eq:doubling-0},\eqref{Gauss-local},\eqref{lip}
.

Proof.

Inequality (
4.34
) follows readily from (
2.3
) and (
4.32
)
taking into account that for each
j=1,2
and
\sigma_{j}>0

(4.36)

\exp\Big(-\frac{c\rho_{j}^{2}(x_{j},y_{j})}{t_{j}}\Big)\leq c^{\prime}\biggl(1+\frac{\rho_{j}(x_{j},y_{j})}{\sqrt{t_{j}}}\biggr)^{-\sigma_{j}}.

We next establish estimate (
4.10
).
Assume that
\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\sqrt{t_{i}}
,
i=1,2
.
Then using (
2.4
) and (
2.10
) we obtain

\displaystyle|p_{(t_{1},t_{2})}

\displaystyle({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-p_{(t_{1},t_{2})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle=|p_{1,t_{1}}(x_{1},y_{1})p_{2,t_{2}}(x_{2},y_{2})-p_{1,t_{1}}(x_{1},y_{1}^{\prime})p_{2,t_{2}}(x_{2},y_{2}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq|p_{1,t_{1}}(x_{1},y_{1})-p_{1,t_{1}}(x_{1},y_{1}^{\prime})||p_{2,t_{2}}(x_{2},y_{2})|

\displaystyle\qquad\hskip 108.405pt+|p_{1,t_{1}}(x_{1},y_{1}^{\prime})||p_{2,t_{2}}(x_{2},y_{2})-p_{2,t_{2}}(x_{2},y_{2}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq c\Big(\frac{\rho_{1}(y_{1},y_{1}^{\prime})}{\sqrt{t_{1}}}\Big)^{\alpha_{1}}\frac{\exp\big(-\frac{\rho_{1}^{2}(x_{1},y_{1})}{c_{12}t_{1}}\big)}{\big[V_{1}(x_{1},\sqrt{t_{1}})V_{1}(y_{1},\sqrt{t_{1}})\big]^{1/2}}\frac{\exp\big(-\frac{\rho_{2}^{2}(x_{2},y_{2})}{c_{22}t_{2}}\big)}{\big[V_{2}(x_{2},\sqrt{t_{2}})V_{2}(y_{2},\sqrt{t_{2}})\big]^{1/2}}

\displaystyle+c\Big(\frac{\rho_{2}(y_{2},y_{2}^{\prime})}{\sqrt{t_{2}}}\Big)^{\alpha_{2}}\frac{\exp\big(-\frac{\rho_{2}^{2}(x_{2},y_{2})}{c_{22}t_{2}}\big)}{\big[V_{2}(x_{2},\sqrt{t_{2}})V_{2}(y_{2},\sqrt{t_{2}})\big]^{1/2}}\frac{\exp\big(-\frac{\rho_{1}^{2}(x_{1},y_{1}^{\prime})}{c_{12}t_{1}}\big)}{\big[V_{1}(x_{1},\sqrt{t_{1}})V_{1}(y_{1}^{\prime},\sqrt{t_{1}})\big]^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{i=1,2}\Big(\frac{\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})}{\sqrt{t_{i}}}\Big)^{\alpha_{i}}\frac{\prod_{j=1,2}\exp\big(-\frac{\rho_{j}^{2}(x_{j},y_{j})}{2c_{j2}t_{j}}\big)}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},\sqrt{{\mathbf{t}}})V({\mathbf{y}},\sqrt{{\mathbf{t}}})\big]^{1/2}}.

This coupled with (
4.36
) yields (
4.10
).
∎

4.6.
Integral operators

We will constantly work with integral operators.
We start with a basic statement on the boundedness on
L^{p}
of integral operators.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
H
be an integral operator with a measurable kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{H}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
, i.e.

Hf({\mathbf{x}})=\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{H}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})f({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

and assume that

{\mathscr{K}}_{H}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})={\mathscr{K}}_{H}({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{x}})
and for some
{\bm{\delta}}>{\bm{0}}
and
{\bm{\sigma}}>3{\mathbf{d}}/2

(4.37)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{H}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}),\quad{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X.

Then we have:

(i)

H
is a self-adjoint bounded operator on
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
.

(ii)
For
1\leq p\leq\infty

(4.38)

\|Hf\|_{p}\leq c_{p}\|f\|_{p},\quad\forall f\in L^{p}(X,d\mu).

Proof.

From (
4.37
) and (
3.14
) it follows that

|{\mathscr{K}}_{H}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

and hence using inequality (
3.19
) and that
{\bm{\sigma}}>3{\mathbf{d}}/2
we get

\int_{X}|{\mathscr{K}}_{H}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|d\mu({\mathbf{y}})=\int_{X}|{\mathscr{K}}_{H}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|d\mu({\mathbf{x}})\leq c<\infty.

Therefore, the operator
H
is bounded from
L^{1}
to
L^{1}
and from
L^{\infty}
to
L^{\infty}
.
Then (
4.38
) follows by interpolation.

Furthermore, since the operator
H
is symmetric and bounded on
L^{2}
it is self-adjoint.
∎

The following proposition will be instrumental in dealing with products of integral and non-integral operators.

Proposition 4.12
.

In the general setting of a doubling metric measure space
(X,\rho,\mu)
as above,
let
U,W:L^{2}(X,d\mu)\to L^{2}(X,d\mu)
be integral operators
with kernels
{\mathscr{K}}_{U}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
and
{\mathscr{K}}_{W}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
.
Assume that for some
{\bm{\delta}}=(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
,
\delta_{i}>0
,
and
{\bm{\sigma}}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
,
\sigma_{i}\geq d_{i}+1
, we have

(4.39)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{U}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c_{1}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\quad\hbox{and}\quad|{\mathscr{K}}_{W}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c_{1}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

Let
R:L^{2}(X,d\mu)\to L^{2}(X,d\mu)
be a bounded operator, not necessarily an integral operator.
Consider the operator

G:=V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{\frac{1}{2}}URWV(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad Gf({\mathbf{x}}):=V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{\frac{1}{2}}URW[V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{\frac{1}{2}}f(\cdot)]({\mathbf{x}}).

Then the operator
G:L^{1}(X,d\mu)\to L^{\infty}(X,d\mu)
is bounded and hence an integral operator
with kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{G}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
satisfying

\|{\mathscr{K}}_{G}\|_{\infty}=\|G\|_{1\to\infty}\leq c\|R\|_{2\to 2}
.

Consequently,
URW
is an integral operator
with kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{URW}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
of the form

(4.40)

{\mathscr{K}}_{URW}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=\frac{{\mathscr{K}}_{G}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}}

and hence

(4.41)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{URW}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq\frac{c\|R\|_{2\to 2}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}}\quad\hbox{for a.a. ${\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X$}.

More precisely, the action of the operator
URW
can be described as follows:
For any
f\in L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}d\mu)

(4.42)

\big\|V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}URWf(\cdot)\big\|_{\infty}\leq c\|f\|_{L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}d\mu)}.

The proof of this proposition relies on the following well-known result (
[
17
]
, Theorem 6, p. 503).

Proposition 4.13
.

An operator
T:L^{1}(X,d\mu)\to L^{\infty}(X,d\mu)
is bounded if and only if

T
is an integral operator with kernel
K\in L^{\infty}(X\times X)
, i.e.

Tf({\mathbf{x}})=\int_{X}K({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})f({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})
a.e. on
X
,

and if this is the case

\|T\|_{1\to\infty}=\|K\|_{L^{\infty}}
.
Moreover, the boundedness of
T
can be expressed in the bilinear form

|\langle Tf,g\rangle|\leq c\|f\|_{L^{1}}\|g\|_{L^{1}}
,
\forall f,g\in L^{1}
.

Proof of Proposition
4.12
.

Consider the operators

(4.43)

S:=V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}U\quad\hbox{and}\quad T:=WV(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}.

More precisely,

\displaystyle Sf({\mathbf{x}}):=V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}Uf({\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle=V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{U}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})f({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle=\int_{X}V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}{\mathscr{K}}_{U}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})f({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}}).

Hence,
S
is an integral operator with kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{S}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
given by

{\mathscr{K}}_{S}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}{\mathscr{K}}_{U}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

Observe that from (
4.39
), (
3.11
), and (
3.13
) it follows that

|{\mathscr{K}}_{S}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

We now use (
3.19
) and that
\sigma_{i}\geq d_{i}+1
to obtain

\displaystyle\|{\mathscr{K}}_{S}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1}\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},2{\bm{\sigma}}-{\mathbf{d}}}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\leq c.

In turn, using Cauchy-Schwarz, this implies that for any
f\in L^{2}(X,d\mu)

\|Sf\|_{\infty}\leq\Big\|\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{S}(\cdot,{\mathbf{y}})f({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\Big\|_{\infty}\leq\|{\mathscr{K}}_{S}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)\|_{2}\|f\|_{2}\leq c\|f\|_{2}

and hence

S:L^{2}(X,d\mu)\to L^{\infty}(X,d\mu)
is a bounded operator (
\|S\|_{2\to\infty}\leq c
).

We now focus on the operator
T
. We have

\displaystyle Tf({\mathbf{x}}):=W[V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}f]({\mathbf{x}})=\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{W}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}f({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

and hence,
T
is an integral operator with kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{T}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
given by

{\mathscr{K}}_{T}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})={\mathscr{K}}_{W}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}.

As above from (
4.39
), (
3.11
), and (
3.13
) it follows that

|{\mathscr{K}}_{T}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\leq cV({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

Using (
3.19
) and that
\sigma_{i}\geq d+1
we obtain

\displaystyle\|{\mathscr{K}}_{T}(\cdot,{\mathbf{y}})\|_{2}^{2}\leq c^{\prime}V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1}\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{x}})\leq c.

Therefore, using Minkowski’s inequality, for any
f\in L^{1}(X,d\mu)

\|Tf\|_{2}=\Big\|\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{T}(\cdot,{\mathbf{y}})f({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\Big\|_{2}\leq\int_{X}\|{\mathscr{K}}_{T}(\cdot,{\mathbf{y}})\|_{2}|f({\mathbf{y}})|d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\leq c\|f\|_{1},

implying that

T:L^{1}(X,d\mu)\to L^{2}(X,d\mu)
is a bounded operator (
\|T\|_{1\to 2}\leq c
).

Using the above and the assumption that
\|R\|_{2\to 2}<\infty
we get

\|G\|_{1\to\infty}=\|SRT\|_{1\to\infty}\leq\|S\|_{2\to\infty}\|R\|_{2\to 2}\|T\|_{1\to 2}\leq c\|R\|_{2\to 2}.

Applying Proposition
4.13
we conclude that
the operator
G
is an integral operator
with kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{G}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
satisfying

\|{\mathscr{K}}_{G}\|_{\infty}=\|G\|_{1\to\infty}\leq c\|R\|_{2\to 2}
.

For convenience we introduce the abbreviated notation

Q:=URW.

By definition

G=V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}URWV(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}=V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}QV(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}

and hence for any
f\in L^{1}(X,d\mu)

Gf({\mathbf{x}})=V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}Q[V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}f(\cdot)]({\mathbf{x}}),

implying

Q[V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}f(\cdot)]({\mathbf{x}})=V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}Gf({\mathbf{x}}).

Substituting
g({\mathbf{x}})=V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}f({\mathbf{x}})
we get

\displaystyle Qg({\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle=V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}G\big[V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}g(\cdot)\big]({\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle=V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{G}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}g({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}}).

Therefore, for any
g\in L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}d\mu)
we have

Qg({\mathbf{x}})=\int_{X}\frac{{\mathscr{K}}_{G}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}}g({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}}).

Thus
Q=URW
is an integral operator with kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{URW}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

satisfying (
4.40
) and (
4.41
).
The proof of Proposition
4.12
is complete.
∎

4.7.
Integral operators
F(\sqrt{L_{1}},\sqrt{L_{2}})
with localized kernels

Our next goal is to establish localization estimates for the kernels of operators

F(\sqrt{L_{1}},\sqrt{L_{2}})
generated by smooth functions
F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
.
We first consider the case of operators
F(\sqrt{L_{1}},\sqrt{L_{2}})
generated by
compactly supported functions
F
.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
F
be a bounded measurable function on
[0,\infty)^{2}
with the property

\operatorname{supp}F\subset[0,\tau_{1}]\times[0,\tau_{2}]
for some
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}>0
. Then the operator

F(\sqrt{L}):=F(\sqrt{L}_{1},\sqrt{L}_{2})

is an integral operator with kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{F(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

satisfying

(4.44)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq\frac{{c_{\flat}}\|F\|_{\infty}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})\big]^{1/2}},\quad\forall{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X,\quad{\bm{\tau}}=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

More specifically, the action of
F(\sqrt{L})
can be described as follows:
For any
f\in L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})^{-1/2}d\mu)
we have

(4.45)

\|V(\cdot,{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})^{1/2}F(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq c\|F||_{\infty}\|f\|_{L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})^{-1/2}d\mu)}

and, of course,
F(\sqrt{L})
is bounded from
L^{2}
to
L^{2}
.

Furthermore, for any
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\in X
with

\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})\leq\tau_{i}^{-1}
and
\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\tau_{i}^{-1}
,
i=1,2
,

(4.46)

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{K}}_{F(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{F(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|\leq{c_{\flat}}\sum_{i=1,2}\frac{\big[\big(\tau_{i}\rho_{i}(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})\big)^{\alpha_{i}}+\big(\tau_{i}\rho_{i}(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})\big)^{\alpha_{i}}\big]\|F\|_{\infty}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})\big]^{1/2}}.

The constant
{c_{\flat}}>0
above depends only on the constants
c_{i0},c_{i1},c_{i2}

from
(
2.1
)
,
(
2.3
)
,
(
2.4
)
.

Proof.

Given
{\mathbf{t}}=(t_{1},t_{2})
,
t_{1},t_{2}>0
, we have, using Theorem
4.7
,

F(\sqrt{L})=e^{-t_{1}^{2}L_{1}-t_{2}^{2}L_{2}}\big[e^{2t_{1}^{2}L_{1}+2t_{2}^{2}L_{2}}F(\sqrt{L})\big]e^{-t_{1}^{2}L_{1}-t_{2}^{2}L_{2}}.

We choose
t_{i}:=\tau_{i}^{-1}
,
i=1,2
.
Clearly,

\displaystyle\big\|e^{2t_{1}^{2}L_{1}+2t_{2}^{2}L_{2}}F(\sqrt{L})\big\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in[0,\tau_{1}]\times[0,\tau_{2}]}|e^{2t_{1}^{2}\lambda_{1}^{2}+2t_{2}^{2}\lambda_{2}^{2}}F(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\sqrt{\lambda_{2}})|

\displaystyle=e^{2t_{1}^{2}\tau_{1}^{2}+2t_{2}^{2}\tau_{2}^{2}}\|F\|_{\infty}\leq c\|F\|_{\infty}.

On the other hand, from (
4.34
)

|p_{{\mathbf{t}}^{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c({\bm{\sigma}}){\mathfrak{D}}_{{\mathbf{t}},{\bm{\sigma}}}(x,y)

for any
{\bm{\sigma}}>{\bm{0}}
.
We choose
{\bm{\sigma}}:=(3/2){\mathbf{d}}+{\bm{1}}
. Now, applying Proposition
4.12
(see (
4.42
)) we conclude that

F(\sqrt{L})

is an integral operator with kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{F(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

satisfying (
4.44
) and (
4.45
).

To prove (
4.46
) we set

G(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})e^{t_{1}^{2}\lambda_{1}^{2}+t_{2}^{2}\lambda_{2}^{2}}
.
Then we have the representation

F(\sqrt{L})=G(\sqrt{L})e^{-t_{1}^{2}L_{1}-t_{2}^{2}L_{2}}
.

Let
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\in X
with

\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})\leq\tau_{i}^{-1}
and
\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\tau_{i}^{-1}
,
i=1,2
.
We use estimate (
4.10
) with
\sigma_{i}=3d_{i}/2+1
and
t_{i}=\tau_{i}^{-1}
,
i=1,2
,
along with (
4.44
), applied to
G(\sqrt{L})
, to obtain

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{K}}_{F(\sqrt{L})}

\displaystyle({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{F(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{G(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}})\big[p_{{\mathbf{t}}^{2}}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{y}})-p_{{\mathbf{t}}^{2}}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})\big]d\mu({\mathbf{z}})\Big|

(4.47)

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{i=1,2}\Big(\frac{\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})}{t_{i}}\Big)^{\alpha_{i}}\frac{\|G\|_{\infty}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})\big]^{1/2}}\int_{X}\frac{{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\mathbf{t}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{y}})}{\big[V({\mathbf{z}},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})\big]^{1/2}}d\mu({\mathbf{z}})

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{i=1,2}\Big(\frac{\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})}{t_{i}}\Big)^{\alpha_{i}}\frac{\|F\|_{\infty}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})\big]^{1/2}}\int_{X}\frac{{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\mathbf{t}},{\bm{\sigma}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}^{*}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{y}})}{V({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{t}})}d\mu({\mathbf{z}})

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{i=1,2}(\tau_{i}\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime}))^{\alpha_{i}}\frac{\|F\|_{\infty}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})\big]^{1/2}},

where for the second inequality we used (
3.14
),
and for the last (
3.19
) along with the fact that
{\bm{\sigma}}>(3/2){\mathbf{d}}
. Because of the symmetry we also have from above

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{K}}_{F(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})-{\mathscr{K}}_{F(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{i=1,2}(\tau_{i}\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime}))^{\alpha_{i}}\frac{\|F\|_{\infty}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})V({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})\big]^{1/2}}

(4.48)

\displaystyle\leq c^{\prime}\sum_{i=1,2}(\tau_{i}\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime}))^{\alpha_{i}}\frac{\|F\|_{\infty}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})\big]^{1/2}}.

Here for the last inequality we used that

V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})\leq cV({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime},{\bm{\tau}}^{-1})

which follows by (
3.13
) and the assumption
\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\tau_{i}^{-1}
,
i=1,2
.
Estimate (
4.46
) follows readily from (
4.7
) and (
4.7
).
The proof of the proposition is complete.
∎

Decomposition of unity.

We will frequently use a decomposition of unity argument that is presented in the following

Lemma 4.15
.

(a)

There exist functions

\varphi_{0},\varphi\subset\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with the properties:

(4.49)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\hbox{$(i)$ $\;\varphi_{0},\varphi$ are real-valued and }

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=\varphi_{0}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\;\varphi(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=\varphi(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),

\displaystyle\hbox{$(ii)$ $\;\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{0}\subset[-2,2]^{2}$, $\varphi_{0}=1$ on $[-1,1]^{2}$,}

\displaystyle\hbox{$(iii)$ $\;\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset[-2,2]^{2}\setminus(-1,1)^{2}$, and}

\displaystyle\hbox{$(iv)$ $\;\varphi_{0}({\bm{\lambda}})+\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi(2^{-j}{\bm{\lambda}})=1$, ${\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$.}

(b)

Let
F
be a bounded Borel measurable function on
[0,\infty)^{2}
.
Denote
\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}}):=\varphi(2^{-j}{\bm{\lambda}})
,
j\geq 1
, where
\varphi
is from above,
and
F_{j}:=F\cdot\varphi_{j}
,
j\geq 0
.
Then

(4.50)

F(\sqrt{L})=\sum_{j\geq 0}F_{j}(\sqrt{L}),

where the convergence is in the strong
L^{2}
-sense.

Proof.

(a) Choose a function
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with the properties:
\phi
is real-valued and even,

\operatorname{supp}\phi\subset[-2,2]
,
0\leq\phi\leq 1
, and

\phi(\lambda)=1
for
\lambda\in[-1,1]
.
Then set
\varphi_{0}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=\phi(\lambda_{1})\phi(\lambda_{2})
.
Define
\varphi({\bm{\lambda}}):=\varphi_{0}({\bm{\lambda}})-\varphi_{0}(2{\bm{\lambda}})
.
Clearly, the constructed
\varphi_{0},\varphi
have the claimed properties.

(b)
We define

\Phi_{n}({\bm{\lambda}}):=\varphi_{0}({\bm{\lambda}})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\varphi(2^{-j}{\bm{\lambda}}),\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

From (
4.49
) it follows that
\operatorname{supp}\Phi_{n}\subset[-2^{n+1},2^{n+1}]^{2}
and

\Phi_{n}({\bm{\lambda}})=1
for
\lambda\in[-2^{n-1},2^{n-1}]^{2}

and hence

F(\sqrt{{\bm{\lambda}}})-\sum_{j=0}^{n}F_{j}(\sqrt{{\bm{\lambda}}})=F(\sqrt{{\bm{\lambda}}})(1-\Phi_{n}(\sqrt{{\bm{\lambda}}})),

{\bm{\lambda}}\geq{\bm{0}}
.
Therefore, for any
f\in L^{2}(X,d\mu)

\displaystyle\Big\|F(\sqrt{L})f-\sum_{j=0}^{n}F_{j}(\sqrt{L})f\Big\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|F(\sqrt{L})(1-\Phi_{n}(\sqrt{L}))f\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}\setminus[0,4^{n-1})^{2}}F(\sqrt{\lambda})(1-\Phi_{n}(\sqrt{\lambda}))dE_{\bm{\lambda}}f\Big\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}\setminus[0,4^{n-1})^{2}}|F(\sqrt{{\bm{\lambda}}})(1-\Phi_{n}(\sqrt{{\bm{\lambda}}}))|^{2}d\|E_{\bm{\lambda}}f\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|F\|_{\infty}^{2}\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}\setminus[0,4^{n-1})^{2}}d\|E_{\bm{\lambda}}f\|_{2}^{2}\to 0\quad\hbox{as}\;\;n\to\infty.

Above we used that
\Phi_{n}(\sqrt{{\bm{\lambda}}})=1
for
{\bm{\lambda}}\in[0,4^{n-1}]^{2}
.
∎

We next extend the result of Proposition
4.14
to the case when
F
in not compactly supported.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
F
be a Borel measurable real-valued function on
[0,\infty)^{2}
with the property

(4.51)

|F({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq c(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-m}\quad\hbox{ for some $m>d_{1}+d_{2}+\alpha_{1}\vee\alpha_{2}$.}

Then
F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})
is an integral operator whose kernel

{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
satisfies

(4.52)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{-1/2},\quad\forall{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X.

More specifically, the action of
F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})
can be described as follows:
For any function
f\in L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}d\mu)
we have

\|V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}F(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq c\|f\|_{L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}d\mu)}

and, of course,
F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})
is bounded from
L^{2}
to
L^{2}
.

Furthermore,
{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
is continuous on
X
.
More precisely,

(4.53)
