|{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|\leq c\frac{\sum_{i=1,2}\big[(\rho_{i}(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})/\delta_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}+(\rho_{i}(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})/\delta_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}\big]}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}},

if
\rho_{i}(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})\leq\delta_{i}
and
\rho(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})\leq\delta_{i}
,
i=1,2
.

Proof.

Choose functions

\varphi_{0},\varphi\subset\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with properties (
4.49
).
Set
\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}}):=\varphi(2^{-j}{\bm{\lambda}})
,
j\geq 1
.
Then

\sum_{j\geq 0}\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}})=1

for
{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

and hence

F({\bm{\delta}}{\bm{\lambda}})=\sum_{j\geq 0}F_{j}({\bm{\lambda}}),

where

F_{j}({\bm{\lambda}})=F({\bm{\delta}}{\bm{\lambda}})\varphi_{j}({\bm{\delta}}{\bm{\lambda}}).

Therefore,

(4.54)

F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})=\sum_{j\geq 0}F_{j}(\sqrt{L}),

where the convergence is strong in
L^{2}
, see Lemma
4.15
.
Clearly

\operatorname{supp}F_{0}\subset[-2/\delta_{1},2/\delta_{1}]\times[-2/\delta_{2},2/\delta_{2}],\quad\operatorname{supp}F_{j}\subset I_{j}\setminus J_{j},\quad j\geq 1,

where

\displaystyle I_{j}

\displaystyle:=[-2^{j+1}/\delta_{1},2^{j+1}/\delta_{1}]\times[-2^{j+1}/\delta_{2},2^{j+1}/\delta_{2}],

\displaystyle J_{j}

\displaystyle:=(-2^{j-1}/\delta_{1},2^{j-1}/\delta_{1})\times(-2^{j-1}/\delta_{2},2^{j-1}/\delta_{2}).

From above and the decay condition (
4.51
) on
F

it follows that
\|F_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq c2^{-jm}
.
We now apply Proposition
4.14
to
F_{j}
to conclude that

F_{j}(\sqrt{L})
is an integral operator with kernel satisfying

(4.55)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq\frac{c2^{-jm}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-j-1}{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},2^{-j-1}{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}}\leq\frac{c2^{-jm}2^{(d_{1}+d_{2})(j+1)}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}},

where we used that

V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})\leq c2^{(d_{1}+d_{2})(j+1)}V(\cdot,2^{-j-1}{\bm{\delta}})
in light of (
3.12
).

Denote

(4.56)

{\mathscr{K}}_{{\bm{\delta}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}):=\sum_{j\geq 0}{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

From (
4.55
) it follows that

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{K}}_{{\bm{\delta}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq\sum_{j\geq 0}|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}}\sum_{j\geq 0}2^{-j(m-d_{1}-d_{2})}

(4.57)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}},

where we used that
m>d_{1}+d_{2}
.

We next show that the kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{\bm{\delta}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
satisfies (
4.53
) and hence it is continuous.
To this end we estimate each

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|

assuming that

\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\delta_{i}
,
i=1,2
.
Two cases present themselves here:

Case 1:
Let
\rho_{1}(y_{1},y_{1}^{\prime})\leq\delta_{1}2^{-j-1}
and
\rho_{2}(y_{2},y_{2}^{\prime})\leq\delta_{2}2^{-j-1}
.
We apply (
4.46
) to
F_{j}(\sqrt{L})
,
j\geq 0
,
using that

\operatorname{supp}F_{j}\subset[-2^{j+1}/\delta_{1},2^{j+1}/\delta_{1}]\times[-2^{j+1}/\delta_{2},2^{j+1}/\delta_{2}]

and

\|F_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq c2^{-jm}

to obtain

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{i=1,2}\frac{\big(2^{j+1}\delta_{i}^{-1}\rho_{i}(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})\big)^{\alpha_{i}}\|F_{j}\|_{\infty}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-(j+1)}{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},2^{-(j+1)}{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}}

(4.58)

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{i=1,2}\frac{((\rho_{i}(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})/\delta_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}}2^{j(d_{1}+d_{2}+\alpha_{1}\vee\alpha_{2}-m)},

where we used (
3.12
).

Case 2:

\rho_{1}(y_{1},y_{1}^{\prime})>\delta_{1}2^{-j-1}
or
\rho_{2}(y_{2},y_{2}^{\prime})>\delta_{2}2^{-j-1}
.
Using (
4.55
) we obtain

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|+|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c2^{j(d_{1}+d_{2}-m)}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}}+\frac{c2^{j(d_{1}+d_{2}-m)}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{i=1,2}\frac{((\rho_{i}(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})/\delta_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}}2^{j(d_{1}+d_{2}+\alpha_{1}\vee\alpha_{2}-m)},

where we used that

V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\leq c2^{d_{1}+d_{2}}V({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime},{\bm{\delta}})

which follows from (
3.13
).
Therefore, estimate (
4.7
) is valid whenever
\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\delta_{i}
,
i=1,2
.

Summing up inequalities (
4.7
) we arrive at

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{K}}_{{\bm{\delta}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{{\bm{\delta}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\geq 0}|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{i=1,2}\frac{((\rho_{i}(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})/\delta_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}},

where we used the fact that
m>d_{1}+d_{2}+\alpha_{1}\vee\alpha_{2}
.
Because of the symmetry of
{\mathscr{K}}_{{\bm{\delta}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
the above implies the estimate

(4.59)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{{\bm{\delta}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{{\bm{\delta}}}({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|\leq c\sum_{i=1,2}\frac{((\rho_{i}(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})/\delta_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}+((\rho_{i}(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})/\delta_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}},

if
\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})\leq\delta_{i}
and
\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\delta_{i}
,
i=1,2
.

It remains to show that
{\mathscr{K}}_{{\bm{\delta}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
can be identified as the kernel of the operator
F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})
.
Denote by
L^{2}_{0}(X)
the set of all compactly supported functions in
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
.
It is straightforward to show that

L^{2}_{0}(X)
is dense in
L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}d\mu)
.

Let
H_{\bm{\delta}}
be the operator with kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{\bm{\delta}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
, that is,

H_{\bm{\delta}}f({\mathbf{x}}):=\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{\bm{\delta}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})f({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}}).

From (
4.7
) it readily follow that for any
L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}d\mu)
we have

(4.60)

\|V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2}H_{\bm{\delta}}f(\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq c\|f\|_{L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}d\mu)}.

Let
f\in L^{2}_{0}(X)
. Then from (
4.54
) it follows that

F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f=\sum_{j\geq 0}F_{j}(\sqrt{L})f\quad\hbox{in}\quad L^{2}.

On the other hand, since
f\in L^{2}_{0}(X)\subset L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}d\mu)

it follows from (
4.7
) and (
4.59
) that

\sum_{j\geq 0}F_{j}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}})=\sum_{j\geq 0}\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})f(y)d\mu({\mathbf{y}})=\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{\bm{\delta}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})f({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}}),

where the series converges point-wise for every
{\mathbf{x}}\in X
to a continuous function on
X
.
Therefore,

F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}})=\sum_{j\geq 0}F_{j}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}})=\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{\bm{\delta}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})f({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})=H_{\bm{\delta}}f({\mathbf{x}}),\;\;\forall f\in L^{2}_{0}(X).

Using that

L^{2}_{0}(X)
is dense in
L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}d\mu)

it follows that
H_{\bm{\delta}}
is the unique extension of the operator
F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})
from
L^{2}_{0}(X)
to
L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}d\mu)
.
We define

F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}}):=\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{\bm{\delta}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})f(y)d\mu({\mathbf{y}})=H_{\bm{\delta}}f({\mathbf{x}}),\quad\forall f\in L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}d\mu).

Thus
{\mathscr{K}}_{\bm{\delta}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
is the kernel of the operator
F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})
.

Finally, note that (
4.52
) follows from (
4.7
) and (
4.53
) from (
4.59
).
∎

The Fourier transform of an integrable function
F:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is standardly defined by

\widehat{F}({\bm{\xi}}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F({\mathbf{x}})e^{-i{\mathbf{x}}\cdot{\bm{\xi}}}dx,\quad{\bm{\xi}}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}),{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2}).

The finite speed propagation property of the operators
L_{1},L_{2}
(see (
2.19
))
leads to the following localization result for
the kernels of operators of the form
F(t\sqrt{L})
whenever
F
is band-limited.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
F\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\widehat{F}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and

|F({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq c(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-m}
,
{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, for some
m\geq d_{1}+d_{2}+1
.

(a)

If
F(-\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
and

\operatorname{supp}\widehat{F}\subset[-A,A]\times\mathbb{R}
for some
A>0
,
then for any
{\mathbf{t}}=(t_{1},t_{2})>{\bm{0}}
, and
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X
, we have

(4.61)

{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=0\quad\hbox{if}\quad\rho_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})>{\tilde{c}}_{1}t_{1}A.

(b)

If
F(\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{2})=F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
and

\operatorname{supp}\widehat{F}\subset\mathbb{R}\times[-A,A]
for some
A>0
,
then for any
{\mathbf{t}}=(t_{1},t_{2})>{\bm{0}}
, and
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X
,

(4.62)

{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=0\quad\hbox{if}\quad\rho_{2}(x_{2},y_{2})>{\tilde{c}}_{2}t_{2}A.

Above the constants
{\tilde{c}}_{1}
,
{\tilde{c}}_{2}
are from (
2.19
).
Recall that
F({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L}):=F(t_{1}\sqrt{L_{1}},t_{2}\sqrt{L_{2}})
.

Proof.

We will prove only part (a) of this proposition. The proof of part (b) is the same (symmetric).

From the Fourier inversion formula, using that
F,\widehat{F}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we have

(4.63)

F(t_{1}\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},t_{2}\sqrt{\lambda_{2}})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{F}({\bm{\xi}})e^{i(t_{1}\xi_{1}\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}+t_{2}\xi_{2}\sqrt{\lambda_{2}})}d{\bm{\xi}},\quad d{\bm{\xi}}=d\xi_{1}d\xi_{2}.

Assume that
\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2},\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}\in L^{2}(X)\cap L^{1}(X)
.
Then using (
4.63
) we get

\displaystyle\big\langle F({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2},\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}F(t_{1}\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},t_{2}\sqrt{\lambda_{2}})d\langle E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2},\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{F}({\bm{\xi}})e^{i(t_{1}\xi_{1}\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}+t_{2}\xi_{2}\sqrt{\lambda_{2}})}d{\bm{\xi}}\Big)d\langle E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2},\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{[0,A]\times\mathbb{R}}\widehat{F}({\bm{\xi}})\cos(t_{1}\xi_{1}\sqrt{\lambda_{1}})e^{it_{2}\xi_{2}\sqrt{\lambda_{2}}}d{\bm{\xi}}\Big)d\langle E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2},\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{[0,A]\times\mathbb{R}}\widehat{F}({\bm{\xi}})\Big(\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}\cos(t_{1}\xi_{1}\sqrt{\lambda_{1}})e^{it_{2}\xi_{2}\sqrt{\lambda_{2}}}d\langle E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2},\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}\rangle\Big)d{\bm{\xi}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{[0,A]\times\mathbb{R}}\widehat{F}({\bm{\xi}})\big\langle\cos(t_{1}\xi_{1}\sqrt{L_{1}})\varphi_{1}\otimes e^{it_{2}\xi_{2}\sqrt{L_{2}}}\varphi_{2},\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}\big\rangle d{\bm{\xi}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{[0,A]\times\mathbb{R}}\widehat{F}({\bm{\xi}})\big\langle\cos(t_{1}\xi_{1}\sqrt{L_{1}})\varphi_{1},\psi_{1}\big\rangle\big\langle e^{it_{2}\xi_{2}\sqrt{L_{2}}}\varphi_{2},\psi_{2}\big\rangle d{\bm{\xi}},

where we used that
\widehat{F}(-\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\widehat{F}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
,
which follows from the assumption
F(-\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
.
To justify the above change of the order of integration we use Fubini’s theorem and the fact that

\displaystyle\int_{[0,A]\times\mathbb{R}}|\widehat{F}({\bm{\xi}})|\biggl(\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}|\cos(t_{1}\xi_{1}\sqrt{\lambda_{1}})e^{it_{2}\xi_{2}\sqrt{\lambda_{2}}}||d\langle E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2},\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}\rangle|\biggr)d{\bm{\xi}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\widehat{F}({\bm{\xi}})|\biggl(\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}|d\langle E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2},\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}\rangle|\biggr)d{\bm{\xi}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\widehat{F}({\bm{\xi}})|d{\bm{\xi}}\biggl(\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}d||E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}||^{2}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\biggl(\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}d||E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}||^{2}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle=\|\widehat{F}\|_{L^{1}}\|\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}\|_{L^{2}}\|\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}|\|_{L^{2}}<\infty.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\big\langle F({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2},\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}\big\rangle

(4.64)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{[0,A]\times\mathbb{R}}\widehat{F}({\bm{\xi}})\big\langle\cos(t_{1}\xi_{1}\sqrt{L_{1}})\varphi_{1},\psi_{1}\big\rangle\big\langle e^{it_{2}\xi_{2}\sqrt{L_{2}}}\varphi_{2},\psi_{2}\big\rangle d{\bm{\xi}}.

For
i=1,2,
we let

\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon}=\mu_{i}(B(y_{i},\epsilon))^{-1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{B(y_{i},\epsilon)}

and

\psi_{i}^{\epsilon}=\mu_{i}(B(x_{i},\epsilon))^{-1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{B(x_{i},\epsilon)}

be defined on
X_{i}
, where
\epsilon>0
is such that

\rho_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})-2\epsilon>{\tilde{c}}_{1}At_{1}
.
Recall that
{\mathbbm{1}}_{E}
stands for the characteristic function of the set
E
.
Then applying the finite speed propagation formula (
2.19
) we get

\big\langle\cos(t_{1}\xi_{1}\sqrt{L_{1}})\varphi_{1}^{\epsilon},\psi_{1}^{\epsilon}\big\rangle=0,\quad\xi_{1}\in[0,A].

Using this in (
4.7
) it follows that

\displaystyle\big\langle F({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})\varphi^{\epsilon}_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}^{\epsilon},\psi_{1}^{\epsilon}\otimes\psi_{2}^{\epsilon}\big\rangle=0.

Finally, taking into account that the kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{F(t_{1}\sqrt{L_{1}},t_{2}\sqrt{L_{2}})}

is continuous on
X
, using Proposition
4.16
,
we conclude that for all
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X
with
\rho_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})>{\tilde{c}}_{1}t_{1}A
we have

{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\langle F({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})\varphi_{1}^{\epsilon}\otimes\varphi_{2}^{\epsilon},\psi_{1}^{\epsilon}\otimes\psi_{2}^{\epsilon}\big\rangle=0,

which completes the proof.
∎

We now come to our principle localization estimate for the kernels of
operators of the form

F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L}):=F(\delta_{1}\sqrt{L_{1}},\delta_{2}\sqrt{L_{2}})
,
where
F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
is smooth and satisfies the condition

F(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
,
i.e.
F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
is even in
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
.
For example,

F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=f(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})
with
f
a univariate function
or

F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=f_{1}(\lambda_{1})f_{2}(\lambda_{2})
with
f_{1},f_{2}
even
are such functions.

Theorem 4.18
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{C}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
k_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
k_{i}>3d_{i}/2
,
i=1,2
,

k_{1}+k_{2}\geq 4
, be real-valued,

\operatorname{supp}F\subset[-R,R]^{2}
for some
R\geq 1

and

F(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

for all
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in[-R,R]^{2}
.
Then
F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L}):=F(\delta_{1}\sqrt{L_{1}},\delta_{2}\sqrt{L_{2}}),{\bm{\delta}}=(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})>{\bm{0}}
,
is an integral operator bounded from
L^{p}(X,d\mu)
to
L^{p}(X,d\mu)
,
1\leq p\leq\infty
,
with kernel

{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

satisfying

(4.65)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c_{\mathbf{k}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\quad\text{and}

(4.66)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|\leq c_{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1,2}\Big(\frac{\rho_{i}(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})}{\delta_{i}}\Big)^{\alpha_{i}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

if
\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\delta_{i}
,
i=1,2
, where
{\mathbf{k}}=(k_{1},k_{2})
.
Moreover,

c_{\mathbf{k}}=cR^{d_{1}+d_{2}}\big(\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}+R^{k_{1}+k_{2}}\|F\|_{W^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{\infty}}\big)

and
c_{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}}=R^{\alpha_{1}\vee\alpha_{2}}c_{\mathbf{k}}
,
where
c
depends on the constants
c_{i0},c_{i1},c_{i2}
from
(\ref{eq:doubling-0})-(\ref{lip})
and
{\mathbf{k}}
.
In addition,

(4.67)

\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})=F({\bm{0}}),\quad\forall{\mathbf{x}}\in X.

For the proof of this theorem we need some preparation.
We will apply some well known approximation techniques using polynomial reproducing kernel operators.
We will use the standard notation
\Phi_{h}(\cdot):=h^{-2}\Phi(h^{-1}\cdot)
,
h>0
,
for any function
\Phi
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
\phi\in S(\mathbb{R})
be an even function such that

\operatorname{supp}\widehat{\phi}\subset[-1,1]
,
\widehat{\phi}(0)=1
and

\widehat{\phi}^{(k)}(0)=0
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
If
\Phi(x_{1},x_{2})=\phi(x_{1})\phi(x_{2}),(x_{1},x_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
then for any
{\bm{\beta}}=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}

(4.68)

\Phi_{h}*{\mathbf{x}}^{\bm{\beta}}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}){\mathbf{y}}^{\bm{\beta}}d{\mathbf{y}}={\mathbf{x}}^{\bm{\beta}},\quad h>0.

Furthermore, for any
{\bm{\beta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}

(4.69)

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{y}}){\mathbf{y}}^{\bm{\beta}}d{\mathbf{y}}=0.

Recall that
{\mathbf{x}}^{\bm{\beta}}:=x_{1}^{\beta_{1}}x_{2}^{\beta_{2}}
.

Proof.

From standard properties of the Fourier transform we get

(4.70)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(y)y^{k}\,dy=i^{k}\widehat{\phi}^{(k)}(0)=i^{k}\delta_{k0},\quad\forall k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}.

From this it follows that for any
h>0
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
we have

\displaystyle h^{-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\phi\big(\frac{x-y}{h}\big)y^{k}dy

\displaystyle=h^{k}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\phi(y)(x/h-y)^{k}\,dy

\displaystyle=h^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{k}\binom{k}{m}(x/h)^{k-m}(-1)^{m}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\phi(y)y^{m}dy=x^{k}.

Now, it easily follows that for any
h>0
and
{\bm{\beta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}){\mathbf{y}}^{\bm{\beta}}d{\mathbf{y}}=h^{-2}\prod_{i=1,2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\phi\big(\frac{x_{i}-y_{i}}{h}\big)y_{i}^{\beta_{i}}dy_{i}=x_{1}^{\beta_{1}}x_{2}^{\beta_{2}},

which confirms (
4.68
).
Identity (
4.69
) follows at once from (
4.70
).
∎

The next approximation-type lemma will be instrumental in proving our basic Theorem
4.18
.

Lemma 4.20
.

Assume
F\in\mathcal{C}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
,
k_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
i=1,2
,
\operatorname{supp}F\subset[-1,1]^{2}
,
and let
\Phi
be the function from Lemma
4.19
.
Then for any
h>0
we have

(4.71)

\|F-F*\Phi_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq ch^{k_{1}+k_{2}}\|F\|_{W^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{\infty}}.

Furthermore, if
|{\mathbf{x}}|>3
, then

(4.72)

|F({\mathbf{x}})-F*\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}})|=|F*\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}})|\leq c\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}h^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(1+|{\mathbf{x}}|)^{-k_{1}-k_{2}}.

Proof.

By Taylor’s formula we have, for
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

(4.73)

F({\mathbf{y}})=P_{k_{1}+k_{2},{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathbf{y}})+R_{k_{1}+k_{2},{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathbf{y}}),

where

P_{k_{1}+k_{2},{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathbf{y}})=\sum_{|{\bm{\beta}}|<k_{1}+k_{2}}\frac{\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}F({\mathbf{x}})}{{\bm{\beta}}!}({\mathbf{y}}-{\mathbf{x}})^{{\bm{\beta}}}

and

(4.74)

R_{k_{1}+k_{2},{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathbf{y}})=\sum_{|{\bm{\beta}}|=k_{1}+k_{2}}\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{{\bm{\beta}}!}({\mathbf{y}}-{\mathbf{x}})^{{\bm{\beta}}}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)^{k_{1}+k_{2}-1}\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}F({\mathbf{x}}+t({\mathbf{y}}-{\mathbf{x}}))dt.

Clearly, (
4.69
) implies that if
|{\bm{\beta}}|>0
, then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}({\mathbf{y}}-{\mathbf{x}})^{\bm{\beta}}\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}})d{\mathbf{y}}=0.

Using this and (
4.73
) we obtain for
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

\displaystyle|F({\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle-F\ast\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}})|=\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}[F({\mathbf{x}})-F({\mathbf{y}})]\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}})d{\mathbf{y}}\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}[F({\mathbf{x}})-P_{k_{1}+k_{2},{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathbf{y}})]\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}})d{\mathbf{y}}-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}R_{k_{1}+k_{2},{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathbf{y}})\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}})d{\mathbf{y}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}|R_{k_{1}+k_{2},{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathbf{y}})\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}})|d{\mathbf{y}}.

We now use (
4.74
) to obtain

\displaystyle|F({\mathbf{x}})-F\ast\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}})|

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{1}+k_{2}}\|F\|_{W^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\Bigl(\frac{|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|}{h}\Bigr)^{k_{1}+k_{2}}|\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}})|d{\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle=ch^{k_{1}+k_{2}}\|F\|_{W^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|{\mathbf{y}}|^{k_{1}+k_{2}}|\Phi({\mathbf{y}})|d{\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{1}+k_{2}}\|F\|_{W^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

where for the last inequality we used that
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
This confirms (
4.71
).

We next prove estimate (
4.72
).
Assume
|{\mathbf{x}}|>3
.
Because
\operatorname{supp}F\subset[-1,1]^{2}

we have
|F({\mathbf{x}})-F*\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}})|=|F*\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}})|
.
Choose
K:=k_{1}+k_{2}+2
.
From the assumption
\Phi\in S({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
it follows that

|\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}})|\leq ch^{-2}\big(1+|{\mathbf{x}}|/h\big)^{-K}
with
c>0
depending on
K
.
Using this we get

(4.75)

\displaystyle|F({\mathbf{x}})-F*\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}})|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{[-1,1]^{2}}|F({\mathbf{y}})||\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}})|d{\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle\leq\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{[-1,1]^{2}}|\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}})|d{\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle\leq c\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}h^{-2}\int_{|{\mathbf{y}}|\leq\sqrt{2}}\Bigl(1+\frac{|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|}{h}\Bigr)^{-K}d{\mathbf{y}}.

From
|{\mathbf{y}}|\leq\sqrt{2}
it follows that

|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|\geq|{\mathbf{x}}|-|{\mathbf{y}}|\geq|{\mathbf{x}}|-\sqrt{2}
.
We use this and the fact that
|{\mathbf{x}}|\geq 3
to obtain

\displaystyle h^{-2}\int_{|{\mathbf{y}}|\leq\sqrt{2}}\Bigl(1+\frac{|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|}{h}\Bigr)^{-K}d{\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|{\mathbf{u}}|\geq\frac{|{\mathbf{x}}|-\sqrt{2}}{h}}(1+|{\mathbf{u}}|)^{-K}d{\mathbf{u}}

\displaystyle\leq c\Bigl(\frac{|{\mathbf{x}}|-\sqrt{2}}{h}\Bigr)^{-K+2}\leq ch^{K-2}(1+|{\mathbf{x}}|)^{-K+2},

where we applied the substitution
{\mathbf{u}}=({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}})/h
.
Finally, combining this with (
4.75
) and using that
K=k_{1}+k_{2}+2
we obtain

|F({\mathbf{x}})-F\ast\Phi_{h}({\mathbf{x}})|\leq c\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}h^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(1+|{\mathbf{x}}|)^{-k_{1}-k_{2}}.

The proof of the lemma is complete.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.18
.

We first show that it suffices to prove the theorem in the case
R=1
.
Indeed, assume that the theorem holds for
R=1
and
let
F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
satisfy its assumptions for some
R>1
.
We set

G(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=F(R\lambda_{1},R\lambda_{2})
and note that
\operatorname{supp}G\subset[-1,1]^{2}
.
Applying the result to
G
we get

(4.76)

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|

\displaystyle=|{\mathscr{K}}_{G(R^{-1}{\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c_{k}(G){\mathfrak{D}}_{R^{-1}{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c\Big(\|G\|_{\infty}+\sum_{|{\bm{\beta}}|=k_{1}+k_{2}}\|\partial^{\bm{\beta}}G\|_{\infty}\Big)R^{d_{1}+d_{2}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c\big(\|F\|_{\infty}+R^{k_{1}+k_{2}}\|F\|_{W^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{\infty}}\big)R^{d_{1}+d_{2}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

Similarly, if
\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\delta_{i}/R
,
i=1,2
, then

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle=|{\mathscr{K}}_{G(R^{-1}{\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{G(R^{-1}{\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq c_{\mathbf{k}}(G)\sum_{i=1,2}\Big(\frac{\rho_{i}(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})}{\delta_{i}/R}\Big)^{\alpha_{i}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{R^{-1}{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c_{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\sum_{i=1,2}\Big(\frac{\rho_{i}(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})}{\delta_{i}}\Big)^{\alpha_{i}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

If
\delta_{i}/R\leq\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\delta_{i}
, then (
4.66
) follows directly from (
4.76
).

We next prove the theorem in the case
R=1
.
Applying Proposition
4.14
with
F({\bm{\lambda}})
replaced by
F({\bm{\delta}}{\bm{\lambda}})
,

\operatorname{supp}F({\bm{\delta}}{\bm{\lambda}})\subset[-1/\delta_{1},1/\delta_{2}]\times[-1/\delta_{2},1/\delta_{2}]
,
we conclude that
F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})
is an integral operator whose kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
is continuous and

(4.77)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq\frac{c\|F\|_{\infty}}{[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})]^{1/2}}.

Our goal is to show that
{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
satisfies (
4.65
) and (
4.66
).

Assume
0<h\leq 2({\tilde{c}}_{1}\vee{\tilde{c}}_{2})
with the constants
{\tilde{c}}_{1},{\tilde{c}}_{2}
from (
2.19
).
Let
\Phi:=\phi\otimes\phi
be the function from Lemmas
4.19
,
4.20
.
Choose
\varphi_{0},\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with the properties from (
4.49
).
Set

\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}}):=\varphi(2^{-j}{\bm{\lambda}})
,
j\geq 1
.
Then

\sum_{j\geq 0}\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}})=1
for
{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and hence

F({\bm{\delta}}{\bm{\lambda}})-F*\Phi_{h}({\bm{\delta}}{\bm{\lambda}})=\sum_{j\geq 0}[F({\bm{\delta}}{\bm{\lambda}})-F*\Phi_{h}({\bm{\delta}}{\bm{\lambda}})]\varphi_{j}({\bm{\delta}}{\bm{\lambda}}),\quad{\bm{\delta}}>{\bm{0}}.

Set
F_{j}({\bm{\lambda}}):=[F({\bm{\lambda}})-F*\Phi_{h}({\bm{\lambda}})]\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}})
.
From above it follows that

(4.78)

F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})-F*\Phi_{h}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})=\sum_{j\geq 0}F_{j}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L}),

where the convergence is in the strong sense in
L^{2}
, see Lemma
4.15
.

From conditions (
4.49
),(i)–(ii) on
\operatorname{supp}\phi_{0}
and
\operatorname{supp}\phi
it follows that

\operatorname{supp}F_{0}({\bm{\delta}}\cdot\cdot)\cap[0,\infty)^{2}\subset[0,2\delta_{1}^{-1}]\times[0,2\delta_{2}^{-1}]\quad\hbox{and}\quad

\operatorname{supp}F_{j}({\bm{\delta}}\cdot\cdot)\cap[0,\infty)^{2}\subset[0,2^{j+1}\delta_{1}^{-1}]\times[0,2^{j+1}\delta_{2}^{-1}].

We now apply Proposition
4.14
to conclude that

(4.79)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})|\leq\frac{c\|F_{j}\|_{\infty}}{\big[V({\mathbf{u}},2^{-j-1}{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{v}},2^{-j-1}{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}}\leq\frac{c2^{j(d_{1}+d_{2})}\|F_{j}\|_{\infty}}{\big[V({\mathbf{u}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{v}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}},

where for the last inequality we used (
3.12
).

We next estimate
\|F_{j}\|_{\infty}
.
For
j=0,1,2
, using (
4.71
), we get

(4.80)

\|F_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\phi_{j}\|_{\infty}\|F-F*\Phi_{h}\|_{\infty}\leq ch^{k_{1}+k_{2}}\|F\|_{W^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{\infty}}.

Further, for
j\geq 3
we use (
4.72
) to obtain

(4.81)

\displaystyle\|F_{j}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi_{j}\|_{\infty}\|F-F*\Phi_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(B(0,2^{j+1})\setminus B(0,2^{j-1}))}

\displaystyle\leq c\|F\|_{\infty}h^{k_{1}+k_{2}}2^{-j(k_{1}+k_{2})}.

Combining (
4.79
)–(
4.81
) and using that
k_{1}+k_{2}>d_{1}+d_{2}
we obtain

(4.82)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})|\leq\frac{c(\|F_{j}\|_{\infty}+\|F\|_{W^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{\infty}})h^{k_{1}+k_{2}}2^{j(d_{1}+d_{2}-k_{1}-k_{2})}}{\big[V({\mathbf{u}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{v}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}}.

Denote

{\mathscr{K}}_{h,{\bm{\delta}}}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}):=\sum_{j\geq 0}{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}).

Summing up estimates (
4.82
) taking into account that
k_{1}+k_{2}>d_{1}+d_{2}
we obtain

(4.83)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{h,{\bm{\delta}}}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})|\leq\frac{c^{\star}(\|F_{j}\|_{\infty}+\|F\|_{W^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{\infty}})h^{k_{1}+k_{2}}}{\big[V({\mathbf{u}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{v}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}},\quad\forall{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}\in X,\;0<h\leq 2({\tilde{c}}_{1}\vee{\tilde{c}}_{2}),

where the constant
c^{\star}>0
is independent of
h
.

Since
F
is compactly supported and
\Phi\in S({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
,
then
F*\Phi_{h}\in S({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
.
Moreover,
\widehat{F*\Phi_{h}}
is even with respect to each variable because
\Phi_{h}
and
F
are even.
In addition,

\widehat{F\ast\Phi_{h}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=(\widehat{F}\widehat{\Phi_{h}})(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\widehat{F}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\widehat{\phi}(h\xi_{1})\widehat{\phi}(h\xi_{2})

which implies that

\operatorname{supp}\widehat{F*\Phi_{h}}\subset[-1/h,1/h]^{2}
.

From Proposition
4.16
it follows that the operator
F*\Phi_{h}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})
is an integral operator
whose kernel is continuous and

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F*\Phi_{h}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})|\leq\frac{c}{[V({\mathbf{u}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{v}},{\bm{\delta}})]^{1/2}}.

Let
f\in L^{2}_{0}(X)
. Recall that
L^{2}_{0}(X)
is the set of all compactly supported
L^{2}
functions.
From (
4.78
) it follows that

F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f-F*\Phi_{h}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f=\sum_{j\geq 0}F_{j}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f\quad\hbox{in}\;\;L^{2}.

On the other hand, since
L^{2}_{0}(X)\subset L^{1}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{-1/2}d\mu)
it follows from (
4.83
) that

\sum_{j\geq 0}F_{j}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{u}})=\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{h,{\bm{\delta}}}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})f({\mathbf{v}})d\mu({\mathbf{v}}),\quad\forall{\mathbf{u}}\in X.

Therefore,

F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{u}})-F*\Phi_{h}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{u}})=\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{h,{\bm{\delta}}}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})f({\mathbf{v}})d\mu({\mathbf{v}}),\quad\forall{\mathbf{u}}\in X,

and hence for each
f\in L^{2}_{0}(X)

\displaystyle\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})f({\mathbf{v}})d\mu({\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle-\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{F*\Phi_{h}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})f({\mathbf{v}})d\mu({\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle=\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{h,{\bm{\delta}}}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})f({\mathbf{v}})d\mu({\mathbf{v}}),\quad\forall{\mathbf{u}}\in X.

Using the fact that the kernels from above are continuous it follows from this identity that

(4.84)

{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})={\mathscr{K}}_{F*\Phi_{h}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})+{\mathscr{K}}_{h,{\bm{\delta}}}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}),\;\;\forall{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}\in X,0<h\leq 2({\tilde{c}}_{1}\vee{\tilde{c}}_{2}).

To prove (
4.65
) we will make use of Proposition
4.17
.
Let
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X
.
Several cases naturally occur here:

Case 1:

\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\geq\delta_{i}
,
i=1,2
.

Case 2:

\rho_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})\geq\delta_{1}
and
\rho_{2}(x_{2},y_{2})<\delta_{2}
.

Case 3:

\rho_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})<\delta_{1}
and
\rho_{2}(x_{2},y_{2})\geq\delta_{2}
.

Case 4:

\rho_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})<\delta_{1}
and
\rho_{2}(x_{2},y_{2})<\delta_{2}
.

We will consider in detail only Case 2. Then we choose
h>0
so that

(4.85)

\frac{\rho_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})}{2\delta_{1}}\leq\frac{{\tilde{c}}_{1}}{h}<\frac{\rho_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})}{\delta_{1}}.

Hence
0<h\leq 2{\tilde{c}}_{1}
.
As already alluded to above
F*\Phi_{h}\in S({\mathbb{R}}^{2})

and

\operatorname{supp}\widehat{F*\Phi_{h}}\subset[-1/h,1/h]^{2}
.
Therefore, in light of (
4.85
) the function

F*\Phi_{h}
satisfies the assumptions
of Proposition
4.17
(a) with
A=1/h

and hence

{\mathscr{K}}_{F*\Phi_{h}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=0.

From this, (
4.83
) with
{\mathbf{u}}={\mathbf{x}}
,
{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{y}}
, (
4.84
), (
4.85
) and the fact that
\rho_{2}(x_{2},y_{2})<\delta_{2}

it follows that

(4.86)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq\frac{c(\|F_{j}\|_{\infty}+\|F\|_{W^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{\infty}})}{\big[V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{\delta}})\big]^{1/2}\big(1+\frac{\rho_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})}{\delta_{1}}\big)^{k_{1}}\big(1+\frac{\rho_{2}(x_{2},y_{2})}{\delta_{2}}\big)^{k_{2}}},

which confirms estimate (
4.65
).
The proof of estimate (
4.65
) in Case 3 is symmetric and will be omitted.
In Case 1 we may assume that
\rho_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})/\delta_{1}\geq\rho_{2}(x_{2},y_{2})/\delta_{2}
.
Then we proceed similarly as above using that

h^{k_{1}+k_{2}}\leq c\Big(1+\frac{\rho_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})}{\delta_{1}}\Big)^{-k_{1}}\Big(1+\frac{\rho_{2}(x_{2},y_{2})}{\delta_{2}}\Big)^{-k_{2}}

to obtain (
4.86
). We omit the further details.
In Case 4 estimate (
4.65
) follows by Proposition
4.14
.

So far we have shown that
F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})
is a bounded integral operator from
L^{2}_{0}(X)
to
L^{\infty}(X,V(\cdot,{\bm{\delta}})^{1/2})

with kernel
F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
satisfying (
4.65
).
Identifying
F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})
in general with the operator with this kernel we conclude, using Proposition
4.11
and the fact that
{\mathbf{k}}>3{\mathbf{d}}/2
,
that
F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})
is a bounded linear operator from
L^{p}(X,d\mu)
to
L^{p}(X,d\mu)
,
1\leq p\leq\infty
.

To prove (
4.66
) we proceed similarly as in the proof of estimate (
4.46
).
Define

G(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})e^{\delta_{1}^{2}\lambda_{1}^{2}+\delta_{2}^{2}\lambda_{2}^{2}}.

Then

F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})=G({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})e^{-\delta_{1}^{2}L_{1}-\delta_{2}^{2}L_{2}}
.
Using (
4.65
) and (
4.10
) we obtain

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}

\displaystyle({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{G({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}})\big(p_{(\delta_{1}^{2},\delta_{2}^{2})}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{y}})-p_{(\delta_{1}^{2},\delta_{2}^{2})}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})\big)d{\mathbf{z}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq cc_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{i=1,2}\big(\frac{\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})}{\delta_{i}}\big)^{\alpha_{i}}\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}}){\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{z}})

\displaystyle\leq cc_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{i=1,2}\Big(\frac{\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})}{\delta_{i}}\Big)^{\alpha_{i}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}),

where for the last inequality we used (
3.21
).
Thus estimate (
4.66
) is established.

To prove (
4.67
) we introduce the function

H(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=e^{|\lambda_{1}|+|\lambda_{2}|}F(\sqrt{|\lambda_{1}|},\sqrt{|\lambda_{2}|})
.
Then

F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=H(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2})e^{-\lambda_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{2}^{2}}

and hence

F({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})=H({\mathbf{t}}^{2}L)e^{-t_{1}^{2}L_{1}-t_{2}^{2}L_{2}}
.
We claim that

(4.87)

\widehat{H}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=O\Big(\prod_{i=1,2}(1+|\xi_{i}|)^{-3/2}\Big).

To prove this we use that
\operatorname{supp}H\subset[-R^{2},R^{2}]^{2}
and
F(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

to write

\displaystyle\widehat{H}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{[-a,a]^{2}}H(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})e^{-i(\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}+\lambda_{2}\xi_{2})}d\lambda_{1}d\lambda_{2}

\displaystyle=4\int_{[0,a]^{2}}H(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\cos\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}\cos\lambda_{2}\xi_{2}\,d\lambda_{1}d\lambda_{2},\quad a:=R^{2}.

Integrating by parts with respect to
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
we obtain

\widehat{H}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\frac{1}{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}}\int_{[0,a]^{2}}4\frac{\partial^{2}H}{\partial\lambda_{1}\partial\lambda_{2}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\sin\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}\sin\lambda_{2}\xi_{2}\,d\lambda_{1}d\lambda_{2}.

Evidently,

\displaystyle 4\frac{\partial^{2}H}{\partial\lambda_{1}\partial\lambda_{2}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=4e^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}F(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\sqrt{\lambda_{2}})+2\lambda_{1}^{-1/2}e^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}\frac{\partial F}{\partial\lambda_{1}}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\sqrt{\lambda_{2}})

\displaystyle\quad+2\lambda_{2}^{-1/2}e^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}\frac{\partial F}{\partial\lambda_{2}}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\sqrt{\lambda_{2}})+\lambda_{1}^{-1/2}\lambda_{2}^{-1/2}e^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}\partial\lambda_{2}}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\sqrt{\lambda_{2}})

\displaystyle\quad=:W_{1}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})+W_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})+W_{3}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})+W_{4}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}).

We next show that

(4.88)

\int_{[0,a]^{2}}W_{4}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\sin\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}\sin\lambda_{2}\xi_{2}\,d\lambda_{1}d\lambda_{2}=O\Big(\prod_{i=1,2}(1+|\xi_{i}|)^{-1/2}\Big).

One similarly shows that for
i=1,2,3

(4.89)

\int_{[0,a]^{2}}W_{i}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\sin\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}\sin\lambda_{2}\xi_{2}\,d\lambda_{1}d\lambda_{2}=O\Big(\prod_{i=1,2}(1+|\xi_{i}|)^{-1/2}\Big);

we omit the details.

Observe that from the condition
F(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
it follows that

\frac{\partial F}{\partial\lambda_{1}}(0,\lambda_{2})=\frac{\partial F}{\partial\lambda_{2}}(\lambda_{1},0)=0

and hence

(4.90)

\frac{\partial^{k+1}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}\partial^{k}\lambda_{2}}(0,\lambda_{2})=\frac{\partial^{k+1}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}^{k}\partial\lambda_{2}}(\lambda_{1},0)=0,\quad k\in{\mathbb{N}}.

We use the above to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}\partial\lambda_{2}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}\partial\lambda_{2}}(0,\lambda_{2})+\lambda_{1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial^{3}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}^{2}\partial\lambda_{2}}(t\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})dt

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial^{3}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}^{2}\partial\lambda_{2}}(t\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})dt.

Similarly, using again (
4.90
) we get

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{3}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}^{2}\partial\lambda_{2}}(t\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{3}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}^{2}\partial\lambda_{2}}(t\lambda_{1},0)+\lambda_{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial^{4}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}^{2}\partial\lambda_{2}^{2}}(t\lambda_{1},s\lambda_{2})ds

\displaystyle=\lambda_{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial^{4}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}^{2}\partial\lambda_{2}^{2}}(t\lambda_{1},s\lambda_{2})ds.

Combining the above identities we arrive at

\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}\partial\lambda_{2}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial^{4}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}^{2}\partial\lambda_{2}^{2}}(t\lambda_{1},s\lambda_{2})dtds.

In turn this yields

(4.91)

|W_{4}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})|\leq c\Big\|\frac{\partial^{4}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}^{2}\partial\lambda_{2}^{2}}\Big\|_{\infty},\quad{\bm{\lambda}}>{\bm{0}}.

Consider the case when
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}>1/a
; the other cases are easier to handle.
To prove (
4.88
) we split the set of integration as follows:

\displaystyle[0,a]^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big[0,\frac{1}{\xi_{1}}\Big]\times\Big[0,\frac{1}{\xi_{2}}\Big]\cup\Big[0,\frac{1}{\xi_{1}}\Big]\times\Big[\frac{1}{\xi_{2}},a\Big]\cup\Big[\frac{1}{\xi_{1}},a\Big]\times\Big[0,\frac{1}{\xi_{2}}\Big]\cup\Big[\frac{1}{\xi_{1}},a\Big]\times\Big[\frac{1}{\xi_{2}},a\Big]

\displaystyle=:A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}\cup A_{4}.

From (
4.91
) it readily follows that

(4.92)

J_{1}:=\int_{A_{1}}W_{4}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\sin\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}\sin\lambda_{2}\xi_{2}\,d\lambda_{1}d\lambda_{2}=O\Big(\prod_{i=1,2}(1+|\xi_{i}|)^{-1}\Big).

We next show that

(4.93)

J_{4}:=\int_{A_{4}}W_{4}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\sin\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}\sin\lambda_{2}\xi_{2}\,d\lambda_{1}d\lambda_{2}=O\Big(\prod_{i=1,2}(1+|\xi_{i}|)^{-1/2}\Big).

The integrals over
A_{2}
and
A_{3}
are estimated similarly.

Integrating by parts with respect to
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
and using that
W_{4}(\lambda_{1},a)=W_{4}(a,\lambda_{2})=0

we get

\displaystyle J_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}}\Big(W_{4}\big(\xi_{1}^{-1},\xi_{2}^{-1}\big)\cos^{2}1+\int_{1/\xi_{1}}^{a}\frac{\partial W_{4}}{\partial\lambda_{1}}\big(\lambda_{1},\xi_{2}^{-1}\big)\cos\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}\cos 1\,d\lambda_{1}

\displaystyle+\int_{1/\xi_{2}}^{a}\frac{\partial W_{4}}{\partial\lambda_{2}}\big(\xi_{1}^{-1},\lambda_{2}\big)\cos 1\cos\lambda_{2}\xi_{2}\,d\lambda_{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{1/\xi_{1}}^{a}\int_{1/\xi_{2}}^{a}\frac{\partial^{2}W_{4}}{\partial\lambda_{1}\partial\lambda_{2}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\cos\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}\cos\lambda_{2}\xi_{2}\,d\lambda_{1}d\lambda_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=:\frac{1}{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}}(J_{41}+J_{42}+J_{43}+J_{44}).

From (
4.91
) it follows that

|J_{41}|\leq c
.
We next estimate
J_{44}
. The estimation of
J_{41},J_{42},J_{43}
is similar.
One easily derives that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}W_{4}}{\partial\lambda_{1}\partial\lambda_{2}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{1}^{-3/2}\lambda_{2}^{-3/2}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}^{-3/2}\lambda_{2}^{-1/2}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}^{-1/2}\lambda_{2}^{-3/2}\Big)e^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}\partial\lambda_{2}}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\sqrt{\lambda_{2}})

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\,+\Big(-\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{1}^{-3/2}\lambda_{2}^{-1}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{2}^{-1/2}\Big)e^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}\frac{\partial^{3}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}^{2}\partial\lambda_{2}}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\sqrt{\lambda_{2}})

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\quad\;\,+\Big(\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}^{-1/2}\lambda_{2}^{-1}-\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{2}^{-3/2}\Big)e^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}\frac{\partial^{3}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}\partial\lambda_{2}^{2}}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\sqrt{\lambda_{2}})

\displaystyle\hskip 144.54pt\qquad\;+\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{2}^{-1}e^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}\frac{\partial^{4}F}{\partial\lambda_{1}^{2}\partial\lambda_{2}^{2}}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\sqrt{\lambda_{2}})

and using that
F\in\mathcal{C}^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
F
is compactly supported we get

J_{44}=O(\xi_{1}^{1/2}\xi_{2}^{1/2})
.
This along with the similar estimates for
J_{42},J_{43}
and the estimate
|J_{41}|\leq c
imply (
4.93
).
In turn, (
4.92
), (
4.93
), and the similar estimates for the integrals over
A_{2}
and
A_{3}

yield (
4.88
).
Finally, (
4.88
) and (
4.89
) imply (
4.87
).

From (
4.87
) it follows that
\widehat{H}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and obviously
H\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Then the Fourier inversion formula gives

(4.94)

H({\bm{\lambda}})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{H}({\bm{\xi}})e^{i{\bm{\lambda}}\cdot{\bm{\xi}}}d{\bm{\xi}}.

Assuming that
\varphi,\psi\in L^{2}(X)\cap L^{1}(X)
we get using that

F({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})=H({\mathbf{t}}^{2}L)e^{-{\mathbf{t}}^{2}L}
and (
4.94
)

\displaystyle\langle F({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})\varphi,

\displaystyle\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}H(t_{1}^{2}\lambda_{1},t_{2}^{2}\lambda_{2})e^{-t_{1}^{2}\lambda_{1}-t_{2}^{2}\lambda_{2}}d\langle E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\varphi,\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{H}({\bm{\xi}})e^{i(t_{1}^{2}\lambda_{1}\xi_{1}+t_{2}^{2}\lambda_{2}\xi_{2})}d{\bm{\xi}}\Big)e^{-t_{1}^{2}\lambda_{1}-t_{2}^{2}\lambda_{2}}d\langle E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\varphi,\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{H}({\bm{\xi}})\Big(\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}e^{-t_{1}^{2}\lambda_{1}(1-i\xi_{1})-t_{2}^{2}\lambda_{2}(1-i\xi_{2})}d\langle E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\varphi,\psi\rangle\Big)d{\bm{\xi}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{H}({\bm{\xi}})\Big\langle e^{-t_{1}^{2}\lambda_{1}(1-i\xi_{1})L_{1}-t_{2}^{2}\lambda_{2}(1-i\xi_{2})L_{2}}\varphi,\psi\Big\rangle d{\bm{\xi}}.

Above the shift of order of integration is justified by Fubini’s theorem and the fact that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\widehat{H}({\bm{\xi}})|

\displaystyle\Big(\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}\big|e^{-t_{1}^{2}\lambda_{1}(1-i\xi_{1})-t_{2}^{2}\lambda_{2}(1-i\xi_{2})}\big||d\langle E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\varphi,\psi\rangle|\Big)d{\bm{\xi}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\widehat{H}({\bm{\xi}})|\Big(\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}|d\langle E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\varphi,\psi\rangle|\Big)d{\bm{\xi}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\widehat{H}({\bm{\xi}})|d{\bm{\xi}}\Big(\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}d\|E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\varphi\|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}d\|E_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}\psi\|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{H}\|_{L^{1}}\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}}<\infty.

In going further we get from above

\displaystyle\langle F({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})\varphi,\overline{\psi}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{H}({\bm{\xi}})\Big\langle e^{-t_{1}^{2}\lambda_{1}(1-i\xi_{1})L_{1}-t_{2}^{2}\lambda_{2}(1-i\xi_{2})L_{2}}\varphi,\overline{\psi}\Big\rangle d\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{H}({\bm{\xi}})\int_{X}\int_{X}p_{(t_{1}^{2}(1-i\xi_{1}),t_{2}^{2}(1-i\xi_{2}))}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\varphi({\mathbf{y}})\psi({\mathbf{x}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{x}})d{\bm{\xi}}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\int_{X}\Big(\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{H}({\bm{\xi}})p_{(t_{1}^{2}(1-i\xi_{1}),t_{2}^{2}(1-i\xi_{2}))}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d{\bm{\xi}}\Big)\varphi({\mathbf{y}})\psi({\mathbf{x}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{x}})

To justify the shift of order of integration above we apply Fubini’s theorem taking into account that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{X}\int_{X}

\displaystyle|\widehat{H}({\bm{\xi}})||p_{(t_{1}^{2}(1-i\xi_{1}),t_{2}^{2}(1-i\xi_{2}))}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})||\varphi({\mathbf{y}})||\psi({\mathbf{x}})|d\mu({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{x}})d{\bm{\xi}}

\displaystyle\leq c\|\widehat{H}\|_{L^{1}}\|\varphi\|_{L^{1}}\|\psi\|_{L^{1}}<\infty.

From above it follows that

{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{H}(\xi)p_{(t_{1}^{2}(1-i\xi_{1}),t_{2}^{2}(1-i\xi_{2}))}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d{\bm{\xi}},

implying

\displaystyle\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{H}({\bm{\xi}})\int_{X}p_{(t_{1}^{2}(1-i\xi_{1}),t_{2}^{2}(1-i\xi_{2}))}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d{\mathbf{y}}d\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{H}({\bm{\xi}})d{\bm{\xi}}=H({\bm{0}})=F({\bm{0}}),

where we used (
2.8
), (
2.7
), (
4.33
), and (
4.94
).
The shift of the order of integration above is easily justified by applying Fubini’s theorem.
The proof of (
4.67
) is complete.
∎

We next extend Theorem
4.18
from the case of compactly supported functions
to the case of smooth ones with sufficiently fast decay on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Theorem 4.21
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{C}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
,
k_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
k_{i}\geq 3d_{i}/2
,
i=1,2
,

k_{1}+k_{2}\geq 4
,
be real-valued and
obey the conditions:

F(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

and for
{\bm{\beta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
such that
|{\bm{\beta}}|\leq k_{1}+k_{2}

|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}F({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq C_{\mathbf{k}}(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-r},\quad r>d_{1}+d_{2}+k_{1}+k_{2}.

Then
F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})
,
{\bm{\delta}}=(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})>{\bm{0}}
, is an integral operator
with kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
satisfying

(4.95)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c_{\mathbf{k}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\quad\text{and}

(4.96)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|\leq c_{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\sum_{i=1,2}\Big(\frac{\rho_{i}(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})}{\delta_{i}}\Big)^{\alpha_{i}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

if
\rho_{i}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\delta_{i}
,
i=1,2
, where
{\mathbf{k}}=(k_{1},k_{2})

and the constants
c_{\mathbf{k}},c_{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}}
depend on
k_{1}
,
k_{2}
,
{\mathbf{d}}
and
C_{\mathbf{k}}
.
Moreover,

(4.97)

\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})=F({\bm{0}}),\quad\forall{\mathbf{x}}\in X.

Proof.

Let
\varphi_{0},\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be functions satisfying conditions (
4.49
).
Set
\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}}):=\varphi(2^{-j}{\bm{\lambda}})
,
j\geq 1
, and
F_{j}({\bm{\lambda}}):=F({\bm{\lambda}})\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}})
,
j\geq 0
.
Then

(4.98)

F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})=\sum_{j\geq 0}F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})\varphi_{j}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})=\sum_{j\geq 0}F_{j}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L}),

where the convergence is strong in
L^{2}
.
Observe that
F_{j}(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=F_{j}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

and for
j\geq 1
we have

\operatorname{supp}F_{j}\subset\Omega_{j}:=\{{\bm{\lambda}}:2^{j-1}\leq|{\bm{\lambda}}|\leq 2^{j+1}\}

and

\|F_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|F\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{j})}\leq c2^{-jr},\quad\|F_{j}\|_{W^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{\infty}}\leq c\|F\|_{W^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{\infty}(\Omega_{j})}\leq c2^{-jr}.

We now apply Theorem
4.18
to
F_{j}
with
R=2^{j+1}
to obtain

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq\sum_{j\geq 0}|{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|

(4.99)

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{j\geq 0}2^{j(d_{1}+d_{2})}\big(\|F_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}+2^{j(k_{1}+k_{2})}\|F_{j}\|_{W^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{\infty}}\big){\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{j\geq 0}2^{j(d_{1}+d_{2})}2^{-jr}2^{j(k_{1}+k_{2})}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},k}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\leq c{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}),

where for the last inequality we used that
r>(d_{1}+d_{2})+(k_{1}+k_{2})
.
Therefore,
{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
satisfies (
4.95
).

The proof that
{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
obeys (
4.96
) is similar.
We omit it.

To prove (
4.97
) we use (
4.98
) and (
4.7
) to write

{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=\sum_{j\geq 0}{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

and integrating we get

\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})=\sum_{j\geq 0}\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})=\sum_{j\geq 0}F_{j}({\bm{1}})=F({\bm{1}}).

Here we applied (
4.67
) to each of the kernels
{\mathscr{K}}_{F_{j}({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
using the fact that
F_{j}
is compactly supported.
This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

We will frequently need the decay of kernels of operators of the form

L^{{\bm{\nu}}}F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L}):=(L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}})F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L}),\quad{\bm{\nu}}=(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}.

In this regard we have the following corollary of Theorem
4.21
.

Corollary 4.22
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
be real-valued,

F(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
and

|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}F({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq c_{r,{\bm{\beta}}}(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-r},\quad\forall r>0,\;{\bm{\beta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}.

Then for any
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
and
{\bm{\delta}}>{\bm{0}}
,

L^{{\bm{\nu}}}F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})
is an integral operator with
kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{{\bm{\nu}}}F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
satisfying the following inequalities:
For any
{\bm{\sigma}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
there exists a constant
c_{\bm{\sigma}}>0
such that

(4.100)

\big|{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{{\bm{\nu}}}F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\big|\leq c_{\bm{\sigma}}{\bm{\delta}}^{-2{\bm{\nu}}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

and

(4.101)

\big|{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{{\bm{\nu}}}F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{{\bm{\nu}}}F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})\big|\leq c_{\bm{\sigma}}{\bm{\delta}}^{-2{\bm{\nu}}}\sum_{i=1,2}\Big(\frac{\rho_{i}(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})}{\delta_{i}}\Big)^{\alpha_{i}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

if
\rho_{i}(y_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})\leq\delta_{i}
,
i=1,2
.

Proof.

Set
G(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=\lambda_{1}^{2\nu_{1}}\lambda_{2}^{2\nu_{2}}F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

and observe that

G({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})=\delta_{1}^{2\nu_{1}}\delta_{2}^{2\nu_{2}}L^{{\bm{\nu}}}F({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L}).

It is easily seen that
G
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
4.21

and, therefore, applying that theorem to
G
the result follows.
∎

4.8.
Maximal operators

The Hardy-Littlewood maximal operator is a handful tool in proving estimates in Harmonic analysis.
We will use it extensively in what follows.

In our setting the (strong) maximal operator
\mathcal{M}_{r}
with parameter
r>0
is defined by

(4.102)

\mathcal{M}_{r}f({\mathbf{x}}):=\sup_{B_{1}\times B_{2}\ni{\mathbf{x}}}\Big(\frac{1}{\mu(B_{1}\times B_{2})}\int_{B_{1}\times B_{2}}|f({\mathbf{y}})|^{r}d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\Big)^{1/r},\quad{\mathbf{x}}\in X,

where the
\sup
is taken over all balls
B_{1}\subset X_{1}
,
B_{2}\subset X_{2}

such that
{\mathbf{x}}\in B_{1}\times B_{2}
.

The maximal operator
\mathscr{M}_{1}
in the direction of
x_{1}\in X_{1}
is defined by

\mathscr{M}_{1}f({\mathbf{x}})=\sup_{B_{1}\ni x_{1}}\frac{1}{\mu_{1}(B_{1})}\int_{B_{1}}|f(y_{1},x_{2})|d\mu_{1}(y_{1}),\;\;{\mathbf{x}}\in X,

where the
\sup
is taken over all balls
B_{1}
in
X_{1}
containing
x_{1}
.
The maximal operator
\mathscr{M}_{2}
in the direction of
x_{2}\in X_{2}
is defined similarly.
It is easy to see that this inequality is valid:

(4.103)

\mathcal{M}_{r}f({\mathbf{x}})\leq\left(\mathscr{M}_{2}(\mathscr{M}_{1}|f|^{r})\right)^{1/r}({\mathbf{x}}),\quad{\mathbf{x}}\in X.

We will utilize the following version of the Fefferman-Stein vector-valued maximal inequality:
If
p\in(0,\infty),\;q\in(0,\infty]
, and
0<r<\min\{p,q\}
,
then for any sequence of functions
\{f_{j}\}
on
X

(4.104)

\Big\|\Big(\sum_{j}|\mathcal{M}_{r}f_{j}|^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}\leq c\Big\|\Big(\sum_{j}|f_{j}|^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}.

From the fact that the Radon measure
\mu_{i}
(
i=1,2
) obeys the doubling condition (
2.1
)
it follows that the maximal inequality is valid in
(X_{i},\mu_{i})
(see
[
47
]
and also
[
34
]
)
and then (
4.104
) follows by iteration.

For future use we next prove the following

Lemma 4.23
.

Let
{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\tau}}\in(0,\infty)^{2}
,
{\bm{\tau}}>{\mathbf{d}}
, and
r>0
.
Then there exists a constant
c>0
such that for any measurable function
f
on
X
we have

(4.105)

\int_{X}\frac{|f({\mathbf{y}})|^{r}}{V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\delta_{i}^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{\tau_{i}}}d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\leq c[\mathcal{M}_{r}f({\mathbf{x}})]^{r},\quad{\mathbf{x}}\in X.

Proof.

We introduce the abbreviated notation

F({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}):=\frac{|f({\mathbf{y}})|^{r}}{V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\delta_{i}^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{\tau_{i}}}

and

B_{i,j}:=B_{i}(x_{i},2^{j}\delta_{i})
,
i=1,2
.
Set

W_{i,0}:=B_{i,0}\quad\hbox{and}\quad W_{i,j}:=B_{i,j}\setminus B_{i,j-1},\quad j\geq 1.

Note that

X=\cup_{j,k\geq 0}W_{1,j}\times W_{2,k}

and

W_{1,j}\times W_{2,k}\subset B_{1,j}\times B_{2,k}
.
Therefore,

(4.106)

\int_{X}F({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})=\sum_{j,k\geq 0}\int_{W_{1,j}\times W_{2,k}}F({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}}).

Observe that

F({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\leq c2^{-j\tau_{1}-k\tau_{2}}V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1}|f({\mathbf{y}})|^{r},\quad{\mathbf{y}}\in W_{1,j}\times W_{2,k},

and hence

\displaystyle\int_{W_{1,j}\times W_{2,k}}F({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\leq c2^{-j\tau_{1}-k\tau_{2}}V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1}\int_{B_{1,j}\times B_{2,k}}|f({\mathbf{y}})|^{r}d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

(4.107)

\displaystyle=c2^{-j\tau_{1}-k\tau_{2}}\frac{\mu(B_{1,j}\times B_{2,k})}{V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})}\frac{1}{\mu(B_{1,j}\times B_{2,k})}\int_{B_{1,j}\times B_{2,k}}|f({\mathbf{y}})|^{r}d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c2^{-j(\tau_{1}-d_{1})-k(\tau_{2}-d_{2})}\mathcal{M}_{r}f({\mathbf{x}})^{r},

where we used that in light of (
2.9
) and (
3.9
)

\displaystyle\mu(B_{1,j}\times B_{2,k})

\displaystyle=\mu_{1}(B_{1}(x_{1},2^{j}\delta_{1}))\mu_{2}(B_{2}(x_{2},2^{k}\delta_{2}))

\displaystyle\leq c2^{jd_{1}+kd_{2}}\mu_{1}(B_{1}(x_{1},\delta_{1}))\mu_{2}(B_{2}(x_{2},\delta_{2}))=c2^{jd_{1}+kd_{2}}V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}}).

Finally, we insert (
4.8
) in (
4.106
) and sum up using that
\tau_{i}>d_{i}

to obtain (
4.105
).
∎

5.
Distributions associated to operators

Distributions associated to operators have been introduced in
[
39
]
. Here we develop distributions in the two-parameter setting of this article.

5.1.
Test functions and distributions

We will operate in the setting described in Subsection
2.1
.
We define the class of test functions
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}(L_{1},L_{2})

as the set of all function

\phi\in\cap_{{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}D(L^{{\bm{\nu}}}),\quad L^{{\bm{\nu}}}:=L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}},\;{\bm{\nu}}=(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}),

with
D(L^{{\bm{\nu}}})
being the domain of the operator
L^{{\bm{\nu}}}
, such that

(5.1)

\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi):=\sup_{{\mathbf{x}}\in X}\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{k}\max_{0\leq\nu_{i}\leq m}\big|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\phi({\mathbf{x}})\big|<\infty,\quad\forall\,k,m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}.

Here the point
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=(x_{01},x_{02})\in X
is selected arbitrarily and fixed once and for all.
Clearly, the definition of the class
\mathcal{S}
is independent of the choice of
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}
.

It should be pointed out that the term
\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{k}
in (
5.1
)
can be removed if
\mu(X_{i})<\infty
for
i=1
or
i=2

because
\mu(X_{i})<\infty
if and only if
\operatorname{diam}(X_{i})<\infty
, see
[
12
, Proposition 2.1]
.

Proposition 5.1
.

The vector space
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}(L_{1},L_{2})
with the natural topology induced
by the norms
\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi)
,
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,
is a Fréchet space.

Proof.

Clearly,
\{\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi)\}_{m,k\geq 0}
is a countable family of norms
and hence
\mathcal{S}
is a metrizable locally convex space.
Therefore, to prove that
\mathcal{S}
is Fréchet we only need to show that it is complete.
Assume that
\{\phi_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{S}
,
i.e.
\lim_{n,j\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi_{n}-\phi_{j})=0
for all
m,k\geq 0
.
Fix
k\geq(d_{1}\vee d_{2}+1)/2
. Then for any
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
with
0\leq\nu_{i}\leq m
we have

\displaystyle\big\|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}(\phi_{n}-\phi_{j})\big\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi_{n}-\phi_{j})\int_{X_{1}\times X_{2}}\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{-2k}d\mu_{1}\times d\mu_{2}

\displaystyle\leq cV_{1}(x_{01},1)V_{2}(x_{02},1)\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi_{n}-\phi_{j}),

where we used (
2.12
).
Therefore,
\{L^{\bm{\nu}}\phi_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{2}(X,d\mu)

and hence there exists

\psi_{\bm{\nu}}\in L^{2}(X,d\mu)
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\phi_{n}-\psi_{\bm{\nu}}\|_{2}=0
.

Denote
\phi:=\psi_{(0,0)}
and let
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
.
From

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\phi_{n}-\phi\|_{2}=0\quad\hbox{and}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\phi_{n}-\psi_{\bm{\nu}}\|_{2}=0

and the fact that the operator

L^{{\bm{\nu}}}=L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}}
is closed (as a self-adjoint operator, see Proposition
4.1
)
it follows that
\phi\in D(L^{\bm{\nu}})
and

(5.2)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\phi_{n}-L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\phi\|_{2}=0.

Furthermore,

\big\|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\phi_{n}-L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\phi_{j}\big\|_{\infty}\leq\mathcal{P}_{m,0}(\phi_{n}-\phi_{j})\to 0
as
n,j\to\infty
.
From this, (
5.2
) and the completeness of
L^{\infty}
it follows that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\phi_{n}-L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\phi\|_{\infty}=0,\quad\forall{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}.

In turn, this and the fact that

\lim_{n,j\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi_{n}-\phi_{j})=0
for all
m,k\geq 0

imply that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi_{n}-\phi)=0
for all
m,k\geq 0
,
which completes the proof.
∎

Definition 5.2
.

The space
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(L_{1},L_{2})
of distributions associated to
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
is defined as the set of all
continuous linear functionals on
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}(L_{1},L_{2})
.
The action of
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
on
\overline{\phi}\in\mathcal{S}
will be denoted by

\langle f,\phi\rangle:=f(\overline{\phi})
,
which is consistent with the inner product in
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
.

Clearly, for any
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
there exist constants
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
c>0
such that

(5.3)

|\langle f,\phi\rangle|\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi),\quad\forall\phi\in\mathcal{S}.

We next show that there are sufficiently many test functions.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
be real-valued and

\varphi(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=\varphi(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Recall the notation
\varphi(\sqrt{L}):=\varphi(\sqrt{L_{1}},\sqrt{L_{2}})

and
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}(L_{1},L_{2})
.
Then:

(i)

\varphi(\sqrt{L})
is an integral operator whose kernel

{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)\in\mathcal{S}
,
\forall{\mathbf{x}}\in X
,
and
{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}(\cdot,{\mathbf{y}})\in\mathcal{S}
,
\forall{\mathbf{y}}\in X
.

(ii)
For each
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
we have
\varphi(\sqrt{L})\phi\in\mathcal{S}
.
Furthermore, the spectral multiplier
\varphi(\sqrt{L})
maps continuously
\mathcal{S}
into
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

(i) Since
{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})={\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{x}})
for
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X
(
\varphi
is real-valued)
it suffices to only show that
{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)\in\mathcal{S}
.
To this end we need to show that

(5.4)

\mathcal{P}_{m,k}({\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot))<\infty,\quad\forall m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}.

Fix
{\mathbf{x}}\in X
.
From Corollary
4.22
it readily follows that
for any
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
there exists a constant
c>0
such that for all
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
with
\nu_{i}\leq m

\sup_{{\mathbf{y}}\in X}\prod_{i=1,2}(1+\rho(x_{i},y_{i})^{k}|{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{\bm{\nu}}\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{1}})^{-1}.

Therefore, to show that
{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)\in\mathcal{S}
it suffices to show that

(5.5)

{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{\bm{\nu}}\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=L^{\bm{\nu}}[{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)]({\mathbf{y}}),\quad{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in X.

For this we first need to verify that

{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)\in D(L^{\bm{\nu}})
.
Let
g\in D(L^{\bm{\nu}})
.
As is well known (e.g.
[
55
, Chapter XI, Section 12, Theorem 3, (iv)]
)

L^{\bm{\nu}}g\in D(\varphi(\sqrt{L}))
if and only if
g\in D(\varphi(\sqrt{L})L^{\bm{\nu}})
, and

(5.6)

\varphi(\sqrt{L})(L^{\bm{\nu}}g)=[\varphi(\sqrt{L})L^{\bm{\nu}}]g=[L^{\bm{\nu}}\varphi(\sqrt{L})]g.

But
\varphi(\sqrt{L})
is a bounded operator on
L^{2}(X,d\mu)

and hence
L^{\bm{\nu}}g\in D(\varphi(\sqrt{L}))
,
which implies that
g\in D(\varphi(\sqrt{L})L^{\bm{\nu}})

and (
5.6
) is valid.
Hence, for almost all
{\mathbf{x}}\in X

(5.7)

\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{\bm{\nu}}\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})g({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})=\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})L^{\bm{\nu}}g({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}}).

But the kernels
{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{\bm{\nu}}\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
and
{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
are continuous
and hence (
5.7
) is valid for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in X
.
Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we obtain from (
5.7
)

\Big|\int_{X}L^{\bm{\nu}}g({\mathbf{y}}){\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\Big|\leq\|{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{\bm{\nu}}\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)\|_{2}\|g\|_{2}\leq c\|g\|_{2},

where we used Corollary
4.22
and (
3.19
).
Thus

|\big\langle L^{\bm{\nu}}g,{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)\big\rangle|\leq c\|g\|_{2},\quad\forall g\in D(L^{\bm{\nu}}),

which leads to the conclusion that

{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)\in D((L^{\bm{\nu}})^{*})
.
But
D(L^{\bm{\nu}})=D((L^{\bm{\nu}})^{*})
because
L^{\bm{\nu}}
is self-adjoint.
Hence
{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)\in D(L^{\bm{\nu}})
.

We now show (
5.5
).
Appealing to (
5.7
) and the fact that
L^{\bm{\nu}}
is self-adjoint
we obtain for all
g\in D(L^{\bm{\nu}})
and
{\mathbf{x}}\in X

\displaystyle\big\langle{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{\bm{\nu}}\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot),\overline{g}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle L^{\bm{\nu}}g,{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)\big\rangle=\big\langle g,(L^{\bm{\nu}})^{*}[{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)]\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle g,L^{\bm{\nu}}[{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)]\big\rangle=\big\langle L^{\bm{\nu}}[{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)],\overline{g}\big\rangle,

which implies (
5.5
).

(ii) Let
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and assume that
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
with
{\bm{\nu}}_{i}\leq m
and

{\bm{\sigma}}\in(0,\infty)^{2}
is such that
\sigma_{i}\geq k+2d_{i}+1
,
i=1,2
.
Denote
\tilde{d}:=\lfloor d_{1}\vee d_{2}\rfloor+1
.
Using (
4.100
) and (
3.20
) we get for
\phi\in\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle\big|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\varphi(\sqrt{L})

\displaystyle\phi({\mathbf{x}})\big|=\big|\varphi(\sqrt{L})L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\phi({\mathbf{x}})\big|\leq\int_{X}|{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})||L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\phi({\mathbf{y}})|d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{1}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\phi({\mathbf{y}})|d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m,k+\tilde{d}+1}(\phi)\int_{X}V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{1}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{1}},\bar{k}+{\mathbf{d}}+{\bm{1}}}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}){\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{1}},\bar{k}+{\mathbf{d}}+{\bm{1}}}^{*}({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m,k+\tilde{d}+1}(\phi){\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{1}},\bar{k}+{\bm{1}}}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0})\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m,k+\tilde{d}+1}(\phi){\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{1}},\bar{k}}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0}),

where
\bar{k}=:(k,k)
and
{\bm{1}}:=(1,1)
.
From above and (
3.10
) it readily follows that

\varphi(\sqrt{L})\phi\in\mathcal{S}
and

\mathcal{P}_{m,k}((\varphi\sqrt{L})\phi)\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m,k+\tilde{d}+1}(\phi)
,
which leads to the conclusion that

\varphi(\sqrt{L})
maps continuously
\mathcal{S}
into
\mathcal{S}
.
∎

We use Proposition
5.3
(ii) to make the following

Definition 5.4
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
be real-valued and

\varphi(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=\varphi(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
For any
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(L_{1},L_{2})
we define
\varphi(\sqrt{L})f
by

(5.8)

\langle\varphi(\sqrt{L})f,\phi\rangle=\langle f,\varphi(\sqrt{L})\phi\rangle,\quad\forall\phi\in\mathcal{S}.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\varphi
be as in Definition
5.4
.
Then for any
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(L_{1},L_{2})

(5.9)

\varphi(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}}):=\langle f,{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)\rangle\quad\hbox{for}\quad{\mathbf{x}}\in X.

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
Just as in (
5.3
) there exist constants
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
c>0
such that

(5.10)

|\langle f,\phi\rangle|\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi),\quad\forall\phi\in\mathcal{S}.

Fix
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
.
Clearly,

\varphi(\sqrt{L})\phi({\mathbf{x}})=\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\phi({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}}),\quad{\mathbf{x}}\in X.

We will regard the above integral as a Bochner integral over the Banach space

V_{m,k}:=\{g\in\cap_{0\leq\nu_{i}\leq m}D(L^{\bm{\nu}}):\|g\|_{V_{m,k}}:=\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(g)<\infty\}

with
\mathcal{P}_{m,k}
defined in (
5.1
), see e.g.
[
55
]
, pp. 131–133.
Just as in the proof of Proposition
5.1
it follows that
the space
V_{m,k}
is complete and hence it is a Banach space.
By the Hahn-Banach theorem and (
5.10
) the continuous linear functional
f

can be extended to
V_{m,k}
with the same norm.
We denote this extension by
f
again.

Denote
F_{\mathbf{y}}({\mathbf{x}}):={\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\phi({\mathbf{y}})
. Then

\displaystyle\|F_{\mathbf{y}}\|_{V_{m,k}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{{\mathbf{x}}\in X}\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{k}\max_{0\leq{\bm{\nu}}_{i}\leq m}\big|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\phi({\mathbf{y}})\big|

\displaystyle=\sup_{{\mathbf{x}}\in X}\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{k}\max_{0\leq{\bm{\nu}}_{i}\leq m}\big|{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\phi({\mathbf{y}})\big|,

where we used (
5.5
).
Clearly, the function
\varphi
obeys the conditions of Corollary
4.22
and hence
there exists a constant
c_{k}>0
such that

|{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\varphi(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c_{k}V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{1}})^{-1}\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{-k},\quad 0\leq\nu_{i}\leq m.

On the other hand, since
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
for any
N>0
we have

|\phi({\mathbf{y}})|\leq c_{N}\mathcal{P}_{0,N}(\phi)\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(y_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{-N}.

We choose
N=k+2(d_{1}\vee d_{2})+1
.
Putting the above estimates together we get

\displaystyle\|F_{\mathbf{y}}\|_{V_{m,k}}\leq\frac{c\mathcal{P}_{0,N}(\phi)}{V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{1}})}\sup_{{\mathbf{x}}\in X}\frac{\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{k}}{\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{k}\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(y_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{N}}.

Using the obvious inequality

1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\leq(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i}))(1+\rho_{i}(y_{i},x_{0i}))

and (
3.13
) we obtain

\displaystyle\|F_{\mathbf{y}}\|_{V_{m,k}}\leq\frac{c\mathcal{P}_{0,N}(\phi)}{V({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\bm{1}})\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(y_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{d_{i}+1}}.

From this and (
3.19
) it follows that

\int_{X}\|{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}(\cdot,{\mathbf{y}})\phi({\mathbf{y}})\|_{V_{m,k}}d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\leq c\mathcal{P}_{0,N}(\phi)<\infty.

Now, applying the theory of Bochner’s integral we obtain

\displaystyle\langle f,\varphi(\sqrt{L})\phi\rangle=\Big\langle f,\int_{X}{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}(\cdot,{\mathbf{y}})\phi({\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\Big\rangle=\int_{X}\big\langle f,{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi(\sqrt{L})}(\cdot,{\mathbf{y}})\big\rangle\overline{\phi({\mathbf{y}})}d\mu({\mathbf{y}}).

This coupled with (
5.8
) implies (
5.9
).
∎

Remark 5.6
.

From Proposition
5.3
(ii) it readily follows that the operator

\varphi(\sqrt{L})
from above maps continuously
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
into
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Our next step is to show that
\varphi(\sqrt{L})f
is a slowly growing continuous function
for smooth
\varphi
and
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
be real-valued and

\varphi(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=\varphi(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Let
{\bm{\delta}}=(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})>{\bm{0}}
.
Then for any
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(L_{1},L_{2})
,
\varphi(\delta\sqrt{L})f
is a slowly growing continuous function.
More explicitly,
there exist constants
N,c>0

(
depending on
f
)
such that

(5.11)

|\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}})|\leq cV(x_{0},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1}\prod_{i=1,2}(\delta_{i}^{N}+\delta_{i}^{-N})\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{N}\quad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle|\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle-\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime})|

(5.12)

\displaystyle\leq cV({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1}\prod_{i=1,2}(\delta_{i}^{N}+\delta_{i}^{-N})\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{N}\sum_{i=1,2}\rho_{i}(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}

if
\rho_{i}(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})\leq\delta_{i}
.
Here
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
are from (
2.4
).

Proof.

We will only prove (
5.7
); the proof of (
5.11
) is easier and will be omitted.
Let
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
Just as in (
5.3
) there exist
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
such that

|\langle f,\phi\rangle|\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi),\quad\forall\phi\in\mathcal{S}.

We use this, (
5.8
), and (
5.5
) to obtain

\displaystyle|\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}})-\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle=\big|\big\langle f,{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)-{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\cdot)\big\rangle\big|\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m,k}\big({\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)-{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\cdot)\big)

\displaystyle=c\sup_{{\mathbf{y}}\in X}\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(y_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 101.17755pt\times\max_{0\leq\nu_{i}\leq m}\big|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}[{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},\cdot)]({\mathbf{y}})-L^{{\bm{\nu}}}[{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\cdot)]({\mathbf{y}})\big|

\displaystyle\leq c\max_{0\leq\nu_{i}\leq m}\sup_{{\mathbf{y}}\in X}\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(y_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{k}\big|{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{\bm{\nu}}\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{\bm{\nu}}\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}})\big|.

The function
\varphi
obeys the conditions of Corollary
4.22

and hence if
\rho_{i}(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})\leq\delta_{i}
, then

\displaystyle\big|{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{\bm{\nu}}\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{\bm{\nu}}\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}})\big|

\displaystyle\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1}{\bm{\delta}}^{-2{\bm{\nu}}}\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\delta_{i}^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{-k}\sum_{i=1,2}(\rho_{i}(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})/\delta_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{-1}\prod_{i=1,2}(1+\delta_{i})^{k}(1+\delta_{i}^{-1})^{2\nu_{i}+\alpha_{i}}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{-k}\sum_{i=1,2}\rho_{i}(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}.

Now, we use that

1+\rho_{i}(y_{i},x_{0i})\leq(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i}))(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i}))

and

\displaystyle V({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\bm{\delta}})

\displaystyle\leq c\prod_{i=1,2}(1+\delta_{i}^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i}))^{d_{i}}V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})

\displaystyle\leq c\prod_{i=1,2}(1+\delta_{i}^{-1})^{d_{i}}(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i}))^{d_{i}}V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}}),

which follows from (
3.13
) to obtain

\displaystyle\big|{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{\bm{\nu}}\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{L^{\bm{\nu}}\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}})\big|

\displaystyle\leq c\frac{\prod_{i=1,2}(1+\delta_{i})^{k}(1+\delta_{i}^{-1})^{2\nu_{i}+\alpha_{i}+d_{i}}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{k+d_{i}}}{V({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\bm{\delta}})\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(y_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{k}}\sum_{i=1,2}\rho_{i}(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}.

Putting all of the above together we obtain (
5.7
)
with
N
sufficiently large.
∎

5.2.
Decomposition

We next establish a basic convergence theorem and a
Calderón type reproducing
formula,
which will play an important role in this study.
We begin with the following

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be a real-valued function that satisfies the conditions:

\varphi({\bm{0}})=1
,

\partial^{\bm{\beta}}\varphi({\bm{0}})=0
for all
{\bm{\beta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
with
|{\bm{\beta}}|\geq 1
,
{\bm{0}}:=(0,0)
, and

\varphi(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=\varphi(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Then for any
\phi\in\mathcal{S}

(5.13)

\phi=\lim_{{\bm{\delta}}\to{\bm{0}}}\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})\phi\quad\mbox{ in }\;\mathcal{S}

and, therefore, for any
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}

(5.14)

f=\lim_{{\bm{\delta}}\to{\bm{0}}}\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})f\quad\mbox{ in }\;\mathcal{S}^{\prime}.

Proof.

We need to show that for any
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}

(5.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{{\bm{\delta}}\rightarrow{\bm{0}}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi-\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})\phi)

\displaystyle=\lim_{{\bm{\delta}}\rightarrow{\bm{0}}}\sup_{{\mathbf{x}}\in X}\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{k}\max_{0\leq\nu_{i}\leq m}\big|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}\big(\phi-\varphi({\bm{\delta}}\sqrt{L})\phi\big)({\mathbf{x}})|=0.

Choose
{\mathbf{n}}=(n_{1},n_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
,
n_{i}>k+5d_{i}/2
, and

r\in{\mathbb{N}},r>(d_{1}+d_{2}+n_{1}+n_{2})/2
.
We define

\omega({\bm{\lambda}}):=(1-\varphi({\bm{\lambda}}))|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{-2r}
.
Clearly,
