\displaystyle\leq cM_{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{**}(f;\varphi)({\mathbf{x}})^{1-\theta}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-k\tilde{{\varepsilon}}}\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-k},\theta{\bm{\gamma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}})\frac{|F_{{\mathbf{j}},k}({\mathbf{z}})|^{\theta}}{V({\mathbf{z}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})}d\mu({\mathbf{z}}).

By (
3.13
) it follows that

(7.26)

V({\mathbf{z}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-1}\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{-{\mathbf{j}}},{\mathbf{d}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}})^{-1}\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}},{\mathbf{d}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}})^{-1},

which applied in (
7.1
) leads to

\displaystyle|\varphi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\mathbf{t}},{\bm{\gamma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c[M_{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{**}(f;\varphi)({\mathbf{x}})]^{1-\theta}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-k\tilde{{\varepsilon}}}\int_{X}V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}}){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}},\theta{\bm{\gamma}}-{\mathbf{d}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}})|F_{{\mathbf{j}},k}({\mathbf{z}})|^{\theta}d\mu({\mathbf{z}})

\displaystyle\leq c[M_{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{**}(f;\varphi)({\mathbf{x}})]^{1-\theta}\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}2^{-\nu\epsilon}\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(F_{{\mathbf{j}},k})({\mathbf{x}})^{\theta}

\displaystyle\leq c[M_{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{**}(f;\varphi)({\mathbf{x}})]^{1-\theta}[\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(M(f;\varphi))({\mathbf{x}})]^{\theta},

where for the former inequality we used Lemma
4.23

and the fact that
\theta{\bm{\gamma}}-{\mathbf{d}}>{\mathbf{d}}
due to
{\bm{\gamma}}>(2/\theta){\mathbf{d}}
.
Therefore, using the definition of
M_{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{**}(f;\varphi)
(see (
7.7
)) we get

M_{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{**}(f;\varphi)(x)\leq c[M_{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{**}(f;\varphi)({\mathbf{x}})]^{1-\theta}[\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(M(f;\varphi))({\mathbf{x}})]^{\theta}.

At this point we use our assumption that
M_{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{**}(f;\varphi)({\mathbf{x}})<\infty
from (
7.19
) to obtain
the desired estimate (
7.18
).

Part 2.

Our next step is to show that under the hypothesis of the proposition
if
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and
M_{\theta}(M(f;\varphi))({\mathbf{x}})<\infty
,
then

M_{\bm{\gamma}}^{**}(f;\varphi)({\mathbf{x}})<\infty
,
i.e. (
7.19
) is valid.
As we know from Theorem
5.7
since
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, then
\varphi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})f
is slowly growing (and continuous).
More explicitly, there exist constants
K,c>0
depending on
f
such that

(7.27)

|\varphi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|\leq c\prod_{i=1,2}(t_{i}^{-K}+t_{i}^{K})(1+\rho(y_{i},x_{0i}))^{K},\quad\forall{\mathbf{y}}\in X,{\mathbf{t}}>{\bm{0}}.

With
K
from above and
0<{\varepsilon}<1
,
{\bm{\varepsilon}}:=({\varepsilon},{\varepsilon})
, we introduce the following auxiliary maximal operator

\displaystyle M_{\bm{\gamma}}^{**}

\displaystyle(f;\varphi)^{{\varepsilon},K}({\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle:=\sup_{{\bm{0}}<{\mathbf{t}}\leq\frac{{\bm{1}}}{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}}\sup_{{\mathbf{y}}\in X}\frac{|\varphi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|}{\prod_{i=1,2}(1+t_{i}^{-1}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{\gamma_{i}}(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{K}(1+{\varepsilon}t_{i}^{-K})}.

It is easy to see that

(7.28)

M_{\bm{\gamma}}^{**}(f;\varphi)^{{\varepsilon},K}({\mathbf{x}})\leq c({\mathbf{x}},{\varepsilon},K)<\infty.

Indeed, from (
7.27
) it follows that for
{\bm{0}}<{\mathbf{t}}\leq\frac{{\bm{1}}}{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}

\displaystyle\frac{|\varphi(t\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|}{\prod_{i=1,2}(1+t_{i}^{-1}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{\gamma_{i}}(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{K}(1+{\varepsilon}t_{i}^{-K})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{c\prod_{i=1,2}(t_{i}^{-K}+t_{i}^{K})(1+\rho(y_{i},x_{0i}))^{K}}{\prod_{i=1,2}(1+t_{i}^{-1}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{\gamma_{i}}(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{K}(1+{\varepsilon}t_{i}^{-K})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{c\prod_{i=1,2}(1+t_{i}^{2K})(1+\rho(y_{i},x_{i}))^{K}(1+\rho(x_{i},x_{0i}))^{K}}{{\varepsilon}^{2K+2}\prod_{i=1,2}(1+\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{K}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq c{\varepsilon}^{-2K-2}(1+{\varepsilon}^{-2K})^{2}\prod_{i=1,2}(1+\rho(x_{i},x_{0i}))^{K},

which confirms (
7.28
).

Our next goal is to establish the inequality

(7.29)

M_{\bm{\gamma}}^{**}(f;\varphi)^{{\varepsilon},K}({\mathbf{x}})\leq\tilde{c}\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(M(f;\varphi))({\mathbf{x}}),

where the constant
\tilde{c}=\tilde{c}(f)
is allowed to depend on
f
, but is independent of
{\varepsilon}
.
Then passing to the limit as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
it will follow that

M_{\bm{\gamma}}^{**}(f;\varphi)({\mathbf{x}})\leq\tilde{c}\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(M(f;\varphi))({\mathbf{x}})
,
which in turn shows that
(
7.19
) holds and the proof will be complete.

The proof of inequality (
7.29
) will follow very closely in the footsteps of the proof from Part 1.
For this reason we will only indicated the main differences and omit most of the details.

Just as in Part 1 we apply Lemma
7.6
but this time with

(7.30)

N:=\lfloor 3|{\bm{\gamma}}|+6K+3|{\mathbf{d}}|/2+4\rfloor,

where
K
is from (
7.27
).
Fix
0<{\varepsilon}\leq 1
.
Let
{\bm{0}}<{\mathbf{t}}\leq{\bm{1}}/{\bm{\varepsilon}}
and choose
{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
so that
2^{-{\mathbf{j}}}\leq{\mathbf{t}}<2^{-{\mathbf{j}}+{\bm{1}}}
.
Hence,
2^{\mathbf{j}}\geq{\bm{1}}/{\mathbf{t}}\geq{\bm{\varepsilon}}
.
By Lemma
7.6

there exist an admissible function
\psi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
with
\psi(0,0)=1

and a function
\Psi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
with the properties

\Psi(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=\Psi(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
for
{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and

\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}\Psi(0,0)=0
for
|{\bm{\beta}}|\leq N

such that
for any
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
identity (
7.14
) is valid.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{|\varphi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|}{\prod_{i=1,2}(1+t_{i}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{\gamma_{i}}(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{K}(1+{\varepsilon}t_{i}^{-K})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq c\frac{|\varphi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})\psi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}}\sqrt{L})\varphi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|}{\prod_{i=1,2}(1+2^{j_{i}}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{\gamma_{i}}(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{K}(1+{\varepsilon}t_{i}^{-K})}

\displaystyle\qquad+c\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{|\varphi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})\Psi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})[\varphi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})-\varphi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}+{\bm{1}}}\sqrt{L})]f(y)|}{\prod_{i=1,2}(1+2^{j_{i}}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{\gamma_{i}}(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{K}(1+{\varepsilon}t_{i}^{-K})}.

Let
\omega({\bm{\lambda}}):=\varphi({\mathbf{t}}2^{\mathbf{j}}{\bm{\lambda}})\Psi(2^{-{\mathbf{k}}}{\bm{\lambda}})
.
Then
\varphi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})\Psi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})=\omega(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}}\sqrt{L})
.
Choose
m:=\lfloor\gamma_{1}+K+d_{1}/2+1\rfloor+\lfloor\gamma_{2}+K+d_{2}/2+1\rfloor

and
r:=m+|{\mathbf{d}}|+1
.
Now just as in the derivation of (
7.22
) for some constant
\tilde{{\varepsilon}}>0

(7.31)

|\partial^{\bm{\beta}}\omega({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq c2^{-k(|{\bm{\gamma}}|+2K+\tilde{{\varepsilon}})}(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-r},\;\;{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\;\;|{\bm{\beta}}|\leq m.

Applying Theorem
4.21
to
\omega(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}}\sqrt{L})

with
\lfloor\gamma_{i}+K+d_{i}/2+1\rfloor
,
i=1,2
, playing the role of
k_{1},k_{2}

and using that
2^{j_{i}}\geq{\varepsilon}
,
i=1,2
, we get

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})\Psi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{z}})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c2^{-k(|{\bm{\gamma}}|+2K+\tilde{{\varepsilon}})}}{V({\mathbf{z}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+2^{j_{i}}\rho(y_{i},z_{i})\big)^{\gamma_{i}+K}}

(7.32)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c2^{-k(|{\bm{\gamma}}|+2K+\tilde{{\varepsilon}})}}{V({\mathbf{z}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+2^{j_{i}}\rho(y_{i},z_{i})\big)^{\gamma_{i}}\big(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(y_{i},z_{i})\big)^{K}},

This and the inequalities

1+2^{j_{i}}\rho(x_{i},z_{i})\leq(1+2^{j_{i}}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))(1+2^{j_{i}}\rho(y_{i},z_{i}))

and

1+{\varepsilon}\rho(x_{i},z_{i})\leq(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(y_{i},z_{i}))

lead to

\displaystyle\frac{|\varphi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})\Psi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})\varphi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|}{\prod_{i=1,2}(1+2^{j_{i}}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{\gamma_{i}}(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{K}(1+{\varepsilon}t_{i}^{-K})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c2^{-k(|{\bm{\gamma}}|+2K+\tilde{{\varepsilon}})}}{\prod_{i=1,2}(1+2^{j_{i}}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{\gamma_{i}}(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{K}(1+{\varepsilon}t_{i}^{-K})}

\displaystyle\hskip 79.49744pt\times\int_{X}\frac{|\varphi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{z}})|d\mu({\mathbf{z}})}{V({\mathbf{z}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+2^{j_{i}}\rho(y_{i},z_{i})\big)^{\gamma_{i}}\big(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(y_{i},z_{i})\big)^{K}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c2^{-k\tilde{{\varepsilon}}}}{\prod_{i=1,2}(1+2^{kK}{\varepsilon}2^{j_{i}K})}

\displaystyle\hskip 79.49744pt\times\int_{X}\frac{2^{-k|{\bm{\gamma}}|}|\varphi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{z}})|d\mu({\mathbf{z}})}{V({\mathbf{z}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+2^{j_{i}}\rho(x_{i},z_{i})\big)^{\gamma_{i}}\big(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(x_{i},z_{i})\big)^{K}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c2^{-k\tilde{{\varepsilon}}}}{\prod_{i=1,2}(1+{\varepsilon}2^{(j_{i}+k)K})}

\displaystyle\hskip 79.49744pt\times\int_{X}\frac{|\varphi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{z}})|d\mu({\mathbf{z}})}{V({\mathbf{z}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+2^{j_{i}+k}\rho(x_{i},z_{i})\big)^{\gamma_{i}}\big(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(x_{i},z_{i})\big)^{K}}

\displaystyle\leq c2^{-k\tilde{{\varepsilon}}}\sup_{{\mathbf{z}}\in X}\left[\frac{|\varphi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{z}})|}{\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+2^{j_{i}+k}\rho(x_{i},z_{i})\big)^{\gamma_{i}}\big(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(x_{i},z_{i})\big)^{K}(1+{\varepsilon}2^{(j_{i}+k)K})}\right]^{1-\theta}

\displaystyle\hskip 79.49744pt\times\int_{X}\frac{|\varphi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{z}})|^{\theta}d\mu({\mathbf{z}})}{V({\mathbf{z}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+2^{j_{i}}\rho(x_{i},z_{i})\big)^{\theta\gamma_{i}}}

\displaystyle\leq c2^{-k\tilde{{\varepsilon}}}[M_{\bm{\gamma}}^{**}(f;\varphi)^{{\varepsilon},K}(x)]^{1-\theta}[\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(|\varphi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})f|)({\mathbf{x}})]^{\theta}.

\displaystyle\leq c2^{-k\tilde{{\varepsilon}}}[M_{\bm{\gamma}}^{**}(f;\varphi)^{{\varepsilon},K}({\mathbf{x}})]^{1-\theta}[\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(M(f;\varphi))({\mathbf{x}})]^{\theta}.

Here for the last two inequalities we proceeded as in Part 1,
i.e. we used (
7.26
), Lemma
4.23
and the fact that
{\bm{\gamma}}>(2/\theta){\mathbf{d}}
.

We carry out the rest of the proof of (
7.29
) just as in Part 1,
namely, we derive the estimate

M_{\bm{\gamma}}^{**}(f;\varphi)^{{\varepsilon},K}({\mathbf{x}})\leq c[M_{\bm{\gamma}}^{**}(f;\varphi)^{{\varepsilon},K}({\mathbf{x}})]^{1-\theta}[\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(M(f;\varphi))({\mathbf{x}})]^{\theta}

and using (
7.28
) we arrive at (
7.29
).
We omit the further details.

Observe that the constant
\tilde{c}
in (
7.29
) depends on
f
because it depends on
K
,
which in turn depends on
f
.

As was already alluded to above passing to the limit as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
in inequality (
7.29
)
leads to the inequality

(7.33)

M_{\bm{\gamma}}^{**}(f;\varphi)({\mathbf{x}})\leq\tilde{c}\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(M(f;\varphi))({\mathbf{x}}).

This step, however, needs some clarification.
We set

F({\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{y}},{\varepsilon}):=\frac{|\varphi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|}{\prod_{i=1,2}(1+t_{i}^{-1}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{\gamma_{i}}(1+{\varepsilon}\rho(x_{i},y_{i}))^{K}(1+{\varepsilon}t_{i}^{-K})}

and

A:=\tilde{c}\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(M(f;\varphi))({\mathbf{x}})
.
Then

M_{\bm{\gamma}}^{**}(f;\varphi)^{{\varepsilon},K}({\mathbf{x}})=\sup_{{\bm{0}}<{\mathbf{t}}\leq\frac{{\bm{1}}}{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}}\sup_{{\mathbf{y}}\in X}F({\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{y}},{\varepsilon})\;\hbox{and}\;M_{\bm{\gamma}}^{**}(f;\varphi)({\mathbf{x}})=\sup_{{\mathbf{t}}>{\bm{0}}}\sup_{{\mathbf{y}}\in X}F({\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{y}},0).

Now, inequality (
7.29
) can be written as

(7.34)

\sup_{{\bm{0}}<{\mathbf{t}}\leq\frac{{\bm{1}}}{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}}\sup_{{\mathbf{y}}\in X}F({\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{y}},{\varepsilon})\leq A.

Denote
\Omega_{n}:=\{({\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{y}}):{\mathbf{t}}\in[1/n,n]^{2},\rho({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\leq n\}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Then from (
7.34
) it follows that for
{\varepsilon}\leq n
we have

(7.35)

\sup_{({\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{y}})\in\Omega_{n}}F({\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{y}},{\varepsilon})\leq A.

Clearly,
\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}F({\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{y}},{\varepsilon})=F({\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{y}},0)
.
Moreover, in light of (
7.27
) this convergence is uniform on
\Omega_{n}
, that is,

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\sup_{({\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{y}})\in\Omega_{n}}|F({\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{y}},{\varepsilon})-F({\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{y}},0)|=0.

From this and (
7.35
) it follows that

\sup_{({\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{y}})\in\Omega_{n}}F({\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{y}},0)\leq A.
Using this and the fact that
(0,\infty)^{2}\times X=\cup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\Omega_{n}

we arrive at (
7.33
).
The proof of Proposition
7.7
is complete.
∎

A variation of the proof of Proposition
7.7
will give us another important ingredient for the proof of Theorem
7.5
.

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
be admissible and
0<\theta\leq 1
. Let also
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
be so that
N>6|{\mathbf{d}}|/\theta+3|{\mathbf{d}}|/2+3
.
Then there exists a constant
c>0
such that

(7.36)

\mathscr{M}_{N}(f)({\mathbf{x}})\leq c\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(M(f;\varphi))({\mathbf{x}}),\quad\text{for}\;f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\;{\mathbf{x}}\in X.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
be admissible.
Fix
\phi\in\mathcal{F}_{N}
, where as in the hypothesis of the proposition

N>6|{\mathbf{d}}|/\theta+3|{\mathbf{d}}|/2+3
,
0<\theta\leq 1
.
It is readily seen that
M_{{\bm{1}}}^{*}(f;\phi)({\mathbf{x}})\leq 2^{|{\bm{\gamma}}|}M_{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{**}(f;\phi)({\mathbf{x}})
.
Therefore, in order to establish (
7.36
) it suffices to prove that for some
{\bm{\gamma}}>{\bm{0}}

(7.37)

M_{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{**}(f;\phi)({\mathbf{x}})\leq c\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(M(f;\varphi))({\mathbf{x}}),

where
c>0
is a constant independent of
\phi
.
We choose
{\bm{\gamma}}
so that
{\bm{\gamma}}>(2/\theta){\mathbf{d}}
and
N>3|{\bm{\gamma}}|+3|{\mathbf{d}}|/2+3
.

Fix
{\mathbf{t}}>{\bm{0}}
and let
{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, be such that
2^{-{\mathbf{j}}}\leq{\mathbf{t}}<2^{-{\mathbf{j}}+1}
.
Using (
7.20
) we get

\displaystyle|\phi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\mathbf{t}},{\bm{\gamma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\leq c|\phi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})\psi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}}\sqrt{L})\varphi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{-{\mathbf{j}}},{\bm{\gamma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle+c\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|\phi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})\Psi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-k}\sqrt{L})(\varphi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})-\varphi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-{\mathbf{k}}+{\bm{1}}}\sqrt{L}))f({\mathbf{y}})|{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{-{\mathbf{j}}},{\bm{\gamma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

Just as in the proof of Proposition
7.7
(see (
7.23
)) we get

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{K}}_{\phi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})\Psi(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}-k}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|

\displaystyle\leq c2^{-k(|{\bm{\gamma}}|+\varepsilon)}V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{-{\mathbf{j}}},{\bm{\gamma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}),

but with the constant
c>0
independent of
\phi
due to
\mathcal{N}_{N}(\phi)\leq 1
.

Just as in the proof of Proposition
7.7
and using (
7.18
) we get

\displaystyle|\phi({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\mathbf{t}},{\bm{\gamma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq cM_{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{**}(f;\varphi)({\mathbf{x}})^{1-\theta}\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(M(f;\varphi))({\mathbf{x}})^{\theta}

\displaystyle\leq c\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(M(f;\varphi))({\mathbf{x}}),

which implies (
7.37
) and completes the proof.
∎

We are now prepared to prove Theorem
7.5
.

Proof of Theorem
7.5
.

Let
\Phi_{0}({\bm{\lambda}}):=e^{-|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{2}}
. Clearly,
\Phi_{0}
is admissible and
\Phi({\bm{0}})=1
.
Let
0<p\leq 1
and assume
N>6|{\mathbf{d}}|/p+3|{\mathbf{d}}|/2+3
.
We choose
\theta
so that
0<\theta<p
and
N>6|{\mathbf{d}}|/\theta+3|{\mathbf{d}}|/2+3
.
Now, we apply Proposition
7.8
and use the maximal inequality (
4.104
) to obtain

\|\mathscr{M}_{N}f\|_{L^{p}}\leq c\|\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(M(f;\Phi_{0}))\|_{L^{p}}\leq c\|M(f;\Phi_{0})\|_{L^{p}}=c\|f\|_{H^{p}}.

In the other direction we use inequalities (
7.8
) and (
7.12
) and get

\|f\|_{H^{p}}=\|M(f;\Phi_{0})\|_{L^{p}}\leq\|M_{{\bm{1}}}^{*}(f;\Phi_{0})\|_{L^{p}}\leq c\|\mathscr{M}_{N}f\|_{L^{p}}.

Similarly as above we obtain
\|\mathscr{M}_{N}f\|_{p}\sim\|M(f;\varphi)\|_{p}
,
with the equivalence constants depending in addition on the admissible function
\varphi
.

Let
{\bm{\gamma}}>(2/p){\mathbf{d}}
. This time we choose
\theta
so that
0<\theta<p
and
{\bm{\gamma}}>(2/\theta){\mathbf{d}}
.
Applying Proposition
7.7
and the maximal inequality (
4.104
) we obtain

\|M_{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{**}(f;\varphi)\|_{p}\leq c\|\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(M(f;\varphi))\|_{p}\leq c\|M(f;\varphi)\|_{p}.

The remaining estimates in (
7.13
) follow using (
7.8
) and (
7.12
).
∎

7.2.
Product Hardy spaces
H^{p}
when
p>1

We close this section by establishing the well expected coincidence between product Hardy and Lebesgue spaces when
p>1
.

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
1<p<\infty
. Then
H^{p}=L^{p}
with equivalent norms.

Proof.

We carry out this proof similarly as in the classical case on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

(a) We first show that
L^{p}\subset H^{p}
.
Let
f\in L^{p}
,
p>1
.
By Lemma
4.23
, applied with
\varphi({\bm{\lambda}})=\Phi_{0}({\bm{\lambda}}):=e^{-|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{2}}
, we get

|\Phi_{0}({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}})|\leq c\mathcal{M}_{1}(f)({\mathbf{x}})\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;M(f;\Phi_{0})({\mathbf{x}})\leq c\mathcal{M}_{1}f(x),\;\;{\mathbf{x}}\in X.

Now, applying the maximal inequality (
4.104
) we obtain

\|f\|_{H^{p}}=\|M(f;\Phi_{0})\|_{L^{p}}\leq c\|\mathcal{M}_{1}f\|_{L^{p}}\leq c\|f\|_{L^{p}}.

Hence,
f\in H^{p}
.

(b) For the other direction, assume
f\in H^{p}
,
p>1
.
Then by Definition
7.2

\big\|\sup_{{\mathbf{t}}>{\bm{0}}}|\Phi_{0}({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)|\big\|_{L^{p}}=\|f\|_{H^{p}}<\infty.

Hence, the sequence
\{\Phi_{0}((\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{n})\sqrt{L})f\}_{n\geq 1}
is bounded on
L^{p}
.
By the Banach-Alaoglu theorem there exists a subsequence
\{n_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
and a function
f_{0}\in L^{p}
such that

\Phi_{0}\Big(\Big(\frac{1}{n_{k}},\frac{1}{n_{k}}\Big)\sqrt{L}\Big)f\rightarrow f_{0}

in the weak
∗
topology of
L^{p}
.
But by Theorem
5.10

\Phi_{0}\Big(\Big(\frac{1}{n_{k}},\frac{1}{n_{k}}\Big)\sqrt{L}\Big)f\rightarrow f,\quad\text{in}\;\;\mathcal{S}^{\prime}.

Therefore,
f=f_{0}\in L^{p}
.
Further, by Theorem
5.10
it follows that

\|\Phi_{0}({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})f-f\|_{L^{p}}\to 0\quad\hbox{as}\quad{\mathbf{t}}\to{\bm{0}}

and hence

\|f\|_{L^{p}}\leq\big\|\sup_{{\mathbf{t}}>{\bm{0}}}|\Phi_{0}({\mathbf{t}}\sqrt{L})f|\big\|_{L^{p}}=\|f\|_{H^{p}}.

The proof is complete.
∎

7.3.
Open problem

The atomic decomposition of
H^{p}
plays an important role in the theory of Hardy spaces.
The atomic decomposition of
H^{p}
in the classical setting on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
in dimension
n=1
was first established by R. Coifman
[
10
]

and by R. Latter
[
43
]
in dimensions
n>1
.
For more details, see
[
47
]
.
Atomic decompositions of Hardy spaces are also available in nonclassical settings, see e.g.
[
1
]
.
Closely related to our development in the present section is the atomic decomposition of the Hardy spaces
H^{p}

in the general setting of a metric measure space with the doubling property
and in the presence of a non-negative self-adjoint operator whose heat kernel has Gaussian localization
and the Markov property established in
[
15
]
.

The situation is more complicated in the two-parameter case on product spaces.
An atomic decomposition of the
H^{p}
spaces in the two-parameter classical setting has been obtained by
Sun-Yung Chang and R. Fefferman, see
[
6
,
7
,
8
]
.
It is an open problem to obtain an atomic decomposition of the product Hardy spaces
H^{p}
considered in this section.

8.
Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces with dominating mixed smoothness

The purpose of this section is to develop the basic theory
of Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces with dominating mixed smoothness on product domains
X_{1}\times X_{2}

associated with operators
L_{1},L_{2}
in the setting of this article.
The theory of Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces with dominating mixed smoothness in the classical case
on
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
has been mainly developed by H.-J. Schmeisser and H. Triebel
[
49
]
,
see also
[
48
,
54
]
and the references therein.
In
[
48
,
49
,
54
]
these spaces are denoted by

S_{pq}^{r_{1},r_{2}}B(\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
S_{pq}^{r_{1},r_{2}}F(\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})

and the respective norms by

\|f\,|\,S_{pq}^{r_{1},r_{2}}B(\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})\|
and

\|f\,|\,S_{pq}^{r_{1},r_{2}}F(\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})\|
.
We will use the following more compact notation for the analogue of these spaces
on product domains in the setting of this article:

\mathscr{B}_{pq}^{\mathbf{s}}
,
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{pq}^{\mathbf{s}}
,
\mathscr{F}_{pq}^{\mathbf{s}}
, and
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{pq}^{\mathbf{s}}
.
For short we will call them
mixed-smoothness B and F-spaces
.
In the previous sections we have developed all necessary tools for this theory.

The basics of the theory of ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces associated
with the operators
L_{1},L_{2}
in the setting of this article
will be rapidly developed in the next section.
It is useful for better understanding
of the Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces with dominating mixed smoothness.

8.1.
Definition of mixed-smoothness Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces

To deal with possible anisotropic geometries of the coordinate spaces
X_{1},X_{2}

we introduce two types of
mixed-smoothness Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces in the setting of this article:
(i)
Classical mixed-smoothness
B-spaces
\mathscr{B}_{pq}^{s}=\mathscr{B}_{pq}^{s}(L)
and F-spaces
\mathscr{F}_{pq}^{s}=\mathscr{F}_{pq}^{s}(L)
,
and
(ii)
Nonclassical mixed-smoothness
B-spaces
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{pq}^{s}=\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{pq}^{s}(L)
and F-spaces
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{pq}^{s}=\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{pq}^{s}(L)
.
To define these spaces we introduce two pairs of functions

\varphi^{i}_{0},\varphi^{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
i=1,2
, satisfying the conditions:

(8.1)

\displaystyle(i)\ \hbox{$\varphi^{i}_{0},\varphi^{i}$ are real-valued and even,}

\displaystyle(ii)\ \operatorname{supp}\varphi^{i}_{0}\subset[-2,2],\ |\varphi^{i}_{0}(t)|\geq\hat{c}_{i}>0\hbox{ for }t\in[-5/3,5/3],

\displaystyle(iii)\ \operatorname{supp}\varphi^{i}\subset[-2,2]\setminus[-1/2,1/2],\ |\varphi^{i}(t)|\geq\hat{c}_{i}>0\;\hbox{ for }t\in[3/5,5/3].

As before we will use the notation
\varphi^{i}_{n}(t):=\varphi^{i}(2^{-n}t),n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Then from (
8.1
) it follows that

(8.2)

\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}|\varphi^{i}_{n}(t)|\geq\hat{c}_{i}>0,\quad t\in\mathbb{R}.

Further, for each
{\mathbf{j}}=(j_{1},j_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}_{0}
we set
\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}:=\varphi^{1}_{j_{1}}\otimes\varphi^{2}_{j_{2}}
.
Then from above it follow that

\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}|\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}})|=\prod_{i=1,2}\sum_{j_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}|\varphi^{i}_{j_{i}}(\lambda_{i})|\geq\hat{c}_{1}\hat{c}_{2}>0,\quad{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{s}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
0<p,q\leq\infty
.

(i)
The classical mixed-smoothness Besov space
\mathscr{B}_{pq}^{{\mathbf{s}}}=\mathscr{B}_{pq}^{{\mathbf{s}}}(L)

is defined as the set of all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
such that

(8.3)

\|f\|_{\mathscr{B}_{pq}^{{\mathbf{s}}}}:=\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\Big(2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{s}}}\|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\|_{p}\Big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}<\infty.

(ii)
The non-classical mixed-smoothness Besov space
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{pq}^{{\mathbf{s}}}=\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{pq}^{{\mathbf{s}}}(L)
is defined as the set
of all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
such that

(8.4)

\|f\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{pq}^{{\mathbf{s}}}}:=\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\Big(\big\|V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big\|_{p}\Big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}<\infty.

Above the
\ell^{q}
-norm is replaced by the sup-norm if
q=\infty
.

Definition 8.2
.

Let
{\mathbf{s}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
0<p<\infty
and
0<q\leq\infty
.

(i)
The classical mixed-smoothness Triebel-Lizorkin space
\mathscr{F}_{pq}^{{\mathbf{s}}}=\mathscr{F}_{pq}^{{\mathbf{s}}}(L)

is defined as the set of all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
such that

(8.5)

\|f\|_{\mathscr{F}_{pq}^{\mathbf{s}}}:=\Big\|\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\Big(2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{s}}}|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)|\Big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}<\infty.

(ii)
The non-classical mixed-smoothness Triebel-Lizorkin space

\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{pq}^{s}=\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{pq}^{s}(L)
is defined as the set of all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}

such that

(8.6)

\|f\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{pq}^{\mathbf{s}}}:=\Big\|\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\Big(V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)|\Big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}<\infty.

As before above the
\ell^{q}
-norm is replaced by the sup-norm if
q=\infty
.

Note that the mixed-smoothness B and F-spaces are quasi-Banach spaces (Banach spaces if
p,q\geq 1
). Moreover, as is expected, the above spaces
are independent of the selection of the pair of functions
\varphi^{i}_{0},\varphi^{i},i=1,2
,
used in their definition. We establish this in the following proposition.

Proposition 8.3
.

Let the functions
\{\varphi^{i}_{0},\varphi^{i}\}
and
\{\Phi^{i}_{0},\Phi^{i}\}
,
i=1,2
,
be just as in Definitions
8.1
,
8.2
, see (
8.1
).
Then the corresponding spaces
\mathscr{B}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\varphi)
and
\mathscr{B}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\Phi)
are the same
with equivalent quasi-norms:
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{B}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\varphi)}\sim\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{B}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\Phi)}
.
The same holds true for the spaces
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq},\mathscr{F}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}
, and
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}
.

Proof.

We will only prove the above statement for the spaces
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}
.

Claim 1:

\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\varphi)=\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\Phi)
and

\|\cdot\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\varphi)}\sim\|\cdot\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\Phi)}
.
As is well known (e.g.
[
27
]
) there exist functions

\Psi^{i}_{0},\Psi^{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
i=1,2
, satisfying (
8.1
) such that

\Phi^{i}_{0}(t)\Psi^{i}_{0}(t)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Phi^{i}(2^{-n}t)\Psi^{i}(2^{-n}t)=1,\quad t\in\mathbb{R}.

As before for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we denote

\Phi^{i}_{n}(t):=\Phi_{i}(2^{-n}t)
and
\Psi^{i}_{n}(t):=\Psi^{i}(2^{-n}t)
,
i=1,2
.
Also, for
{\mathbf{j}}=(j_{1},j_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}_{0}
we set

\Phi_{\mathbf{j}}:=\Phi^{1}_{j_{1}}\otimes\Phi^{2}_{j_{2}}
and
\Psi_{\mathbf{j}}:=\Psi^{1}_{j_{1}}\otimes\Psi^{2}_{j_{2}}
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. By Corollary
5.9
we have the decomposition

f=\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\Psi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\Phi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\quad\text{in }\mathcal{S}^{\prime},

where we used that for any
{\mathbf{j}}=(j_{1},j_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
,

(\Phi^{1}_{j_{1}}(\sqrt{L_{1}})\Psi^{1}_{j_{1}}(\sqrt{L_{1}}))\otimes(\Phi^{2}_{j_{2}}(\sqrt{L_{2}})\Psi^{2}_{j_{2}}(\sqrt{L_{2}}))=\Phi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\Psi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L}).

Let
{\mathbf{j}}=(j_{1},j_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
. From the support conditions of these spectral multipliers
it follows that

(8.7)

\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f=\sum_{k_{2}=j_{2}-1}^{j_{2}+1}\sum_{k_{1}=j_{1}-1}^{j_{1}+1}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\Psi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f,

where by definition
\Phi^{i}_{-1}=\Psi^{i}_{-1}\equiv 0
.

Fix
0<r<\min\{p,q\}
and choose

\sigma_{i}:=2d_{i}+|s_{i}|+(2d_{i}+1)/r
,
i=1,2
.
Denote by
{\mathscr{K}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
the kernel of the operator
\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\Psi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})
.
From Theorem
4.21
we know that for any
{\bm{\sigma}}>0

there exists a constant
c=c_{\bm{\sigma}}>0
such that

|{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}})|\leq c{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{j}}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}})\quad\text{and}\quad|{\mathscr{K}}_{\Psi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{y}}).

Using (
3.12
) (taking into account that
j_{i}\sim k_{i}
), (
3.21
) (
{\bm{\sigma}}>2{\mathbf{d}}
), and (
3.14
) we get

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{K}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{X}|{\mathscr{K}}_{\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}})||{\mathscr{K}}_{\Psi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{y}})|d\mu({\mathbf{z}})

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}}){\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{z}})

\displaystyle\leq c{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\sigma}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

Hence,

\displaystyle|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\Psi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}})|\leq\int_{X}|{\mathscr{K}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})||\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-1}\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\sigma}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|d\mu({\mathbf{y}}).

We now use (
3.17
), (
3.12
), and the fact that
j_{i}\sim k_{i}
to obtain

\displaystyle V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\sigma}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq cV({\mathbf{y}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},\tilde{{\bm{\sigma}}}}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle=cV({\mathbf{y}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\tau}}/r}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}){\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\eta}}}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}),

where
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}:=\sigma_{i}-d_{i}/2-|s_{i}|=(2d_{i}+1)/r+3d_{i}/2

and
\tau_{i}:=2d_{i}+1
,
\eta_{i}:=3d_{i}/2
.
Therefore,

(8.8)

\displaystyle V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\Psi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}})|

\displaystyle\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-1}\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\tau}}/r}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}){\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})V({\mathbf{y}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}|\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c\sup_{{\mathbf{y}}\in X}V({\mathbf{y}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}|\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\tau}}/r}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\int_{X}\frac{{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\eta}}}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})}{V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})}d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c\sup_{{\mathbf{y}}\in X}V({\mathbf{y}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}|\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\tau}}/r}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}),

where for the last inequality we used (
3.19
) (
{\bm{\eta}}>{\mathbf{d}}
).
Because
\operatorname{supp}\Phi_{k}\subset[-2^{k-1},2^{k+1}]
Proposition
6.3
implies that

\Phi_{k}(\sqrt{L})f\in\Sigma_{2^{{\mathbf{k}}+{\bm{1}}}}
and
hence by Theorem
6.4
we get

(8.9)

\displaystyle\sup_{{\mathbf{y}}\in X}V({\mathbf{y}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}

\displaystyle|\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\tau}}/r}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c\mathcal{M}_{r}\big(V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big)({\mathbf{x}}).

Combining (
8.7
)–(
8.9
) we arrive at

(8.10)

\displaystyle V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-j})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}})|

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{k_{2}=j_{2}-1}^{j_{2}+1}\sum_{k_{1}=j_{1}-1}^{j_{1}+1}\mathcal{M}_{r}\big(V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big)({\mathbf{x}}).

We use now the definition of the
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}
-norm and the maximal inequality (
4.104
) to obtain

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\varphi)}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\Big(V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)|\Big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq c\Big\|\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\Big(\sum_{k_{2}=j_{2}-1}^{j_{2}+1}\sum_{k_{1}=j_{1}-1}^{j_{1}+1}\mathcal{M}_{r}\big(V(\cdot,2^{-k})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)|\big)\Big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq c\Big\|\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\sum_{k_{2}=j_{2}-1}^{j_{2}+1}\sum_{k_{1}=j_{1}-1}^{j_{1}+1}\Big[\mathcal{M}_{r}\big(V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)|\big)\Big]^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq c\Big\|\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\Big[\mathcal{M}_{r}\big(V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)|\big)\Big]^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq c\Big\|\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\Big(V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)|\Big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle=c\|f\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{s}_{pq}(\Phi)}.

To prove the inequality in the opposite direction we simply interchange the roles of
the
\varphi
’s and
\Phi
’s above. This completes the proof.

Claim 2:

\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\varphi)=\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\Phi)
and

\|\cdot\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\varphi)}\sim\|\cdot\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\Phi)}
.
The proof follows in the footsteps of the proof of the previous claim.
Assuming that
0<r<p
and applying the maximal inequality (
4.104
)
(for a single function) to (
8.10
) we get that

\displaystyle\|V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{k_{2}=j_{2}-1}^{j_{2}+1}\sum_{k_{1}=j_{1}-1}^{j_{1}+1}\big\|\mathcal{M}_{r}\big(V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big)\big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{k_{2}=j_{2}-1}^{j_{2}+1}\sum_{k_{1}=j_{1}-1}^{j_{1}+1}\big\|V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big)\big\|_{p}.

Finally, taking the
\ell_{q}
-norm we get

\|f\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\varphi)}\leq c\|f\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}(\Phi)}.

For the inequality in the other direction we switch the roles of the
\varphi
’s and
\Phi
’s.
∎

8.2.
Embeddings

The purpose of this subsection is to establish embedding results that involve
mixed-smoothness Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces and other important classes.
Recall that a quasi-normed space
X
is continuously embedded in the quasi-normed space
Y
(
X\hookrightarrow Y
)
if
X\subset Y
and there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\|f\|_{Y}\leq c\|f\|_{X}
for all
f\in X
.

8.2.1.
Embeddings between B and F-spaces and test functions or distributions

We start with the following:

Theorem 8.4
.

Let
{\mathbf{s}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
0<q\leq\infty
. If
0<p\leq\infty
then

\mathcal{S}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{B}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq},\;\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime},

and, if
0<p<\infty
, then

\mathcal{S}\hookrightarrow\;\mathscr{F}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq},\;\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}.

Proof.

We will establish only the embeddings involving the
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}
spaces.
The proofs of the other embeddings are similar; we omit them.

Claim 1:

\mathcal{S}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}
.
We need to show that there exist constants
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
c>0
(depending on
{\mathbf{s}},p,q
) such that

(8.11)

\|\phi\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}}\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi),\quad\forall\phi\in\mathcal{S}.

In light of the definition of the mixed-smoothness Besov spaces it suffices to prove that
there exist
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,
c>0
, and
{\bm{\varepsilon}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}
such that for all
{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}

(8.12)

\big\|V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi(\cdot)\big\|_{p}\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi)2^{-{\bm{\varepsilon}}\cdot{\mathbf{j}}},\quad\forall\phi\in\mathcal{S}.

Assume
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
and let
{\mathbf{j}}=(j_{1},j_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
.
The proof below still works with minor changes in the case when
j_{1}=0
or
j_{2}=0
; we omit it.
Choose

(8.13)

k>\max\{d_{1}(1+1/p)+|s_{1}|,d_{2}(1+1/p)+|s_{2}|\},\quad m>\max\{|s_{1}|+d_{1},|s_{2}|+d_{2}\}/2

and set
{\mathbf{m}}:=(m,m)
.
We define

\omega(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=\lambda_{1}^{-2m}\lambda_{2}^{-2m}\varphi^{1}(\lambda_{1})\varphi^{2}(\lambda_{2})
.
Clearly,

\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})=2^{-2{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{m}}}\omega(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}}\sqrt{L})L^{{\mathbf{m}}}
.
From Theorem
4.21
and (
3.14
) it follows that for any
{\bm{\sigma}}>0

there exists a constant
c_{\bm{\sigma}}>0
such that

(8.14)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{\omega(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c_{\bm{\sigma}}V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{-{\mathbf{j}}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

We choose
{\bm{\sigma}}={\mathbf{k}}:=(k,k)
.
On the other hand, because
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
we have

(8.15)

|L^{{\mathbf{m}}}\phi({\mathbf{y}})|\leq\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi)\prod_{i=1,2}(1+\rho_{i}(y_{i},x_{0i}))^{-k}=\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0}).

Also, from (
3.12
) it follows that

V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-1}\leq c2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{d}}}V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{1}})^{-1}
.
This along with (
8.14
) and (
8.15
) yield

\displaystyle|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi({\mathbf{x}})|

\displaystyle\leq c2^{-2{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{m}}}\int_{X}|{\mathscr{K}}_{\omega(2^{-{\mathbf{j}}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})||L^{{\mathbf{m}}}\phi({\mathbf{y}})|d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

(8.16)

\displaystyle\leq c2^{-2{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{m}}}\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi)V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-1}\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{-{\mathbf{j}}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c2^{-2{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{m}}+{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{d}}}\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi)V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{1}})^{-1}\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c2^{-2{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{m}}+{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{d}}}\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},{\mathbf{k}}-{\mathbf{d}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0}),

where for the last inequality we used (
3.20
) (
{\mathbf{k}}>{\mathbf{d}}
).

We next estimate
V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-j})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}
using (
3.12
) and (
3.17
).
We obtain

\displaystyle V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-j})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}

\displaystyle\leq c2^{j_{1}|s_{1}|+j_{2}|s_{2}|}V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{1}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}

\displaystyle\leq c2^{j_{1}|s_{1}|+j_{2}|s_{2}|}V({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\bm{1}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{|s_{i}|}.

Combining this with (
8.2.1
) we arrive at

\displaystyle V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-j})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi({\mathbf{x}})|

\displaystyle\leq c2^{-j_{1}{\varepsilon}_{1}-j_{2}{\varepsilon}_{2}}V({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\bm{1}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},{\mathbf{k}}-{\mathbf{d}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0})\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{|s_{i}|}

\displaystyle=c2^{-j_{1}{\varepsilon}_{1}-j_{2}{\varepsilon}_{2}}V({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\bm{1}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi)\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{-k+d_{i}+|s_{i}|},

where
{\varepsilon}_{i}=2m-|s_{i}|-d_{i}>0
,
i=1,2
(cf. (
8.13
)).
We now take the
L^{p}
norm and use (
3.19
) and the fact that

k>d_{i}+|s_{i}|+d_{i}/p
(cf. (
8.13
))
to obtain

\displaystyle\|V(\cdot,2^{-j})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi(\cdot)|\|_{p}\leq c2^{-j_{1}{\varepsilon}_{1}-j_{2}{\varepsilon}_{2}}V({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\bm{1}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}+{\bm{1}}/p}\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi),

which confirms (
8.12
) and completes the proof of Claim 1.

Claim 2:

\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{s}_{pq}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
This claim essentially will follow by duality
employing the embedding
\mathcal{S}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{s}_{pq}
just proved.
We need to show that there exist constants
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
c>0
(depending on
{\mathbf{s}},p,q
)
such that

(8.17)

|\langle f,\phi\rangle|\leq c\|f\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{s}_{pq}}\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi),\quad\forall f\in\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{s}_{pq},\;\phi\in\mathcal{S}.

Assume
f\in\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{s}_{pq}
.
Let
\varphi^{i}_{0},\varphi^{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
,
i=1,2
,
be functions as in Definitions
8.1
,
8.2
,
i.e. satisfying (
8.1
), with the additional assumption that they satisfy the condition

(\varphi_{0}^{i})^{2}(t)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(\varphi^{i})^{2}(2^{-n}t)=1,\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\;i=1,2.

For each
{\mathbf{j}}=(j_{1},j_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}_{0}
we set
\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}:=\varphi^{1}_{j_{1}}\otimes\varphi^{2}_{j_{2}}
.
From Corollary
5.9
it follows that

(8.18)

f=\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}^{2}(\sqrt{L})f\quad\text{in }\mathcal{S}^{\prime},

and hence for any
\phi\in\mathcal{S}

(8.19)

\langle f,\phi\rangle=\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\langle\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}^{2}(\sqrt{L})f,\phi\rangle=\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\langle\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f,\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi\rangle.

Therefore, in order to prove (
8.17
)
it suffices to show that there exist constants
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
c>0
, end
{\bm{\varepsilon}}=({\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
such that

(8.20)

|\langle\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f,\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi\rangle|\leq c\|f\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}}\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi)2^{-{\bm{\varepsilon}}\cdot{\mathbf{j}}},\quad\forall\phi\in\mathcal{S},\;{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}.

Without loss of generality we may assume that
j\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
. We further distinguish two cases.

Case 1:

1\leq p\leq\infty
.
We will employ (
8.12
) with
{\mathbf{s}}
replaced by
-{\mathbf{s}}

and
p
replaced by
p^{\prime}
(
1/p+1/p^{\prime}=1
).
In light of (
8.13
) we choose

k>\max\{d_{1}(2-1/p)+|s_{1}|,d_{2}(2-1/p)+|s_{2}|\},\quad m>\max\{|s_{1}|+d_{1},|s_{2}|+d_{2}\}/2.

Then using Hölder’s inequality, Definition
8.1
, and (
8.12
) we get

\displaystyle|\langle\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f,\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\big\|V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big\|_{p}\big\|V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi(\cdot)\big\|_{p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq c\|f\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}}\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi)2^{-{\bm{\varepsilon}}\cdot{\mathbf{j}}},

where
{\varepsilon}_{i}:=2m-|s_{i}|-d_{i}>0
,
i=1,2
.
This confirms (
8.20
).

Case 2:

0<p<1
.
Let
\gamma_{i}:=s_{i}/d_{i}-1/p+1
,
i=1,2
.
By Proposition
6.3
it readily follows that

\varphi_{j}(\sqrt{L})f\in\Sigma_{2^{{\mathbf{j}}+{\bm{1}}}}

and hence using Theorem
6.5
and Definition
8.1
we get

(8.21)

\big\|V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\bm{\gamma}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big\|_{1}\leq c\big\|V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big\|_{p}\leq c\|f\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}}.

On the other hand, we have already shown that (
8.12
)
is valid with
k,m
from (
8.13
) and
{\varepsilon}_{i}=2m-|s_{i}|-d_{i}
,
i=1,2
.
Hence, applying (
8.12
) with

\tilde{s}_{i}:=-s_{i}+(1/p-1)d_{i}
in the place if
s_{i}
,
i=1,2
, and
p=\infty

we obtain

(8.22)

\big\|V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{{\bm{\gamma}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big\|_{\infty}\leq\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi)2^{-\tilde{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}\cdot{\mathbf{j}}},

where

k>\max\{d_{1}+|\tilde{s}_{1}|,d_{2}+|\tilde{s}_{2}|\},\;m>\max\{|\tilde{s}_{1}|+d_{1},|\tilde{s}_{2}|+d_{2}\}/2,\;\tilde{{\varepsilon}}_{i}:=2m-|\tilde{s}_{i}|-d_{i}>0.

The above selection of
\tilde{s}_{i}
is justified by the identity

-\tilde{s}/{\mathbf{d}}={\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}+(1-1/p){\bm{1}}
.
Finally, we use (
8.21
) and (
8.22
) to obtain for any
\phi\in\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle|\langle\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f,\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\big\|V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\bm{\gamma}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big\|_{1}\big\|V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{{\bm{\gamma}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi(\cdot)\big\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq c\|f\|_{\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}}\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi)2^{-\tilde{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}\cdot{\mathbf{j}}},

which confirms (
8.20
).
∎

8.2.2.
Embeddings between mixed-smoothness B-spaces with different parameters

From Definition
8.1
it follows that
if
{\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
s_{i}\geq s^{\prime}_{i}
,
i=1,2
,
0<p\leq\infty
and
0<q\leq r\leq\infty
,
then
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{B}^{{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}}_{pr}}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{B}^{{\mathbf{s}}}_{pq}}
,
i.e.
\mathscr{B}^{{\mathbf{s}}}_{pq}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{B}^{{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}}_{pr}
.

As a consequence of Theorem
6.5
we get:

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
{\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
0<p\leq r\leq\infty
,
0<q\leq\tau\leq\infty
, and

(8.23)

\frac{{\mathbf{s}}}{{\mathbf{d}}}-\frac{{\bm{1}}}{p}=\frac{{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}}{{\mathbf{d}}}-\frac{{\bm{1}}}{r}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{s_{i}}{d_{i}}-\frac{1}{p}=\frac{s^{\prime}_{i}}{d_{i}}-\frac{1}{r},\;i=1,2.

Then
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{s}_{pq}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}}_{r\tau}
.
If in addition the non-collapsing condition
(
2.6
)
holds for the spaces
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
,
then
\mathscr{B}^{{\mathbf{s}}}_{pq}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{B}^{{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}}_{r\tau}
.

Proof.

Let the family
\{\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}\}_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}
be as in Definition
8.1
.

(i)

Let
f\in\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{s}_{pq}
and
j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
.
Then
\varphi_{j}(\sqrt{L})f\in\Sigma_{2^{{\mathbf{j}}+{\bm{1}}}}

and by (
6.26
) there exists a constant
c>0
independent of
f
and
{\mathbf{j}}
such that

\displaystyle\big\|V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}/{\mathbf{d}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big\|_{r}

\displaystyle\leq c\big\|V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-\frac{{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}}{{\mathbf{d}}}+(\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{p}){\bm{1}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big\|_{p}

\displaystyle=c\big\|V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big\|_{p},

using (
8.23
).
Now the embedding follows from Definition
8.1
.

(ii)
Let
f\in\mathscr{B}^{s}_{pq}
and
{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
.
Then
\varphi_{j}(\sqrt{L})f\in\Sigma_{2^{{\mathbf{j}}+{\bm{1}}}}
.
By (
6.27
) there exists a constant
c>0
independent of
f
and
{\mathbf{j}}
such that

2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}}\big\|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\big\|_{r}\leq c2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}}2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{d}}(1/p-1/r)}\big\|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\big\|_{p}=c2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{s}}}\big\|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\big\|_{p}

and the embedding again follows in light of Definition
8.1
.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

Proposition
8.5
shows that the nonclassical mixed-smoothness Besov spaces
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{s}_{pq}

are embedded into each other correctly,
while the Besov spaces
\mathscr{B}^{s}_{pq}
are embedded into each other correctly
under the non-collapsing condition.

8.2.3.
Embedding of mixed-smoothness Besov spaces into Lebesgue spaces

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
0<p,q\leq\infty
.

(i)
If
{\mathbf{s}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{+}
, then
\mathscr{B}^{{\mathbf{s}}}_{pq}\hookrightarrow L^{p}
.

(ii)
If the non-collapsing condition
(\ref{non-collapsing})
is valid
for the coordinate spaces
X_{1},X_{2}
and
{\mathbf{s}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is such that
s_{i}>d_{i}/p
,
i=1,2
,
then
\mathscr{B}^{{\mathbf{s}}}_{pq}\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}
.

Proof.

(i) Let
r:=\min\{1,p,q\}
and
\tau:=\frac{q}{r}\geq 1
.
Assuming
f\in\mathscr{B}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}

we use the decomposition (
5.22
), the
r
-triangle inequality and Hölder’s inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\|f\|_{p}^{r}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\Big\|_{p}^{r}\leq\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\big\|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\big\|_{p}^{r}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\big(2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{s}}}\big\|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\big\|_{p}\big)^{q}\Big)^{r/q}\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}2^{-r\tau^{\prime}{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{s}}}\Big)^{1/\tau^{\prime}}\leq c\|f\|_{\mathscr{B}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}}^{r},

taking into account that
s_{i}>0
,
i=1,2
.

(ii) Let
f\in\mathscr{B}^{s}_{pq}
.
By (
5.22
) and (
6.27
) we get

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\big\|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\big\|_{\infty}\leq c\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\mathbf{d}}/p}\big\|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}2^{-{\mathbf{j}}({\mathbf{s}}-{\mathbf{d}}/p)}\|f\|_{\mathscr{B}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}}\leq c\|f\|_{\mathscr{B}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}},

using that
s_{i}>d_{i}/p
,
i=1,2
.
∎

9.
Spectral multipliers

An important class of operators in the classical (Euclidean) harmonic analysis
is the class of Fourier multipliers.
Let
{\mathfrak{m}}
be a bounded function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. The Fourier multiplier
T_{\mathfrak{m}}
associated to
{\mathfrak{m}}
is defined by

T_{\mathfrak{m}}f({\mathbf{x}}):=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big({\mathfrak{m}}\mathcal{F}f\big)({\mathbf{x}}),\quad f\in L^{2},

where
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}
stand for the Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms.
Plancerel’s inequality guarantees the boundedness of
T_{\mathfrak{m}}
on
L^{2}
.
The fundamental problem here is to find conditions on the symbol function
{\mathfrak{m}}

under which
T_{\mathfrak{m}}
is bounded in certain function classes such as distributions and smoothness spaces.

In the setting of this article a natural substitute for the Fourier theory is the Spectral theory.
Let
{\mathfrak{m}}:[0,\infty)^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a measurable function.
The spectral multiplier
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})={\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L_{1}},\sqrt{J_{2}})
is defined by

(9.1)

{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})=\int_{[0,\infty)^{2}}{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\sqrt{\lambda_{2}})dE_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}.

where
dE_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}
is the spectral measure defined in §
4.4
.
Clearly, if
{\mathfrak{m}}
is bounded on
[0,\infty)^{2}
, then
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
is bounded on
L^{2}(X)
.

Our goal is to study the boundedness of spectral multipliers on the classes of
test functions
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}(L_{1},L_{2})
and distributions
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(L_{1},L_{2})
, see Section
5
,
as well as the mixed-smoothness Besov and Triebel-Lizoskin spaces, introduced in Section
8
.
Single-variable spectral multiplier results are obtained in
[
3
,
32
,
33
,
30
]
.

9.1.
Bounded spectral multipliers on distributions

We begin by introducing spectral multipliers on test functions
and distributions associated to the operators
L_{1},L_{2}
.

Definition 9.1
.

A function
{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is called admissible
if it is real-valued,

{\mathfrak{m}}(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})={\mathfrak{m}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
and all its partial derivatives have at most polynomial growth,
i.e. for any
{\bm{\beta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
there exist constants
c_{{\bm{\beta}}}>0
and
N_{{\bm{\beta}}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
such that

(9.2)

\big|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}{\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})\big|\leq c_{{\bm{\beta}}}(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{N_{{\bm{\beta}}}}.

Assume that
{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is admissible and let

\varphi^{i}_{0},\varphi^{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
i=1,2
, be two pairs of functions
satisfying conditions (
5.18
)–(
5.19
).
For reader’s convenience we recall them here:

(9.3)

\displaystyle(i)\ \hbox{$\varphi^{i}_{0},\varphi^{i}$ are real-valued and even,}

\displaystyle(ii)\ \operatorname{supp}\varphi^{i}_{0}\subset[-2,2],\ \operatorname{supp}\varphi^{i}\subset[-2,2]\setminus[-1/2,1/2],

and

(9.4)

\varphi^{i}_{0}(t)+\sum_{n\geq 1}\varphi^{i}(2^{-n}t)=1,\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\;i=1,2.

Using the standard notation
\varphi^{i}_{n}(t):=\varphi^{i}(2^{-n}t)
, we define
\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}:=\varphi^{1}_{j_{1}}\otimes\varphi^{2}_{j_{2}}
for
{\mathbf{j}}=(j_{1},j_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}_{0}
.
Then from (
9.4
) it readily follows that

\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})=1,\quad{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Further, set
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}}):={\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})
.
Then obviously

(9.5)

{\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})=\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}{\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})=\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}}).

We define the multiplier operator

{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})={\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L_{1}},\sqrt{L_{2}})
on
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}(L_{1},L_{2})

by

(9.6)

{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})\phi:=\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi,\quad\phi\in\mathcal{S},

and on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(L_{1},L_{2})
by

(9.7)

{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})f:=\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f,\quad f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime},

where the convergence is in
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, respectively.

Theorem 9.2
.

Let
{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be admissible
(
Defintion
9.1
)
.
Then the multiplier operator
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
is well defined and continuous on
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be admissible in the sense of Definition
9.1
.
We first show that the operator
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
is well defined by (
9.6
) and (
9.7
).
To this end we will show that
(a) the series in (
9.6
) and (
9.7
) converge
and
(b) the definition of
{\mathfrak{m}}(L)
is independent of the specific selection of the functions
\varphi^{i}_{0},\varphi^{i}
,
i=1,2
.

(a)
To prove that the series in (
9.6
) converges it suffices to
show that the sequence

\Big\{\sum_{{\bm{0}}\leq{\mathbf{j}}\leq{\mathbf{n}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi\Big\}

is a Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{S}
for any
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
, i.e. for
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}

(9.8)

\mathcal{P}_{m,k}\Big(\sum_{{\bm{0}}\leq{\mathbf{j}}\leq{\mathbf{n}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi-\sum_{{\bm{0}}\leq{\mathbf{j}}\leq{\bm{\ell}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi\Big)\to 0\quad\hbox{as}\quad|{\mathbf{n}}|,|{\bm{\ell}}|\to\infty,\;\;\forall\phi\in\mathcal{S}.

We denote
\Phi^{i}(t):=\varphi_{0}^{i}(2t)+\varphi^{i}(t)
,
i=1,2
.
From (
9.3
)–(
9.4
) it follows that

\operatorname{supp}\Phi^{i}\subset[-2,2]
,
\Phi^{i}(t)=1
for
t\in[-1,1]
,
and most importantly

(9.9)

\sum_{j=0}^{n}\varphi^{i}_{j}(t)=\Phi^{i}(2^{-n}t),\quad t\in\mathbb{R},\;n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Hence, for
{\mathbf{n}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}

(9.10)

\sum_{j_{1}=0}^{n_{1}}\sum_{j_{2}=0}^{n_{2}}{\mathfrak{m}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\varphi_{j_{1}}^{1}(\lambda_{1})\varphi_{j_{2}}^{2}(\lambda_{2})={\mathfrak{m}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\Phi^{1}(2^{-n_{1}}\lambda_{1})\Phi^{2}(2^{-n_{2}}\lambda_{2}),

implying

S_{\mathbf{n}}:=\sum_{{\bm{0}}\leq{\mathbf{j}}\leq{\mathbf{n}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})={\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})\Phi(2^{-{\mathbf{n}}}\sqrt{L}),

where

\Phi:=\Phi^{1}\otimes\Phi^{2}
.
If
{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
, then from above

S_{\mathbf{n}}-S_{\bm{\ell}}=\sum_{{\bm{0}}\leq{\mathbf{j}}\leq{\mathbf{n}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})-\sum_{{\bm{0}}\leq{\mathbf{j}}\leq{\bm{\ell}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})={\mathfrak{m}}(L)\big(\Phi(2^{-{\mathbf{n}}}\sqrt{L})-\Phi(2^{-{\bm{\ell}}}\sqrt{L})\big).

Consider the functions

\omega_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=\Phi^{1}(2^{-n_{1}}\lambda_{1})\Phi^{2}(2^{-n_{2}}\lambda_{2})-\Phi^{1}(2^{-\ell_{1}}\lambda_{1})\Phi^{2}(2^{-\ell_{2}}\lambda_{2})

and

(9.11)

\theta_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=\min\{2^{\ell_{1}\wedge n_{1}},2^{\ell_{2}\wedge n_{2}}\}^{r}{\mathfrak{m}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\omega_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})^{-r}.

Clearly,

S_{\mathbf{n}}-S_{\bm{\ell}}=\min\{2^{\ell_{1}\wedge n_{1}},2^{\ell_{2}\wedge n_{2}}\}^{-r}\theta_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}(\sqrt{L_{1}},\sqrt{L_{2}})(L_{1}+L_{2})^{r}

and hence

(9.12)

L^{\bm{\nu}}(S_{\mathbf{n}}-S_{\bm{\ell}})=\min\{2^{\ell_{1}\wedge n_{1}},2^{\ell_{2}\wedge n_{2}}\}^{-r}\theta_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}(\sqrt{L_{1}},\sqrt{L_{2}})\sum_{\kappa=0}^{r}\binom{r}{\kappa}L^{(\nu_{1}+\kappa,\nu_{2}+r-\kappa)}.

Fix
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. Choose
\tilde{k}_{i}>k+5d_{i}/2
,
i=1,2
,
and introduce the number
N:=\max\{N_{\bm{\beta}}:|{\bm{\beta}}|\leq\tilde{k}_{1}+\tilde{k}_{2}\}
.
Also, we choose
r>N+d_{1}+d_{2}+\tilde{k}_{1}+\tilde{k}_{2}
.
We claim that
\theta_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}(\sqrt{L})
is an integral operator whose kernel
satisfies

(9.13)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{\theta_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{1}},\tilde{{\mathbf{k}}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

To prove this, in light of Theorem
4.21
,
it suffices to show that for all
|{\bm{\beta}}|\leq\tilde{k}_{1}+\tilde{k}_{2}

(9.14)

|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}\theta_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq c(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-q},\quad q>d_{1}+d_{2}+\tilde{k}_{1}+\tilde{k}_{2}.

Clearly,

|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}\omega_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq c({\bm{\beta}})\quad\hbox{and}\quad|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}(|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{-2r})|\leq c({\bm{\beta}},r)|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{-2r},\quad|{\bm{\lambda}}|\geq 1.

In addition to this

\omega_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}({\bm{\lambda}})=0
for
{\bm{\lambda}}\in[0,2^{\ell_{1}\wedge n_{1}}]\times[0,2^{\ell_{2}\wedge n_{2}}]
.
Therefore, using the Libniz rule:

\partial^{\bm{\beta}}(fg)=\sum_{{\bm{\gamma}}\leq{\bm{\beta}}}\binom{{\bm{\beta}}}{{\bm{\gamma}}}\partial^{\bm{\gamma}}f\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}-{\bm{\gamma}}}g

we get

\displaystyle|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}(\omega_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}({\bm{\lambda}})|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{-r})|\leq c(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-2r}\leq c\min\{2^{\ell_{1}\wedge n_{1}},2^{\ell_{2}\wedge n_{2}}\}^{-r}(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-r},

for
{\bm{\lambda}}\in[0,\infty)^{2}\setminus[0,2^{\ell_{1}\wedge n_{1}}]\times[0,2^{\ell_{2}\wedge n_{2}}]

and

\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}(\omega_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}({\bm{\lambda}})|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{-r})=0
otherwise.
Consequently,

(9.15)

|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}(\omega_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}({\bm{\lambda}})|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{-r})|\leq c\min\{2^{\ell_{1}\wedge n_{1}},2^{\ell_{2}\wedge n_{2}}\}^{-r}(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-r},\quad\forall{\bm{\lambda}}\in[0,\infty)^{2}.

On the other hand, from the assumption that the function
{\mathfrak{m}}
is admissible it follows that

|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}{\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq c_{{\bm{\beta}}}(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{N_{{\bm{\beta}}}}\leq c(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{N}\quad\hbox{if}\;\;|{\bm{\beta}}|\leq\tilde{k}_{1}+\tilde{k}_{2}.

Using this and (
9.15
) along with the Libniz rule, and (
9.11
) we obtain

|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}\theta_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq c(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-r+N}\quad\hbox{if}\quad|{\bm{\beta}}|\leq\tilde{k}_{1}+\tilde{k}_{2},

which confirms (
9.14
) because
r>N+d_{1}+d_{2}+\tilde{k}_{1}+\tilde{k}_{2}
.

We introduce the abbreviated notation

{\varepsilon}({\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}},r):=\min\{2^{\ell_{1}\wedge n_{1}},2^{\ell_{2}\wedge n_{2}}\}^{-r}
.
Let
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
and fix
K\geq(\tilde{k}_{1}-d_{1}/2)\vee(\tilde{k}_{2}-d_{2}/2)
.
Then using (
9.12
) and (
9.13
) we obtain

\displaystyle|L^{\bm{\nu}}(S_{\mathbf{n}}

\displaystyle-S_{\bm{\ell}})\phi({\mathbf{x}})|

\displaystyle\leq c{\varepsilon}({\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}},r)\sum_{\kappa=0}^{r}\int_{X}|{\mathscr{K}}_{\theta_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})||L^{(\nu_{1}+\kappa,\nu_{2}+r-\kappa)}\phi({\mathbf{y}})|d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

(9.16)

\displaystyle\leq c{\varepsilon}({\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}},r)\mathcal{P}_{m+r,K}(\phi)\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{1}},\tilde{{\mathbf{k}}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},\tilde{{\mathbf{k}}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c{\varepsilon}({\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}},r)\mathcal{P}_{m+r,K}(\phi)\int_{X}V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{1}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},\tilde{{\mathbf{k}}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},\tilde{{\mathbf{k}}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c{\varepsilon}({\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}},r)\mathcal{P}_{m+r,K}(\phi){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},\tilde{{\mathbf{k}}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0}).

Here for the third inequality we used (
3.14
) and
for the last we use (
3.22
) and the fact that
\tilde{k}_{i}-d_{i}/2>2d_{i}
.

Now, using (
9.1
) we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(S_{\mathbf{n}}\phi-S_{\bm{\ell}}\phi)\leq c{\varepsilon}({\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}},r)\mathcal{P}_{m+r,K}(\phi)\sup_{{\mathbf{x}}\in X}\prod_{i=1,2}\frac{(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i}))^{k}}{(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i}))^{\tilde{k}_{i}-d_{i}/2}}.

Finally, taking into account that
\tilde{k}_{i}>k+5d_{i}/2
we conclude that

\mathcal{P}_{m,k}\Big(\sum_{{\bm{0}}\leq{\mathbf{j}}\leq{\mathbf{n}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi-\sum_{{\bm{0}}\leq{\mathbf{j}}\leq{\bm{\ell}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi\Big)\leq c\min\{2^{\ell_{1}\wedge n_{1}},2^{\ell_{2}\wedge n_{2}}\}^{-r}\mathcal{P}_{m+r,K}(\phi),

which implies (
9.8
).

The convergence of the series in (
9.7
) follows by duality from the convergence in (
9.6
).

(b) To show that the definition of the multiplier
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})

is independent of the specific selection of the functions
\varphi_{0}^{i},\varphi^{i}
,
i=1,2
,
we assume that
\psi_{0}^{i},\psi^{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
i=1,2
, are other two pairs of functions
satisfying conditions (
9.3
) and (
9.4
).
Denote
\psi_{\mathbf{j}}:=\psi^{1}_{j_{1}}\otimes\psi^{2}_{j_{2}}
for
{\mathbf{j}}=(j_{1},j_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}_{0}

and set
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{m}}}_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}}):={\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})\psi_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})
.
Then

{\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})=\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}{\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})\psi_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})=\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\widetilde{{\mathfrak{m}}}_{j}({\bm{\lambda}}).

We define the multiplier operator

\widetilde{{\mathfrak{m}}}(\sqrt{L})
on
\mathcal{S}

by

\widetilde{{\mathfrak{m}}}(\sqrt{L})\phi:=\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\widetilde{{\mathfrak{m}}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi,\quad\phi\in\mathcal{S},

and will show that
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{m}}}(\sqrt{L})\phi={\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})\phi
for
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
.

Indeed set
\Psi^{i}(t):=\psi_{0}^{i}(2t)+\psi^{i}(t)
,
i=1,2
.
From (
9.3
)–(
9.4
) it follows that

\operatorname{supp}\Psi^{i}\subset[-2,2]
,
\Psi^{i}(t)=1
for
t\in[-1,1]
,
and

(9.17)

\sum_{j=0}^{n}\psi^{i}_{j}(t)=\Psi^{i}(2^{-n}t),\quad t\in\mathbb{R},\;n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Denote

\widetilde{S}_{\mathbf{n}}:=\sum_{{\bm{0}}\leq{\mathbf{j}}\leq{\mathbf{n}}}\widetilde{{\mathfrak{m}}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})={\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})\Psi(2^{-{\mathbf{n}}}\sqrt{L}),

where
\Psi:=\Psi^{1}\otimes\Psi^{2}
,
and observe that
for
{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
we have

S_{\mathbf{n}}-\widetilde{S}_{\bm{\ell}}=\sum_{{\bm{0}}\leq{\mathbf{j}}\leq{\mathbf{n}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})-\sum_{{\bm{0}}\leq{\mathbf{j}}\leq{\bm{\ell}}}\widetilde{{\mathfrak{m}}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})={\mathfrak{m}}(L)\big(\Phi(2^{-{\mathbf{n}}}\sqrt{L})-\Psi(2^{-{\bm{\ell}}}\sqrt{L})\big).

We introduce the function

\widetilde{\omega}_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=\Phi^{1}(2^{-n_{1}}\lambda_{1})\Phi^{2}(2^{-n_{2}}\lambda_{2})-\Psi^{1}(2^{-\ell_{1}}\lambda_{1})\Psi^{2}(2^{-\ell_{2}}\lambda_{2}).

From this point on we repeat the proof from part (a) above
with the role of
\omega_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}
played by
\widetilde{\omega}_{{\bm{\ell}},{\mathbf{n}}}

to conclude that for any
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}

\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(S_{\mathbf{n}}\phi-\widetilde{S}_{\bm{\ell}}\phi)\to 0\quad\hbox{as}\quad|{\mathbf{n}}|,|{\bm{\ell}}|\to\infty,\quad\forall\phi\in\mathcal{S},

which yields

\widetilde{{\mathfrak{m}}}(\sqrt{L})\phi={\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})\phi
for
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
.
This readily implies that

\widetilde{{\mathfrak{m}}}(\sqrt{L})f={\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})f
for
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

The continuity of the operator
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
.

We now focus on the proof of the continuity of the operator
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
on
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
To a large extent we will proceed similarly as in the above proof of the first part of the theorem.

By duality it suffices to prove the continuity of
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
on
\mathcal{S}
only.
Let
m,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
.
We need to show that there exist constants
m^{*},k^{*}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
c>0
such that

(9.18)

\mathcal{P}_{m,k}({\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})\phi)\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m^{*},k^{*}}(\phi),\quad\forall\phi\in\mathcal{S}.

By (
5.1
) it suffices to prove that there exist constants
m^{*},k^{*}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
c>0
such that
for any
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
,
{\bm{\nu}}=(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
with
|\nu_{i}|\leq m
, and
{\mathbf{x}}\in X

(9.19)

\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{k}|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})\phi({\mathbf{x}})|\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m^{*},k^{*}}(\phi).

Choose
\tilde{k}_{i}>k+5d_{i}/2
,
i=1,2
,
and set
N:=\max\{N_{\bm{\beta}}:|{\bm{\beta}}|\leq\tilde{k}_{1}+\tilde{k}_{2}\}
, see (
9.2
).
Also, choose
r>N+d_{1}+d_{2}+\tilde{k}_{1}+\tilde{k}_{2}
.

From the proof of the first part of the theorem

{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})\phi=\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi

in
\mathcal{S}
,
where
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}}):={\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})

and hence

(9.20)

|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})\phi({\mathbf{x}})|\leq\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi({\mathbf{x}})|.

We next estimate
|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi({\mathbf{x}})|
in the case when
{\mathbf{j}}=(j_{1},j_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
.
The estimation of
|L^{{\bm{\nu}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi({\mathbf{x}})|
when
j_{1}=0
or
j_{2}=0
is similar; we omit it.
Recall that

{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})={\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})={\mathfrak{m}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\varphi^{1}(2^{-j_{1}}\lambda_{1})\varphi^{2}(2^{-j_{2}}\lambda_{2}),\quad{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}.

Consider the function

(9.21)

\displaystyle\theta_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})

\displaystyle=(2^{j_{1}}+2^{j_{2}})^{r}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{-r}

\displaystyle=(2^{j_{1}}+2^{j_{2}})^{r}{\mathfrak{m}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\varphi^{1}(2^{-j_{1}}\lambda_{1})\varphi^{2}(2^{-j_{2}}\lambda_{2})(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})^{-r}.

Clearly,

{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})=(2^{j_{1}}+2^{j_{2}})^{-r}\theta_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})(L_{1}+L_{2})^{r}

and hence

(9.22)

L^{{\bm{\nu}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})=(2^{j_{1}}+2^{j_{2}})^{-r}\theta_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\sum_{\kappa=0}^{r}\binom{r}{\kappa}L^{(\nu_{1}+\kappa,\nu_{2}+r-\kappa)}.

We next show that
\theta_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})
is an integral operator
with kernel satisfying

(9.23)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{\theta_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{1}},\tilde{{\mathbf{k}}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

In light of Theorem
4.21

it suffices to show that for all
|{\bm{\beta}}|\leq\tilde{k}_{1}+\tilde{k}_{2}

(9.24)

|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}\theta_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq c(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-q},\quad q>d_{1}+d_{2}+\tilde{k}_{1}+\tilde{k}_{2}.

It is easy to see that

|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq c({\bm{\beta}})\quad\hbox{and}\quad|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}(|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{-2r})|\leq c({\bm{\beta}},r)|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{-2r},\;\;|{\bm{\lambda}}|\geq 1.

Observe that

\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}\cap[0,\infty)^{2}\subset[2^{j_{1}-1},2^{j_{1}+1}]\times[2^{j_{2}-1},2^{j_{2}+1}]
.
Hence, using the Libniz rule

\displaystyle|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}(\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{-r})|\leq c(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-2r}\leq c(2^{j_{1}}+2^{j_{2}})^{-r}(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-r},

for
{\bm{\lambda}}\in[2^{j_{1}-1},2^{j_{1}+1}]\times[2^{j_{2}-1},2^{j_{2}+1}]

and

\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}(\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{-r})=0
otherwise.
Therefore,

(9.25)

|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}(\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{-r})|\leq c(2^{j_{1}}+2^{j_{2}})^{-r}(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-r},\quad\forall{\bm{\lambda}}\in[0,\infty)^{2}.

On the other hand, from (
9.2
) we have

|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}{\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq c_{{\bm{\beta}}}(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{N_{{\bm{\beta}}}}\leq c(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{N}\quad\hbox{if}\;\;|{\bm{\beta}}|\leq\tilde{k}_{1}+\tilde{k}_{2}.

Using this and (
9.25
) together with the Libniz rule, and (
9.21
) we get

|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}\theta_{\mathbf{j}}({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq c(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-r+N}\quad\hbox{if}\quad|{\bm{\beta}}|\leq\tilde{k}_{1}+\tilde{k}_{2},

which confirms (
9.24
) because
r>N+d_{1}+d_{2}+\tilde{k}_{1}+\tilde{k}_{2}
.

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
and choose
K\geq(\tilde{k}_{1}-d_{1}/2)\vee(\tilde{k}_{2}-d_{2}/2)
.
We use (
9.22
) and (
9.23
) to obtain

\displaystyle|L^{\bm{\nu}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})\phi({\mathbf{x}})|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq c(2^{j_{1}}+2^{j_{2}})^{-r}\sum_{\kappa=0}^{r}\int_{X}|{\mathscr{K}}_{\theta_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})||L^{(\nu_{1}+\kappa,\nu_{2}+r-\kappa)}\phi({\mathbf{y}})|d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\qquad\leq c(2^{j_{1}}+2^{j_{2}})^{-r}\mathcal{P}_{m+r,K}(\phi)\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{1}},\tilde{{\mathbf{k}}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},\tilde{{\mathbf{k}}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\qquad\leq c(2^{j_{1}}+2^{j_{2}})^{-r}\mathcal{P}_{m+r,K}(\phi)\int_{X}V({\mathbf{y}},{\bm{1}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},\tilde{{\mathbf{k}}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},\tilde{{\mathbf{k}}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\qquad\leq c(2^{j_{1}}+2^{j_{2}})^{-r}\mathcal{P}_{m+r,K}(\phi){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},\tilde{{\mathbf{k}}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0}),

where for the third inequality we used (
3.14
) and
for the last we use (
3.22
) and the fact that
\tilde{k}_{i}-d_{i}/2>2d_{i}
.
Using the above in (
9.20
) and summing up we obtain

\displaystyle|L^{\bm{\nu}}{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})\phi({\mathbf{x}})|

\displaystyle\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m+r,K}(\phi){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},\tilde{{\mathbf{k}}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0})\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}(2^{j_{1}}+2^{j_{2}})^{-r}

\displaystyle\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m+r,K}(\phi){\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{{\bm{1}},\tilde{{\mathbf{k}}}-{\mathbf{d}}/2}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0}),

implying

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{k}\big|L^{\bm{\nu}}{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})\phi({\mathbf{x}})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{P}_{m+r,K}(\phi)\prod_{i=1,2}\frac{\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{k}}{\big(1+\rho_{i}(x_{i},x_{0i})\big)^{\tilde{k}-d_{i}/2}}

\displaystyle\leq c\mathcal{P}_{m+r,K}(\phi),

which yields (
9.19
).
Above we used that
\tilde{k}-d_{i}/2\geq k
.
The proof is complete.
∎

9.2.
Spectral multipliers acting on mixed-smoothness B and F-spaces

Here we consider classes of spectral multipliers
that can be extended to bounded operators between mixed-smoothness Besov or Triebel-Lizorkin spaces.

Definition 9.3
.

Assume
{\bm{\tau}}=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
{\bm{\kappa}}=(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}

and let the real-valued function
{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
obey

{\mathfrak{m}}(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})={\mathfrak{m}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
We say that
{\mathfrak{m}}
belongs to the class
{\mathbb{M}}({\bm{\tau}},{\bm{\kappa}})
if
for any
{\bm{\beta}}=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
with
{\bm{\beta}}\leq{\bm{\kappa}}

there exists a constant
c_{{\bm{\beta}}}>0
such that

(9.26)

|\partial^{\bm{\beta}}{\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq c_{{\bm{\beta}}}\big(1+\lambda_{1}\big)^{\tau_{1}-\beta_{1}}(1+\lambda_{2}\big)^{\tau_{2}-\beta_{2}},\quad\forall\;{\bm{\lambda}}\geq{\bm{0}}.

Remark 9.4
.

(1)

The multiplier

{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\bm{\tau}}}({\bm{\lambda}}):=\big(1+\lambda_{1}^{2}\big)^{\tau_{1}/2}\big(1+\lambda_{2}^{2}\big)^{\tau_{2}/2}

belongs to the class
{\mathbb{M}}({\bm{\tau}},{\bm{\kappa}})
for each
{\bm{\kappa}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
.

(2)

The assumption
(\ref{multibounds})
can be replaced by a Hörmander-type one,
giving a broader class of multipliers. We do not elaborate on this issue here.

(3)

We will use the notation
{\mathbb{M}}({\bm{\kappa}}):={\mathbb{M}}((0,0),{\bm{\kappa}})
whenever
{\bm{\tau}}=(0,0)
.

We now present our principle multiplier result.

Theorem 9.5
.

Let
{\mathbf{s}},{\bm{\tau}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
0<q\leq\infty
,
{\bm{\kappa}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathbb{M}({\bm{\tau}},{\bm{\kappa}})
.

(i)
If
0<p\leq\infty
and
{\bm{\kappa}}>\frac{2{\mathbf{d}}}{p}+\frac{3{\mathbf{d}}}{2}
,
then the spectral multiplier
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
is bounded from
\mathscr{B}^{{\mathbf{s}}+{\bm{\tau}}}_{pq}
into
\mathscr{B}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}
.

(ii)
If
0<p<\infty
and
{\bm{\kappa}}>\frac{2{\mathbf{d}}}{\min\{p,q\}}+\frac{3{\mathbf{d}}}{2}
,
then the spectral multiplier
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
is bounded from
\mathscr{F}^{{\mathbf{s}}+{\bm{\tau}}}_{pq}
into
\mathscr{F}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}
.

Proof.

We will prove only part (ii) of the theorem.
The proof of part (i) is similar; we omit it.

Let
{\mathbf{s}},{\bm{\tau}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
0<q\leq\infty
,
0<p<\infty
and

{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathbb{M}}({\bm{\tau}},{\bm{\kappa}})
, where
{\bm{\kappa}}>\frac{2{\mathbf{d}}}{\min\{p,q\}}+\frac{3{\mathbf{d}}}{2}
.
Assume
f\in\mathscr{F}^{{\mathbf{s}}+{\bm{\tau}}}_{pq}
and
let
\varphi^{i}_{0},\varphi^{i},i=1,2
, be two pairs of real-valued functions
satisfying (
9.3
)–(
9.4
).
From Corollary
5.9
we have the decomposition

f=\sum\limits_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\quad(\text{convergence in}\;\mathcal{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}),

and using Theorem
9.2
we get

(9.27)

{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})f=\sum\limits_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\quad(\text{convergence in}\;\mathcal{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}).

We will use (
9.27
) to estimate
\|{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})f\|_{\mathscr{F}^{{\mathbf{s}}}_{pq}}
.
Let
{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
.
Using what we know about the supports of the functions
\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}
we obtain

(9.28)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L}){\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})f

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j_{2}=k_{2}-1}^{k_{2}+1}\sum\limits_{j_{1}=k_{1}-1}^{k_{1}+1}\varphi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L}){\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j_{2}=k_{2}-1}^{k_{2}+1}\sum\limits_{j_{1}=k_{1}-1}^{k_{1}+1}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{k}}(2^{-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f,

where
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{k}}({\bm{\lambda}}):={\mathfrak{m}}(2^{\mathbf{k}}{\bm{\lambda}})\varphi_{\mathbf{k}}(2^{\mathbf{k}}{\bm{\lambda}})
and
\varphi_{-1}\equiv 0
.
Hence, for
{\mathbf{x}}\in X
we have

(9.29)

\displaystyle|\varphi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L})

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}})|\leq\sum\limits_{j_{2}=k_{2}-1}^{k_{2}+1}\sum\limits_{j_{1}=k_{1}-1}^{k_{1}+1}|{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{k}}\big(2^{-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L}\big)\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{j_{2}=k_{2}-1}^{k_{2}+1}\sum\limits_{j_{1}=k_{1}-1}^{k_{1}+1}\int_{X}|{\mathscr{K}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{k}}\big(2^{-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L}\big)}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})||\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|d\mu({\mathbf{y}})
