\displaystyle=:\sum\limits_{j_{2}=k_{2}-1}^{k_{2}+1}\sum\limits_{j_{1}=k_{1}-1}^{k_{1}+1}I_{{\mathbf{j}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}}).

We need to estimate the decay of the kernels of the operators
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{k}}(2^{-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})
.
We claim that for any
{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
and

{\bm{\kappa}}=(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{+}
such that
{\bm{\kappa}}>3{\mathbf{d}}/2

(9.30)

\big|{\mathscr{K}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{k}}(2^{-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\big|\leq c2^{{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\bm{\tau}}}V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\kappa}}-\frac{{\mathbf{d}}}{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

To establish this estimate we will apply Theorem
4.18
and inequality (
3.14
).
Clearly,
\operatorname{supp}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{k}}\subset[-2,2]^{2}

and hence we only need to show that

(9.31)

\|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{k}}\|_{\infty}\leq c_{{\bm{\kappa}}}2^{{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\bm{\tau}}}\quad\text{for}\;\;|{\bm{\beta}}|\leq\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}.

Observe first that
\varphi_{\mathbf{k}}(2^{\mathbf{k}}{\bm{\lambda}})
is one of the the four functions

\varphi^{1}_{0}(\lambda_{1})\varphi^{2}_{0}(\lambda_{2})
,
\varphi^{1}_{0}(\lambda_{1})\varphi^{2}(\lambda_{2})
,

\varphi^{1}(\lambda_{1})\varphi^{2}_{0}(\lambda_{2})
, or
\varphi^{1}(\lambda_{1})\varphi^{2}(\lambda_{2})
,
depending on whether
k_{i}=0
or
k_{i}\geq 1
,
i=1,2
.
Hence

\|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}(\varphi_{\mathbf{k}}(2^{\mathbf{k}}{\bm{\lambda}}))\|_{\infty}\leq c({\bm{\beta}})
.
On the other hand, from the hypothesis of the theorem

{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathbb{M}}({\bm{\tau}},{\bm{\kappa}})
and hence
{\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})
obeys (
9.26
)
leading to the conclusion that

\displaystyle|\partial^{\bm{\beta}}({\mathfrak{m}}(2^{\mathbf{k}}{\bm{\lambda}}))|

\displaystyle=|\partial^{\bm{\beta}}({\mathfrak{m}}(2^{k_{1}}\lambda_{1},2^{k_{2}}\lambda_{2}))|=|2^{k_{1}\beta_{1}+k_{2}\beta_{2}}\partial^{\bm{\beta}}{\mathfrak{m}}(2^{k_{1}}\lambda_{1},2^{k_{2}}\lambda_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq c2^{k_{1}\beta_{1}+k_{2}\beta_{2}}(1+2^{k_{1}}\lambda_{1})^{\tau_{1}-\beta_{1}}(1+2^{k_{2}}\lambda_{2})^{\tau_{2}-\beta_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c2^{k_{1}\tau_{1}+k_{2}\tau_{2}}(1+\lambda_{1})^{\tau_{1}-\beta_{1}}(1+\lambda_{2})^{\tau_{2}-\beta_{2}}\leq c2^{k_{1}\tau_{1}+k_{2}\tau_{2}},

whenever
\beta_{i}\leq\kappa_{i}
,
i=1,2
, and
{\bm{\lambda}}\in[0,2]^{2}
.
The above estimates along with the Libniz rule imply (
9.31
).
Then applying Theorem
4.18
shows that estimate (
9.30
) is valid.

Choose
0<r<\min\{p,q\}
and
{\bm{\eta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\eta_{i}>2d_{i}
so that
\kappa_{i}>\eta_{i}/r+3d_{i}/2
,
i=1,2
.
Assume
{\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
with
|j_{i}-k_{i}|\leq 1
,
i=1,2
.
From this and (
9.30
) it follows that

\displaystyle\big|{\mathscr{K}}_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathbf{k}}(2^{-{\mathbf{k}}}\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\big|

\displaystyle\leq c2^{{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\bm{\tau}}}V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{-{\mathbf{k}}},{\bm{\kappa}}-\frac{{\mathbf{d}}}{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\bm{\tau}}}V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{-{\mathbf{j}}},{\bm{\kappa}}-\frac{{\mathbf{d}}}{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

We use the above to estimate
I_{{\mathbf{j}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}})
.
We obtain

(9.32)

\displaystyle I_{{\mathbf{j}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle\leq c2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\bm{\tau}}}V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-1}\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}_{2^{-{\mathbf{j}}},{\bm{\kappa}}-\frac{{\mathbf{d}}}{2}}^{*}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\bm{\tau}}}\sup_{{\mathbf{y}}\in X}|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})|{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{-{\mathbf{j}}},\frac{{\bm{\eta}}}{r}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\times V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-1}\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{-{\mathbf{j}}},{\bm{\kappa}}-\frac{{\mathbf{d}}}{2}-\frac{{\bm{\eta}}}{r}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq c2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\bm{\tau}}}\mathcal{M}_{r}\big(\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\big)({\mathbf{x}}).

Here for the last inequality we used:
(a) the maximal inequality (
6.5
) and
the fact that
\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\in\Sigma_{2^{{\mathbf{j}}+{\bm{1}}}}
and
\eta_{i}>2d_{i}
;
and
(b) (
3.19
) and the fact that
\kappa_{i}>3d_{i}/2+\eta_{i}/r
.

Now, combining (
9.29
)) and (
9.32
) we obtain

|\varphi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L}){\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}})|\leq c\sum_{j_{2}=k_{2}-1}^{k_{2}+1}\sum_{j_{1}=k_{1}-1}^{k_{1}+1}\mathcal{M}_{r}\big(2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\bm{\tau}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\big)({\mathbf{x}}).

In turn, this and the maximal inequality (
4.104
) lead to

\displaystyle\|{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})f\|_{\mathscr{F}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\Big(2^{{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\mathbf{s}}}|\varphi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L}){\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)|\Big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq c\Big\|\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\Big(2^{{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\mathbf{s}}}\sum_{j_{2}=k_{2}-1}^{k_{2}+1}\sum_{j_{1}=k_{1}-1}^{k_{1}+1}\mathcal{M}_{r}\big(2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot{\bm{\tau}}}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\big)(\cdot)\Big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq c\Big\|\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\sum_{j_{2}=k_{2}-1}^{k_{2}+1}\sum_{j_{1}=k_{1}-1}^{k_{1}+1}\big[\mathcal{M}_{r}\big(2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot({\mathbf{s}}+{\bm{\tau}})}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\big)(\cdot)\big]^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq c\Big\|\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\big[\mathcal{M}_{r}\big(2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot({\mathbf{s}}+{\bm{\tau}})}\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f\big)(\cdot)\big]^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq c\Big\|\Big(\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}\big(2^{{\mathbf{j}}\cdot({\mathbf{s}}+{\bm{\tau}})}|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)|\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq c\|f\|_{\mathscr{F}^{{\mathbf{s}}+{\bm{\tau}}}_{pq}}

and the proof is complete.
∎

9.3.
Lifting property

As we saw in Remark
9.4
the multiplier
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\bm{\tau}}
belongs to
the class
{\mathbb{M}}({\bm{\tau}},{\bm{\kappa}})
for each
{\bm{\kappa}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
.
This means that we can apply Theorem
9.5
to both
{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\bm{\tau}}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}_{-{\bm{\tau}}}
.
On the other hand the spectral multiplier rising from
{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\bm{\tau}}}
is the tensor product
\big(I_{1}+L_{1}\big)^{\tau_{1}/2}\otimes\big(I_{2}+L_{2}\big)^{\tau_{2}/2}
.
As a consequence of Theorem
9.5
we infer the following lifting property:

Corollary 9.6
.

Let
{\mathbf{s}},{\bm{\tau}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
0<q\leq\infty
. Then

(i)

If
0<p\leq\infty
, then the operator

\big(I_{1}+L_{1}\big)^{\tau_{1}/2}\otimes\big(I_{2}+L_{2}\big)^{\tau_{2}/2}

is bounded from
\mathscr{B}^{{\mathbf{s}}}_{pq}
into
\mathscr{B}^{{\mathbf{s}}-{\bm{\tau}}}_{pq}
and
\big\|\big(I_{1}+L_{1}\big)^{\tau_{1}/2}\otimes\big(I_{2}+L_{2}\big)^{\tau_{2}/2}f\big\|_{\mathscr{B}^{{\mathbf{s}}-{\bm{\tau}}}_{pq}}

is an equivalent quasi-norm for
\mathscr{B}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}
.

(ii)

If
0<p<\infty
, then the operator

\big(I_{1}+L_{1}\big)^{\tau_{1}/2}\otimes\big(I_{2}+L_{2}\big)^{\tau_{2}/2}

is bounded from
\mathscr{F}^{{\mathbf{s}}}_{pq}
into
\mathscr{F}^{{\mathbf{s}}-{\bm{\tau}}}_{pq}
and
\big\|\big(I_{1}+L_{1}\big)^{\tau_{1}/2}\otimes\big(I_{2}+L_{2}\big)^{\tau_{2}/2}f\big\|_{\mathscr{F}^{{\mathbf{s}}-{\bm{\tau}}}_{pq}}

is an equivalent quasi-norm for
\mathscr{F}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}
.

This corollary is in the spirit of the classical smoothness reduction on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

by using as multipliers
{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\bm{\tau}}}({\bm{\xi}}):=\big(1+|{\bm{\xi}}|^{2}\big)^{{\bm{\tau}}/2}
,
{\bm{\tau}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

9.4.
Spectral multipliers on non-classical mixed-smoothness B-F-spaces

It is natural to question whether Theorem
9.5
holds for the
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}
spaces.
We next establish the following result:

Theorem 9.7
.

Let
{\mathbf{s}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
0<q\leq\infty
,
{\bm{\kappa}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathbb{M}}({\bm{\kappa}})
.

(i)
If
0<p\leq\infty
and
\kappa_{i}>|s_{i}|+\frac{2d_{i}}{p}+\frac{3d_{i}}{2},i=1,2
,
then the spectral multiplier
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
is bounded on
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}^{{\mathbf{s}}}_{pq}
.

(ii)
If
0<p<\infty
and
\kappa_{i}>|s_{i}|+\frac{2d_{i}}{\min(p,q)}+\frac{3d_{i}}{2}
,
then the spectral multiplier
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
is bounded on
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathbf{s}}_{pq}
.

Proof.

This proof is quite similar to the proof of Theorem
9.5
.
So, we will use the notation and elements of the proof of Theorem
9.5
and only indicate the differences.
Let
{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
.
Just as in (
9.29
) we get

(9.33)

V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\big|\varphi_{\mathbf{k}}(\sqrt{L}){\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{x}})\big|\leq\sum_{j_{1}=k_{1}-1}^{k_{1}+1}\sum_{j_{2}=k_{2}-1}^{k_{2}+1}V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}I_{{\mathbf{j}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}}).

By (
3.17
) and since
j_{i}\sim k_{i}
we have

(9.34)

V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\leq c{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{\mathbf{j}},\tilde{{\mathbf{s}}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})^{-1}V({\mathbf{y}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}},

where
\tilde{{\mathbf{s}}}:=(|s_{1}|,|s_{2}|)
.

Just as in (
9.32
) (with
{\bm{\tau}}=(0,0)
)
using the maximal inequality (
6.5
),
(
9.33
), (
9.34
),
and estimate (
3.19
),
which holds due to the assumption
\kappa_{i}>|s_{i}|+\frac{\eta_{i}}{r}+\frac{3d_{i}}{2}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{k}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}I_{{\mathbf{j}},{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle\qquad\leq cV({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-1}\int_{X}V({\mathbf{y}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{\mathbf{j}},{\bm{\kappa}}-\tilde{{\mathbf{s}}}-\frac{{\mathbf{d}}}{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\big|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})\big|d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\qquad\leq c\sup_{{\mathbf{y}}\in X}V({\mathbf{y}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\big|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f({\mathbf{y}})\big|{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{\mathbf{j}},\frac{{\bm{\eta}}}{r}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\hskip 108.405pt\times V({\mathbf{x}},2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-1}\int_{X}{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}_{2^{\mathbf{j}},{\bm{\kappa}}-\tilde{{\mathbf{s}}}-\frac{{\bm{\eta}}}{r}-\frac{{\mathbf{d}}}{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d\mu({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\qquad\leq c\mathcal{M}_{r}\Big(V(\cdot,2^{-{\mathbf{j}}})^{-{\mathbf{s}}/{\mathbf{d}}}\big|\varphi_{\mathbf{j}}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big|\Big)({\mathbf{x}}).

The rest of the proof is identical to the one of Theorem
9.5
; we skip further details.
∎

10.
Ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces on product domains

In this section we develop the basics of ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces (B and F-spaces)
on the product domain
X=X_{1}\times X_{2}
associated with the operators
L_{1},L_{2}
.
As in the case of mixed-smoothness B and F-spaces, developed in Section
8
,
we will introduce
classical and nonclassical
ordinary B and F-spaces,
which reflects the possible anisotropic geometries of the spaces
X_{1},X_{2}
.
The ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces will be denoted by

B_{pq}^{s}
,
\widetilde{B}_{pq}^{s}
,
F_{pq}^{s}
, and
\widetilde{F}_{pq}^{s}

and developed with full set of parameters
s,p,q
.
Note that here the smoothness parameter
s\in\mathbb{R}
, i.e.
s
is a scalar,
while for the B and F-spaces with dominating mixed smoothness the smoothness parameter
{\mathbf{s}}
is a vector,
i.e.
{\mathbf{s}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
The theory of Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces on product domains is grossly underdeveloped
because B and F-spaces on domains other than
\mathbb{R}^{n}
are much more recent vintage.
We refer the reader to
[
37
]
for examples of ordinary B and F-spaces on specific product domains
such as
[-1,1]^{d}
associated with the Jacobi operator
or
B^{d_{1}}\times[-1,1]^{d_{2}}
associated with operators with polynomial eigenfunctions
on the unit ball
B^{d_{1}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
and
[-1,1]^{d_{2}}
.
Ordinary B and F-spaces on products of other domains such as the sphere, simplex, torus, and
\mathbb{R}^{d}

associated with operators are also discussed in
[
37
]
.
Our development here will cover all these specific examples of ordinary B and F-spaces
as well as other examples that involve e.g. Riemannian manifolds, Lee groups and more general Dirichlet spaces,
see Section
11.1
below.

The theory of ordinary B and F-spaces on product domains is somewhat simpler
than the theory of the mixed-smoothness B and F-spaces, developed in Section
8
.
The proofs of all results that we will present here either follow with minor modifications
from the proofs of the corresponding results for mixed-smoothness B and F-spaces
or can be carried out along the lines of the proofs in Section
8
.
Therefore, we will put the emphasis only on the differences between the two theories
and omit the routine proofs.
Overall our aim here is to exhibit the main differences between
mixed-smoothness B and F-spaces and ordinary B and F-spaces on product domains
associated with operators and put in perspective our development from the previous sections.

Properties of product spaces and notation.

Here we will be operating in the setting described in §
2.1
.
In particular, we consider the product space
X=X_{1}\times X_{2}

equipped with the metric

(10.1)

\rho({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}):=\max\big\{\rho_{1}(x_{1},y_{1}),\rho_{2}(x_{2},y_{2})\big)\},\quad{\mathbf{x}},\;{\mathbf{y}}\in X,

and the product measure
\mu:=\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
.

The ball
B({\mathbf{x}},r)\subset X
centered at
{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in X
of radius
r>0
is obviously given by

(10.2)

B({\mathbf{x}},r)=B_{1}(x_{1},r)\times B_{2}(x_{2},r)

and

(10.3)

V({\mathbf{x}},r):=\mu(B({\mathbf{x}},r))=\mu_{1}(B_{1}(x_{1},r))\mu_{2}(B_{2}(x_{2},r)),

where
B_{i}(x_{i},r)
is the respective ball in
X_{i}
,
i=1,2
.
From (
2.1
) it follows that the metric measure space
(X,\rho,\mu)

has the doubling property and (
2.9
) yields

(10.4)

V({\mathbf{x}},\lambda\delta)\leq c\lambda^{d}V({\mathbf{x}},\delta)),\quad\delta\geq 0,\;\lambda\geq 1,

where
d:=d_{1}+d_{2}
.

We define, for
{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2})
,
{\mathbf{y}}=(y_{1},y_{2})
in
X
and
\delta,\sigma>0
,

(10.5)

{\mathfrak{D}}_{\delta,\sigma}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}):=\frac{\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+\delta^{-1}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{-\sigma}}{[V({\mathbf{x}},\delta)V({\mathbf{y}},\delta)]^{1/2}}.

Remark 10.1
.

Observe that unlike in previous sections in this section the parameters
d
,
\delta
,
\sigma
,
and later
\gamma
,
\tau
,
j
, etc. are scalars, which reflects the nature of
the ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces that will be introduced below
as a single parameter smoothness spaces.
Also the meaning of
V({\mathbf{x}},\delta)^{\gamma}
is somewhat different compared to

V({\mathbf{x}},{\bm{\delta}})^{\bm{\gamma}}
, compare (
10.3
) to (
3.17
); compare
{\mathfrak{D}}_{\delta,\sigma}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
from (
10.5
)
to
{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\bm{\delta}},{\bm{\sigma}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
from (
3.11
) as well.

Integral operators.
Operators of the form

F(\delta\sqrt{L}):=F(\delta\sqrt{L_{1}},\delta\sqrt{L_{2}})
,
where
F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
is smooth and satisfies the condition

F(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
,
will play an important role.
The next theorem is an immediate consequence of Theorem
4.21
.

Theorem 10.2
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{C}^{2k}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
,
k>3(d_{1}\vee d_{2})/2
,
k\geq 2
,
be real-valued and
satisfy the conditions:

F(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=F(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

and

|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}F({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq C_{k}(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{-r},\quad r>d+2k,\;|{\bm{\beta}}|\leq 2k.

Then
F(\delta\sqrt{L}):=F(\delta\sqrt{L_{1}},\delta\sqrt{L_{2}})
,
\delta>0
, is an integral operator
whose kernel
{\mathscr{K}}_{F(\delta\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
satisfies

(10.6)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F(\delta\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|\leq c_{k}{\mathfrak{D}}_{\delta,k}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\quad\text{and}

(10.7)

|{\mathscr{K}}_{F(\delta\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathscr{K}}_{F(\delta\sqrt{L})}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|\leq c_{k}^{\prime}\Big(\frac{\rho({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})}{\delta}\Big)^{\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}}{\mathfrak{D}}_{\delta,k}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

if
\rho({\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})\leq\delta
, where
the constants
c_{k},c_{k^{\prime}}
depend on
k
,
d
, and
C_{k}
.

This theorem makes it possible to develop the theory of ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces
in the setting of this article.

Single parameter functional calculus.

Clearly, (see Proposition
4.1
) the operator

L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2}
is essentially self-adjoint and non-negative.
We denote its closure again by
L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2}
.
Denote by
\mathscr{E}_{\lambda}
,
\lambda\geq 0
, the spectral resolution associated to

L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2}
on
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
.
Then for any bounded measurable function
F
on
[0,\infty)
the operator
F(L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2})

is defined by

F(L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2})=\int_{0}^{\infty}F(\lambda)d\mathscr{E}_{\lambda}.

The semigroup associated to
L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2}
takes the form

e^{-t(L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2})}=e^{-tL_{1}}\otimes e^{-tL_{2}},\quad t>0,

and using the assumptions from §
2.1
it follows that

e^{-t(L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2})}
has Gaussian localization, Hölder continuity,
and the Markov property, see §
2.1
and Proposition
4.10
.
Also, as was alluded to above the space
(X,\rho,\mu)
,
X=X_{1}\times X_{2}
, has the doubling property.
Therefore, a smooth (one parameter) functional calculus
as in §
4.7
or in
[
39
, Section 3]
can be developed in full.

We next make the connection between the single parameter functional calculus
induced by
L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2}

and the two-parameter functional calculus associated to
L_{1},L_{2}
from Section
4
.
As above assume that
F
is a bounded measurable function on
[0,\infty)
.
Consider the function
G(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=F(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})
on
[0,\infty)^{2}
.
Then we have

F(L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2})=G(\sqrt{L_{1}},\sqrt{L_{2}}).

As will be shown in §
10.1
below this leads to the conclusion that
the ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces on the product domain
X_{1}\times X_{2}

can be developed based on the functional calculus associated to
L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2}
.

Maximal operators.
In the current setting the maximal operator
\mathcal{M}_{r}
,
r>0
, is defined as usual by

(10.8)

\mathcal{M}_{r}f({\mathbf{x}}):=\sup_{B\ni{\mathbf{x}}}\Big(\frac{1}{\mu(B)}\int_{B}|f({\mathbf{y}})|^{r}d\mu({\mathbf{y}})\Big)^{1/r},\quad{\mathbf{x}}\in X,

where the
\sup
is taken over all balls
B\subset X

such that
{\mathbf{x}}\in B
.

With this notation the Fefferman-Stein vector-valued maximal inequality takes
the usual form (
4.104
).

Test functions and distributions
Naturally, in the current setting the class of test functions

\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}(L_{1},L_{2})
is defined as the set of all functions

\phi\in\cap_{{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}}D(L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}}),

where
D(L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}})
is the domain of the operator
L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}}
, such that

(10.9)

\mathcal{P}_{m,k}(\phi):=\sup_{{\mathbf{x}}\in X}\big(1+\rho({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{x}}_{0})\big)^{k}\max_{0\leq\nu_{i}\leq m}\big|(L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}})\phi({\mathbf{x}})\big|<\infty,\quad\forall\,k,m\geq 0.

As before here the point
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}\in X
is selected arbitrarily and fixed once and for all.
It is easy to see that this class of test functions coincides with the class
\mathcal{S}

from §
5.1
and has the same topology.
Also, as in §
5.1
the space
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(L_{1},L_{2})
of distributions associated to
L_{1},L_{2}

is defined as the set of all
continuous linear functionals on
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}(L_{1},L_{2})
.

Spectral spaces.
The spectral space
\Sigma_{t}
,
t>0
, is defined by

(10.10)

\Sigma_{t}:=\big\{g\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:\theta(\sqrt{L}_{1},\sqrt{L_{2}})g=g,\text{ for all }\theta\in\mathcal{A}_{t}\big\},\quad\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(L_{1},L_{2}),

where

\mathcal{A}_{t}:=\big\{\theta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}):\theta(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=\theta(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\text{ and }\theta\equiv 1\text{ on }[0,t]^{2}\big\}.

Observe that
\Sigma_{t}=\Sigma_{(t,t)}
, where
\Sigma_{(t,t)}
is the spectral space
introduced in Definition
6.1
.

The Peetre maximal inequality takes this form:
If
\gamma>0
,
\tau>2(d_{1}\vee d_{2})
, and
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
,
then there exists a constant
c>0

such that
for any
g\in\Sigma_{t}
and
t\geq 1

(10.11)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle t^{-2|{\bm{\nu}}|}\sup_{{\mathbf{y}}\in X}\frac{V({\mathbf{y}},t^{-1})^{\gamma}|(L_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L_{2}^{\nu_{2}})g({\mathbf{y}})|}{\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+t\rho(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{\tau/r}}

\displaystyle\leq c\sup_{{\mathbf{y}}\in X}\frac{V({\mathbf{y}},t^{-1})^{\gamma}|g({\mathbf{y}})|}{\prod_{i=1,2}\big(1+t\rho(x_{i},y_{i})\big)^{\tau/r}}\leq c\mathcal{M}_{r}\big(V(\cdot,t^{-1})^{\gamma}g(\cdot)\big)({\mathbf{x}}),\;\;{\mathbf{x}}\in X.

The Nikolski type inequality takes the form:
If
0<p\leq q\leq\infty
,
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, and
\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
then there exists a constant
c>0
such that
for any
g\in\Sigma_{t}
,
t\geq 1
, and
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
, we have

(10.12)

\big\|V(\cdot,t^{-1})^{\gamma}(L^{\nu_{1}}\otimes L^{\nu_{2}})g(\cdot)\big\|_{q}\leq ct^{2|{\bm{\nu}}|}\big\|V(\cdot,t^{-1})^{\gamma+\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p}}g(\cdot)\big\|_{p}.

Inequalities (
10.11
), (
10.12
) follow readily by Theorems
6.4
,
6.5
.

10.1.
Definition of ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces

We now introduce ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces on
X=X_{1}\times X_{2}

associated with the operators
L_{1},L_{2}
.

We introduce two types of ordinary B and F-spaces:
(i) Classical ordinary B-spaces
B_{pq}^{s}=B_{pq}^{s}(L_{1},L_{2})

and F-spaces
F_{pq}^{s}=F_{pq}^{s}(L_{1},L_{2})
,
and
(ii) Nonclassical ordinary B-spaces
\widetilde{B}_{pq}^{s}=\widetilde{B}_{pq}^{s}(L_{1},L_{2})

and F-spaces
\widetilde{F}_{pq}^{s}=\widetilde{F}_{pq}^{s}(L_{1},L_{2})
.
To define these spaces we introduce a pair of real-valued functions

\varphi_{0},\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying the conditions:

(10.13)

\displaystyle(i)\ \varphi_{0}(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=\varphi_{0}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\ \varphi(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=\varphi(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\;{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

\displaystyle(ii)\ \operatorname{supp}\varphi_{0}\subset B(0,2),\ |\varphi_{0}({\bm{\lambda}})|\geq\hat{c}>0\;\hbox{ for }|{\bm{\lambda}}|\leq 5/3,

\displaystyle(iii)\ \operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset B(0,2)\setminus B(0,1/2),\ |\varphi({\bm{\lambda}})|\geq\hat{c}>0\hbox{ for }3/5\leq|{\bm{\lambda}}|\leq 5/3,

where
B(0,r):=\{{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|{\bm{\lambda}}|<r\}
,
|{\bm{\lambda}}|:=(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})^{1/2}
.

Denote
\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}}):=\varphi(2^{-j}{\bm{\lambda}})
,
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Then from above it follows that

(10.14)

\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}|\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}})|\geq\hat{c}>0,\quad{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

In what follows we will use the short-hand notation

\varphi_{j}(\sqrt{L}):=\varphi(2^{-j}\sqrt{L_{1}},2^{-j}\sqrt{L_{2}})

and

\sqrt{L}:=(\sqrt{L_{1}},\sqrt{L_{2}})
.
Also, recall that here
d:=d_{1}+d_{2}
.

Definition 10.3
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
0<p,q\leq\infty
.

(i)
The ordinary Besov space
B_{pq}^{s}=B_{pq}^{s}(L)

is defined as the set of all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
such that

(10.15)

\|f\|_{B_{pq}^{s}}:=\Big(\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\Big(2^{js}\|\varphi_{j}(\sqrt{L})f\|_{p}\Big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}<\infty.

(ii)
The ordinary Besov space
\widetilde{B}_{pq}^{s}=\widetilde{B}_{pq}^{s}(L)
is defined as the set
of all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
such that

(10.16)

\|f\|_{\widetilde{B}_{pq}^{s}}:=\Big(\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\Big(\big\|V(\cdot,2^{-j})^{-s/d}\varphi_{j}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)\big\|_{p}\Big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}<\infty.

Above the
\ell^{q}
-norm is replaced by the sup-norm if
q=\infty
.

Definition 10.4
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
0<p<\infty
and
0<q\leq\infty
.

(i)
The ordinary Triebel-Lizorkin space
F_{pq}^{s}=F_{pq}^{s}(L)

is defined as the set of all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
such that

(10.17)

\|f\|_{F_{pq}^{s}}:=\Big\|\Big(\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\Big(2^{js}|\varphi_{j}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)|\Big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}<\infty.

(ii)
The ordinary Triebel-Lizorkin space
\widetilde{F}_{pq}^{s}=\widetilde{F}_{pq}^{s}(L)
is defined as the set of all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}

such that

(10.18)

\|f\|_{\widetilde{F}_{pq}^{s}}:=\Big\|\Big(\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\Big(V(\cdot,2^{-j})^{-s/d}|\varphi_{j}(\sqrt{L})f(\cdot)|\Big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{p}<\infty.

A straightforward adaptation of the proof of Proposition
8.3

shows that the definition of the ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces from above
is independent of the specific selection of the functions
\varphi_{0},\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

satisfying conditions (
10.13
).

We next show that the ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces defined above
essentially depend only on the operator
L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2}
.
Indeed, let

\psi_{0}({\bm{\lambda}})=\Phi_{0}(|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{2})
and
\psi({\bm{\lambda}})=\Phi(|{\bm{\lambda}}|^{2})
,
where
\Phi_{0},\Phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
are real-valued even functions such that

(10.19)

\displaystyle(i)\ \operatorname{supp}\Phi_{0}\subset[-4,4],\ |\Phi_{0}(t)|\geq\hat{c}>0\;\hbox{ for }|t|\leq 25/9,

\displaystyle(ii)\ \operatorname{supp}\Phi\cap[0,\infty)\subset[1/4,4],\ |\Phi(t)|\geq\hat{c}>0\;\hbox{ for }9/25\leq|t|\leq 25/9.

It is easily seen that the functions
\psi_{0},\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and satisfy conditions (
10.13
).
Therefore, in the definition of the spaces
B_{pq}^{s}
,
\widetilde{B}_{pq}^{s}
,
F_{pq}^{s}
, and
\widetilde{F}_{pq}^{s}
above
the functions
\varphi_{0},\varphi
can be replaced by
\psi_{0},\psi
.
Observe now that for
j\geq 1

\psi_{j}(\sqrt{L})=\Phi(2^{-2j}(L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2}))

and similarly

\psi_{0}(\sqrt{L})=\Phi_{0}(L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2})
.
Furthermore, as was explained above. the smooth functional calculus associated to the operator
L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2}
can be developed
as a single parameter functional calculus.
Therefore, the theory of ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces defined above
can be developed based on the single parameter calculus associated to the operator
L_{1}\otimes I_{2}+I_{1}\otimes L_{2}
.

Embeddings.

The embeddings of the ordinary Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces in the setting of this article
that we present next are quite similar to the embedings of the mixed-smoothness Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces.

The embeddings between ordinary B and F-spaces and test functions or distributions
take the form:
Let
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
0<q\leq\infty
. If
0<p\leq\infty
then

\mathcal{S}\hookrightarrow B^{s}_{pq},\;\widetilde{B}^{s}_{pq}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime},

and, if
0<p<\infty
, then

\mathcal{S}\hookrightarrow\;F^{s}_{pq},\;\widetilde{F}^{s}_{pq}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}.

The proof of this result is carried out along the lines of the proof of Theorem
8.4
.

The following embedding result between ordinary B-spaces with different parameters
follows readily by the Nikolski inequality (
10.12
):
If
s,s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
0<p\leq r\leq\infty
,
0<q\leq\tau\leq\infty
, and

\frac{s}{d}-\frac{1}{p}=\frac{s^{\prime}}{d}-\frac{1}{r},

then
\widetilde{B}^{s}_{pq}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{B}^{s^{\prime}}_{r\tau}
.
If in addition the non-collapsing condition
(
2.6
)
holds for the spaces
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
,
then
B^{s}_{pq}\hookrightarrow B^{s^{\prime}}_{r\tau}
.

Also, just as in Theorem
8.7
we have:
If
s>0
and
0<p,q\leq\infty
, then
B^{s}_{pq}\hookrightarrow L^{p}
.

10.2.
Spectral multipliers

Here we consider spectral multipliers that are compatible with the ordinary
Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin defined in §
10.1
.

Definition 10.5
.

A function
{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is called admissible
if it is real-valued,

{\mathfrak{m}}(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})={\mathfrak{m}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
and all its partial derivatives have at most polynomial growth,
i.e. for any
{\bm{\beta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}
there exist constants
c_{{\bm{\beta}}}>0
and
N_{{\bm{\beta}}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
such that

\big|\partial^{{\bm{\beta}}}{\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})\big|\leq c_{{\bm{\beta}}}(1+|{\bm{\lambda}}|)^{N_{{\bm{\beta}}}}.

Let
{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be admissible and
consider a pair of real-valued functions

\varphi_{0},\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying the conditions:

\displaystyle(i)\ \varphi_{0}(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=\varphi_{0}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\ \varphi(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})=\varphi(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\;{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

\displaystyle(ii)\ \operatorname{supp}\varphi_{0}\subset B(0,2),\ \operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset B(0,2)\setminus B(0,1/2),

where
B(0,r):=\{{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|{\bm{\lambda}}|<r\}
,
and

\varphi_{0}({\bm{\lambda}})+\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}\varphi(2^{-j}{\bm{\lambda}})=1,\quad\forall{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Denote
\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}}):=\varphi(2^{-j}{\bm{\lambda}})
,
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Then from above it follows that

\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}})=1
for
{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Set
{\mathfrak{m}}_{j}({\bm{\lambda}}):={\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}})
.
Then obviously

{\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})\varphi_{j}({\bm{\lambda}})=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{j}({\bm{\lambda}}).

We define the multiplier operator

{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})={\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L_{1}},\sqrt{L_{2}})
on
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}(L_{1},L_{2})

by

{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})\phi:=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{j}(\sqrt{L})\phi,\quad\phi\in\mathcal{S},

and on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(L_{1},L_{2})
by

{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})f:=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{j}(\sqrt{L})f,\quad f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime},

where the convergence is in
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

An easy adaptation of the proof of Theorem
9.2

leads to the following claim:
If
{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is admissible
(
Defintion
10.5
)
,
then the multiplier operator
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
is well defined and continuous on
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

With the next definition we introduce a class of multipliers that
will act on ordinary B and F-spaces.

Definition 10.6
.

Assume
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}

and let the real-valued function
{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfy

{\mathfrak{m}}(\pm\lambda_{1},\pm\lambda_{2})={\mathfrak{m}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
We say that
{\mathfrak{m}}
belongs to the class
{\mathbb{M}}(\tau,\kappa)
if
for any
{\bm{\beta}}=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
with
\beta_{i}\leq\kappa

there exists a constant
c_{{\bm{\beta}}}>0
such that

(10.20)

|\partial^{\bm{\beta}}{\mathfrak{m}}({\bm{\lambda}})|\leq c_{{\bm{\beta}}}\big(1+\lambda_{1}\big)^{\tau-\beta_{1}}(1+\lambda_{2}\big)^{\tau-\beta_{2}},\quad\forall\;{\bm{\lambda}}\geq{\bm{0}}.

The following two theorems are analogues of Theorems
9.5
,
9.7
.

Theorem 10.7
.

Let
s,\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
0<q\leq\infty
,
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathbb{M}}(\tau,\kappa)
.

(i)
If
0<p\leq\infty
and
\kappa>\frac{2d}{p}+\frac{3d}{2}
,
then the spectral multiplier
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
is bounded from
B^{s+2\tau}_{pq}
into
B^{s}_{pq}
.

(ii)
If
0<p<\infty
and
\kappa>\frac{2d}{\min\{p,q\}}+\frac{3d}{2}
,
then the spectral multiplier
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
is bounded from
F^{s+2\tau}_{pq}
into
F^{s}_{pq}
.

Theorem 10.8
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
0<q\leq\infty
,
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathbb{M}}(0,\kappa)
.

(i)
If
0<p\leq\infty
and
\kappa>|s|+\frac{2d}{p}+\frac{3d}{2}
,
then the spectral multiplier
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
is bounded on
\widetilde{B}^{s}_{pq}
.

(ii)
If
0<p<\infty
and
\kappa>|s|+\frac{2d}{\min(p,q)}+\frac{3d}{2}
,
then the spectral multiplier
{\mathfrak{m}}(\sqrt{L})
is bounded on
\widetilde{F}^{s}_{pq}
.

The proofs of these two theorems are carried out along the lines of the proofs of Theorems
9.5
,
9.7
.
We omit them.

11.
Appendix

11.1.
Examples of coordinate spaces covered by our theory

In this section we provide several examples
of metric measure spaces
(X,\rho,\mu)
associated with operators
L

satisfying the conditions on the coordinate spaces from our general setting in §
2.1
.

Example 11.1
.

The classical setup of
X=\mathbb{R}^{n}
equipped with the Euclidean distance and
Lebesgue measure, combined with the Laplace operator
L=-\Delta

is our principle example of a coordinate space covered by our general setting in §
2.1
.

Another classical example is the torus

X=\mathbb{R}^{n}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}

equipped with the Euclidean distance and
Lebesgue measure, combined with the Laplace operator
L=-\Delta
.

Example 11.2
.

The unit sphere
X={\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
equipped with the standard (geodesic) distance and surface measure
associated with the Laplace-Beltrami operator
is also a typical example of a coordinate space in the sense of our general setting from §
2.1
.

Example 11.3
.

Consider
X=[-1,1]
equipped with the weighted measure

d\mu(x):=w(x)dx=(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}dx,\quad\alpha,\beta>-1,

and the distance

\rho(x,y):=|\arccos x-\arccos y|
.
It is easy to see that
(
e.g.
[
12
]
)

V(x,r)\sim r(1-x+r^{2})^{\alpha+1/2}(1+x+r^{2})^{\beta+1/2},\quad x\in[-1,1],\;0<r\leq\pi.

Therefore, we have a doubling metric measure space. Let
L
be the classical Jacobi operator defined by

Lf(x):=-\frac{\big[w(x)(1-x^{2})f^{\prime}(x)\big]^{\prime}}{w(x)}.

As is shown in
[
12
]

(
see also
[
40
]
)

the associated heat kernel has Gaussian localization, Hölder continuity, and the Markov property.

Example 11.4
.

Consider the unit ball
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}:=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|x\|<1\big\}

equipped with measure

d\mu:=(1-\|x\|^{2})^{\gamma-1/2}dx,\quad\gamma>-1,

and metric

\rho(x,y):=\arccos\big(\langle x,y\rangle+\sqrt{1-\|x\|^{2}}\sqrt{1-\|y\|^{2}}\big),

where
\langle x,y\rangle
is the inner product of
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

and
\|x\|:=\sqrt{\langle x,x\rangle}
.
It is easy to see that
({\mathbb{B}}^{n},\rho,\mu)
is a doubling metric measure space, since for the ball centered at
x\in{\mathbb{B}}^{n}
of radius
r
one has

V(x,r)\sim r^{n}(1-\|x\|^{2}+r^{2})^{\gamma}.

As is well known
(
e.g.
[
40
]
)
the operator

(11.1)

L:=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-x_{i}^{2})\partial^{2}_{i}+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}x_{i}x_{j}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}+(n+2\gamma)\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\partial_{i},

acting on smooth functions on
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}

is non-negative and self-adjoint with polynomial eigenfunctions.
More importantly, its heat kernel
has Gaussian localization, Hölder continuity, and the Markov property

(
see
[
40
,
41
]
)
.

Example 11.5
.

Consider the simplex

{\mathbb{T}}^{n}:=\Big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:x_{1}>0,\dots,x_{n}>0,\;|x|<1\Big\},\quad|x|:=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n},

with measure

d\mu(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{\kappa_{i}-1/2}(1-|x|)^{\kappa_{n+1}-1/2}dx,\quad\kappa_{i}>-1/2,

and distance

\rho(x,y)=\arccos\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{x_{i}y_{i}}+\sqrt{1-|x|}\sqrt{1-|y|}\Big).

The volume of the balls here
(
see e.g.
[
40
]
)
behave as

V(x,r)\sim r^{n}(1-|x|+r^{2})^{\kappa_{n+1}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}+r^{2})^{\kappa_{i}}

and hence

({\mathbb{T}}^{n},\rho,\mu)
is a doubling metric measure space.
The associated operator
(
see
[
40
]
)

L:=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\partial_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{j}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\kappa_{i}+\tfrac{1}{2}-(|\kappa|+\tfrac{n+1}{2})x_{i}\big)\partial_{i}

with
|\kappa|:=\kappa_{1}+\dots+\kappa_{n+1}

is non-negative and self-adjoint with polynomial eigenfunctions.
Furthermore, its heat kernel
has Gaussian localization, Hölder continuity, and the Markov property

(
see
[
40
,
41
]
)
.

Example 11.6
.

As three generic examples of coordinate spaces in the sense of the setting from §
2.1
we list the followings:

•

The Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
associated with uniformly elliptic divergence form operators.

•

Lie groups or homogeneous spaces with polynomial volume growth, associated with sub-Laplacians.

•

Complete Riemannian manifolds with Ricci curvature bounded from below associated with the Laplace-Beltrami operator.

There are various other examples of spaces that can serve as coordinate spaces and obey the conditions from §
2.1
.
For more details see the examples presented in
[
12
,
39
]
.

11.2.
Proof of Proposition
4.4

To show that
\overline{E}_{0}(R)
is well defined we first extend the operator
E_{0}
to
\mathscr{B}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
For any
R\in\mathscr{B}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
represented as in (
4.9
) we define

(11.2)

E_{0}(R):=\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{0}(J_{1j}\times J_{2j})\quad\hbox{on}\quad\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu).

We next show that the operator
E_{0}(R)
is well defined.
To this end we need to show that the definition of
E_{0}(R)
in (
11.2
)
is independent of the selection of
\{J_{1j}\times J_{2j}\}
.

We first show that if

R=(a_{1},b_{1}]\times(a_{2},b_{2}]=:\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2}

is represented as in (
4.9
), i.e.

R=\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2}=\cup_{j=1}^{N}J_{1j}\times J_{2j},

then

(11.3)

E_{0}(\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2}):=\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{0}(J_{1j}\times J_{2j}).

Indeed, let us project the end points of the intervals

\{J_{1j}\}
onto
(a_{1},b_{1}]
and the end points of the intervals
\{J_{2j}\}
onto
(a_{2},b_{2}]
.
Denote these points by
\{\xi_{j}\}
and
\{\eta_{k}\}
and
assume that they are ordered as follows

(11.4)

a_{1}=\xi_{0}<\xi_{1}<\cdots<\xi_{n}=b_{1}\quad\hbox{and}\quad a_{2}=\eta_{0}<\eta_{1}<\cdots<\eta_{m}=b_{2}.

Denote
I_{1j}:=(\xi_{j-1},\xi_{j}]
and
I_{2k}:=(\eta_{k-1},\eta_{k}]
.
Then

\tilde{J}_{1}=\cup_{j=1}^{n}I_{1j}

and

\tilde{J}_{2}=\cup_{k=1}^{n}I_{2k}.

From (
4.3
) it follows that

E_{1}(\tilde{J}_{1})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}E_{1}(I_{1j}),\quad E_{2}(\tilde{J}_{2})=\sum_{k=1}^{m}E_{2}(I_{2k})

and using (
4.5
) we obtain

(11.5)

E_{0}(\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{m}E_{0}(I_{1\ell}\times I_{2k}).

On the other hand, clearly each rectangle
J_{1j}\times J_{2j}
either coincides with one of the rectangles

I_{1\ell}\times I_{2k}

or

J_{1j}=\cup_{\ell=\ell_{1}}^{\ell_{2}}I_{1\ell}\quad\hbox{and}\quad J_{2j}=\cup_{k=k_{1}}^{k_{2}}I_{2k}

and hence

J_{1j}\times J_{2j}=\cup_{\ell=\ell_{1}}^{\ell_{2}}\cup_{k=k_{1}}^{k_{2}}I_{1\ell}\times I_{2k}.

Hence, just as above

E_{0}(J_{1j}\times J_{2j})=\sum_{\ell=\ell_{1}}^{\ell_{2}}\sum_{k=k_{1}}^{k_{2}}E_{0}(I_{1\ell}\times I_{2k}).

Combining this with (
11.5
) implies (
11.3
).

Assume now that for
R\in\mathscr{B}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
we have

(11.6)

R=\cup_{j=1}^{N}J_{1j}\times J_{2j}=\cup_{k=1}^{K}I_{1k}\times I_{2k},\quad J_{ij},I_{ik}\in\mathcal{J},

where the rectangles
\{J_{1j}\times J_{2j}\}
are disjoint and

\{I_{1k}\times I_{2k}\}
are also disjoint.
To find a common refinement of these to sets of rectangles
we proceed similarly as above.
We project the end points of the intervals

\{J_{1j}\}
onto the
x_{1}
-axis and the end points of the intervals
\{J_{2j}\}
onto the
x_{2}
-axis.
Denote these points by
\{\xi_{j}\}
and
\{\eta_{k}\}
and
assume that they are ordered just as in (
11.4
), i.e.

\xi_{0}<\xi_{1}<\cdots<\xi_{n}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\eta_{0}<\eta_{1}<\cdots<\eta_{m}.

Denote

Q_{1\ell}:=(\xi_{\ell-1},\xi_{\ell}]
and
Q_{2\nu}:=(\eta_{\nu-1},\eta_{\nu}]
.
Also, denote by
\mathcal{I}_{1}
the set of all indices
\ell
such that
Q_{1\ell}
is contained
in some intervals
J_{1j}
and
I_{1k}
,
and by
\mathcal{I}_{2}
the set of all indices
\nu
such that
Q_{2\nu}
is contained
in some intervals
J_{2j}
and
I_{2k}
.

Clearly, each rectangle
J_{1j}\times J_{2j}
either coincides with one of the rectangles

Q_{1\ell}\times Q_{2\nu}

or

J_{1j}=\cup_{\ell=\ell_{1}}^{\ell_{2}}Q_{1\ell}\quad\hbox{and}\quad J_{2j}=\cup_{\nu=\nu_{1}}^{\nu_{2}}Q_{2\nu},

and hence

J_{1j}\times J_{2j}=\cup_{\ell=\ell_{1}}^{\ell_{2}}\cup_{\nu=\nu_{1}}^{\nu_{2}}Q_{1\ell}\times Q_{2\nu}.

Similarly, each rectangle
I_{1k}\times I_{2k}
is covered by some of the rectangles
\{Q_{1\ell}\times Q_{2\nu}\}
.
Thus,

\{Q_{1\ell}\times Q_{2\nu}\}_{\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{1},\nu\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}

is a common refinement of
the rectangles
\{J_{1j}\times J_{2j}\}
and
\{I_{1k}\times I_{2k}\}
.

Using (
11.3
) we obtain

\cup_{j=1}^{N}E_{0}(J_{1j}\times J_{2j})=\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}E(Q_{1\ell}\times Q_{2\nu})=\cup_{k=1}^{K}E_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2k}).

Therefore, the operator
E_{0}(R)
is well defined by (
11.2
).
Since
\overline{E}_{0}(R)
is the closure of
E_{0}(R)
, then
\overline{E}_{0}(R)
is also well defined by (
4.10
).

The identity
\overline{E}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=I
follows readily by the obvious fact that
E_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=I
on
\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu)
.

Our next step is to show that if

(11.7)

\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2}=(a_{1},b_{1}]\times(a_{2},b_{2}]=\cup_{j=1}^{\infty}J_{1j}\times J_{2j},\quad J_{ij}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}),

where

\{J_{1j}\times J_{2j}\}
are disjoint, then

(11.8)

\overline{E}_{0}(\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2})=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1j}\times J_{2j}),

where the convergence is unconditional in the strong sense.

Case 1.

Consider first the case when there exist two countable collections of disjoint intervals

\{I_{1k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
,

\{I_{1\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{\infty}
in
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R})

such that

\tilde{J_{1}}=\cup_{k=1}^{\infty}I_{1k},\quad\tilde{J_{2}}=\cup_{\ell=1}^{\infty}I_{2\ell},\quad\hbox{and}\quad\{J_{1j}\times J_{2j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}=\{I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell}\}_{k,\ell\geq 1}.

In this particular case (
11.8
) takes the form

(11.9)

\overline{E}_{0}(\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell}).

To prove this we first show that

(11.10)

E_{0}(\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}E_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})\quad\hbox{on}\quad\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu).

Indeed, since
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
are spectral measures we have for any
f\in L^{2}(X_{1},d\mu_{1})
,
g\in L^{2}(X_{2},d\mu_{2})

(11.11)

E_{1}(\tilde{J}_{1})f=\lim_{K\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{K}E_{1}(I_{1k})f\quad\hbox{and}\quad E_{2}(\tilde{J}_{2})f=\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}E_{2}(I_{2\ell})g,

where the convergence is unconditional in the respective
L^{2}
-norms.
Therefore,

\displaystyle E_{0}(\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2})f\otimes g

\displaystyle=\lim_{K,N\to\infty}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{K}E_{1}(I_{1k})f\Big)\otimes\Big(\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}E_{2}(I_{2\ell})g\Big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{K,N\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}E_{1}(I_{1k})f\otimes E_{2}(I_{2\ell})g

\displaystyle=\lim_{K,N\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}E_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})f\otimes g

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}E_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})f\otimes g,

where the convergence is in the
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
-norm.
Here we used the obvious fact that
if
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|F_{n}-F\|_{L^{2}(X_{1})}=0
and
\lim_{m\to\infty}\|G_{m}-G\|_{L^{2}(X_{2})}=0
,
then

\lim_{n,m\to\infty}\|F_{n}\otimes G_{m}-F\otimes G\|_{L^{2}(X)}=0
.
Clearly, (
11.10
) follows from the above.

By Proposition
4.3
we know that for any
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R})

the operator
\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1}\times I_{2})
is a projector
and using (
4.8
) we conclude that for any
K,N\geq 1

the operator

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})

is a projector in
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
(see e.g.
[
45
, Theorem 3.7]
).
Therefore, for any
h\in L^{2}(X,d\mu)
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\|\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})h\|^{2}=\Big\|\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})\Big)h\Big\|^{2}\leq\|h\|^{2}

and hence

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\|\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})h\|^{2}\leq\|h\|^{2}.

Consequently, the series

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})h

converges unconditionally in the
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
-norm for each
h\in L^{2}(X,d\mu)
.
To show that

(11.12)

\overline{E}_{0}(\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2})h=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})h

we take any function
w\in\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu)
.
Then

\displaystyle\Big\langle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})h,w\Big\rangle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\langle\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})h,w\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\langle h,\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})w\rangle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\langle h,E_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})w\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big\langle h,\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}E_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})w\Big\rangle=\langle h,E_{0}(\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2})w\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle h,\overline{E}_{0}(\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2})w\rangle=\langle\overline{E}_{0}(\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2})h,w\rangle.

Above we used (
11.10
) and that the operator
\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})
is self-adjoint
and is the unique extension of the operator
E_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})
from
\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu)
to
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
.
Since the above identities are valid for each
w\in\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu)
and
\mathscr{L}^{2}(X,d\mu)
is dense in
L^{2}(X,d\mu)

they imply (
11.9
).

Case 2.
We now establish (
11.8
) in the general case.
Assume that the cover (
11.7
) of
\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2}
is valid,
where
\{J_{1j}\times J_{2j}\}
are disjoint.
We project the end points of all intervals
\{J_{1j}\}
onto
(a_{1},b_{1}]

to obtain a countable set of points
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
.
Let
\{I_{1k}\}_{k\geq 1}
be the disjoint collection of all interval with end points
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
,
where neither interval
I_{1k}
contains a point from
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
in its interior.
Thus we have the representation

(a_{1},b_{1}]=\cup_{k\geq 1}I_{1k}
.
We similarly construct a disjoint collection of intervals
\{I_{2\ell}\}_{\ell\geq 1}

so that
(a_{2},b_{2}]=\cup_{\ell\geq 1}I_{2\ell}
.
As a result we have

(11.13)

\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2}=(a_{1},b_{1}]\times(a_{2},b_{2}]=\cup_{k=1}^{\infty}\cup_{\ell=1}^{\infty}I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell}

and each rectangle
J_{1j}\times J_{2j}
is a finite or countable disjoint union of rectangles
\{I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell}\}

of the form

J_{1j}\times J_{2j}=\cup_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{j}}\cup_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{j}}I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell},

where

J_{1j}=\cup_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{j}}I_{1k}
and
J_{2j}=\cup_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{j}}I_{2\ell}
.
Clearly, we have the disjoint unions

\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2}=\cup_{k=1}^{\infty}\cup_{\ell=1}^{\infty}I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell}=\cup_{j=1}^{\infty}\cup_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{j}}\cup_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{j}}I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell}.

By the result from Case 1 (see (
11.9
)) for any
h\in L^{2}(X,d\mu)

\overline{E}_{0}(\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2})h=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})h,

where the convergence is unconditional in the
L^{2}(X,d\mu)
-norm.
Since the convergence above is unconditional we can rearrange the terms above as we wish
and hence

(11.14)

\overline{E}_{0}(\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2})h=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})h=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{j}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{j}}\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})h.

Applying again the result from Case 1 and (
11.13
) we obtain for
j=1,2,\dots

\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1j}\times J_{2j})h=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{j}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{j}}\overline{E}_{0}((I_{1k}\times I_{2\ell})h.

This coupled with (
11.14
) leads to

\overline{E}_{0}(\tilde{J}_{1}\times\tilde{J}_{2})h=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1j}\times J_{2j})h,\quad\forall h\in L^{2}(X,d\mu),

which implies (
11.8
).

From the definition in (
4.10
) it readily follows that
to prove (
4.11
) it suffices to show that

(11.15)

\overline{E}_{0}(R)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1j}\times J_{2j})

for any
R\in\mathscr{B}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
that is a disjoint union of the form

(11.16)

R=\cup_{j=1}^{\infty}J_{1j}\times J_{2j},\quad J_{ij}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}).

Assume that
R\in\mathscr{B}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
has this representation.
But, because
R\in\mathscr{B}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
from (
4.9
) it follows that

R
can also be represented as a disjoint union of finitely many rectangles, say,

(11.17)

R=\cup_{k=1}^{K}I_{1k}\times I_{2k},\quad I_{ik}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}).

Then by the definition in (
4.10
) we have

(11.18)

\overline{E}_{0}(R)=\cup_{k=1}^{K}\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2k}).

Evidently, each rectangle
J_{1j}\times J_{2j}
from (
11.16
) is
contained either in a single rectangle
I_{1k}\times I_{2k}
from (
11.17
)
or in the union of several rectangles
\{I_{1k}\times I_{2k}\}
.
Denote by

\{Q_{1j}^{k}\times Q_{2j}^{k}\}_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{j}},\quad Q_{ij}^{k}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}),\;\mathcal{K}_{j}\leq K,

the set of all rectangles obtained by intersecting

J_{1j}\times J_{2j}
with rectangles

\{I_{1k}\times I_{2k}\}
.
We order the rectangles

\{Q_{1j}^{k}\times Q_{2j}^{k}:k\in\mathcal{K}_{j},j\in{\mathbb{N}}\}

in a sequence that we denote by

\{\tilde{Q}_{1\nu}\times\tilde{Q}_{2\nu}\}_{\nu\geq 1}
.

Clearly,

J_{1j}\times J_{2j}=\cup_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{j}}Q_{1j}^{k}\times Q_{2j}^{k}

and hence

\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1j}\times J_{2j})=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{j}}\overline{E}_{0}(Q_{1j}^{k}\times Q_{2j}^{k}).

Summing up we get

(11.19)

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\overline{E}_{0}(J_{1j}\times J_{2j})=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{j}}\overline{E}_{0}(Q_{1j}^{k}\times Q_{2j}^{k})=\sum_{\nu=1}^{\infty}\overline{E}_{0}(\tilde{Q}_{1\nu}\times\tilde{Q}_{2\nu}).

On the other hand,
from (
11.16
)-(
11.17
) and the construction of the rectangles
\{\tilde{Q}_{1\nu}\times\tilde{Q}_{2\nu}\}

it readily follows that each rectangle
I_{1k}\times I_{2k}
is covered by the rectangles

\{\tilde{Q}_{1\nu}\times\tilde{Q}_{2\nu}\}_{\nu\geq 1}
.
More precisely, denoting by
\mathcal{N}_{k}
the indices of the rectangles
\tilde{Q}_{1\nu}\times\tilde{Q}_{2\nu}

contained in
I_{1k}\times I_{2k}
we have

I_{1k}\times I_{2k}=\cup_{\nu\in\mathcal{N}_{k}}\tilde{Q}_{1\nu}\times\tilde{Q}_{2\nu}.

Applying now identity (
11.8
) we obtain

\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2k})=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{N}_{k}}\overline{E}_{0}(\tilde{Q}_{1\nu}\times\tilde{Q}_{2\nu})

and summing up we arrive at

(11.20)

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\overline{E}_{0}(I_{1k}\times I_{2k})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{N}_{k}}\overline{E}_{0}(\tilde{Q}_{1\nu}\times\tilde{Q}_{2\nu})=\sum_{\nu=1}^{\infty}\overline{E}_{0}(\tilde{Q}_{1\nu}\times\tilde{Q}_{2\nu}).

Clearly, identity (
11.15
) follows from (
11.18
), (
11.19
), and (
11.20
).
This completes the proof of Proposition
4.4
.
∎
